## Lesson 11 — Ethics, norms, and the three requisites of empirical inquiry

Progress: 11 / 31 | Stage: Core | Part IV — the forces that pushed political science outward

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3 (empirical and normative approaches) and §5.2, with the Advanced companion's §4's first row, read against the chapter's paragraph contrasting the traditional normative foundation with the empirical approach and listing its requisites (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.138 = printed 123), which supplies the ethics-and-moral-philosophy foundation, the chief problem of traditional political science, the three-way contrast and the lettered requisites.
CA search: "empirical normative political science facts values descriptive prescriptive 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked was returned. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. A boundary first, so that the lesson stays in its lane

➡️ The full treatment of the empirical and normative approaches — the refused equation of traditional-with-normative, the form-against-content test, Weldon's charge, Strauss's counter-charge, the descriptive/prescriptive traffic in both directions — belongs to **Topic 08**, which owns it and teaches it across several lessons.

⚠️ This topic needs the distinction for one purpose only: to explain **why the empirical turn pushed political science outward**. The lesson therefore takes the chapter's own compressed statement of the distinction and stops there. ❌ Nothing from Topic 08's extended treatment is imported.

### 2. What traditional political science was founded on

> Traditional political science was founded on ethics or moral philosophy. Its chief problem was to determine norms of public life, and to evolve institutions according to those norms.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.138 (printed 123)

⚠️ Neither canonical owner carries this sentence, and it is the sentence that makes the turn intelligible. ✅ The traditional discipline had a **two-stage programme**:

```text
   STAGE 1   determine the NORMS of public life
                       |
   STAGE 2   EVOLVE INSTITUTIONS according to those norms

   Notice what this programme does NOT require:
   -- no survey of how existing institutions actually behave
   -- no comparison across cases
   -- no prediction
   -- no data from any neighbouring discipline

   A discipline with this programme has no structural need
   for economics, sociology or psychology. That is why the
   interdisciplinary question could not even arise until the
   programme changed.
```

*Caption: the diagram is the real explanation of the chapter's chronology. Interdisciplinarity did not arrive late because political scientists were incurious; it arrived late because the discipline's governing problem did not generate a demand for outside evidence.*

### 3. The contrast, in the chapter's three pairs

✅ The chapter sets out the contrast compactly:

| ✅ Empirical approach | ✅ Normative approach |
|---|---|
| Concerned with **facts** | Concerned with **values** |
| It is **descriptive** | It is **prescriptive** |
| Deals with **the real** | Deals with **the ideal** |
| Concerned with **what is there** | Concerned with **what ought to be done** |

⚠️ Four rows, not one. ⚠️ The canonical basic owner compresses these to two one-line definitions, and the compression loses the parallelism — which is precisely what makes the table memorable and reproducible under exam pressure.

### 4. The three requisites, restored in their lettered form

✅ The chapter states them as (a), (b) and (c):

> (a) it deals with observable facts and data; (b) it aims at explanation, that is it seeks to establish a correlation between different variables, to explore the cause-and-effect relationship, and to arrive at possible generalizations; and finally, (c) it tends to make predictions; that is when certain data and the precise relationship between different variables are known, the result could be predicted and verified.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.138 (printed 123)

```text
   (a) OBSERVABLE FACTS AND DATA
            |
            |  requires: something to observe
            v
   (b) EXPLANATION
            |-- correlation between different variables
            |-- cause-and-effect relationship
            |-- possible generalizations
            |
            |  requires: variables, and enough cases to
            |            generalize from
            v
   (c) PREDICTION
            |-- when the data and the PRECISE relationship
            |   between variables are known
            |-- the result could be PREDICTED and VERIFIED

   READ THE RIGHT-HAND COLUMN. Each requisite imposes a
   demand for material the political system does not itself
   supply: variables, cases, and the precise relationships
   between them. That demand is the engine of the whole
   chapter.
```

*Caption: the three requisites are usually memorised as a list. Read as a chain of demands, they explain why an empirically oriented political science must go shopping in its neighbours' disciplines.*

⚠️ The canonical basic owner renders this as "focus on observable facts, explanation, correlation, generalization and prediction" — a five-word compression that loses the lettering, loses the internal structure of (b), and loses the final word **verified**, which is the link to implication (b) of Lesson 4.

### 5. Why the requisites generate an interdisciplinary demand

⚠️ Take the requisites one at a time and ask what political science can supply from its own resources.

| Requisite | ⚠️ Can political science supply this alone? |
|---|---|
| ✅ (a) Observable facts and data | ⚠️ Partly — votes, debates, judgments. ❌ But not income distribution, literacy, kinship form or attitude |
| ✅ (b) Correlation between variables | ❌ No — a correlation needs at least two variables, and in most interesting political questions one of them is non-political |
| ✅ (b) Cause-and-effect relationship | ❌ No — the chapter's own cases run from wealth to regime, from mobilization to citizenship, from attitude to obedience |
| ✅ (b) Possible generalizations | ❌ Not reliably — generalization needs variation across cases, which usually means variation in social, economic or cultural conditions |
| ✅ (c) Prediction and verification | ❌ No — the **precise relationship** has to be known, and the chapter's test for that is verification against data the neighbours hold |

🔑 This table is the lesson's payoff and converts a definitional passage into an argument: **the empirical requisites cannot be met from inside the political system, so meeting them requires the neighbours.**

### 6. Comparison: what changed, and what did not

| | ⚠️ Traditional programme | ✅ Empirical programme |
|---|---|---|
| Governing question | ⚠️ What norms should public life follow, and what institutions embody them? | ✅ What is there, and why? |
| Evidence required | ⚠️ Moral argument | ✅ Observable facts and data |
| Need for neighbours | ❌ Structurally absent | ✅ Structurally unavoidable |
| ⚠️ Does the normative question disappear? | — | ❌ No — the chapter later records political philosophy's **renewed prominence** and its role in determining **the goals of public policy** |

⚠️ The last row is the one to carry into an answer. The empirical turn changes what the discipline must do in order to explain; ❌ it does not abolish the question of what the discipline is for.

### 7. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** Requisite (c) is too strong for the social sciences and the chapter should not have included it. Prediction with verification is the standard of the physical sciences; in politics the relationships are rarely precise, the cases are few, and the act of predicting can alter the outcome. By setting prediction as a requisite, the chapter makes empirical political science fail by its own definition — and an approach that cannot satisfy its own requisites cannot be the reason for anything.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's formulation is weaker than the objection assumes. It says the empirical approach **tends to make predictions**, and conditions the prediction on the data and the **precise relationship between different variables** being known. ⚠️ That is a statement of the approach's aspiration and of the condition under which it is realisable, not a claim that the condition is routinely met. ✅ The chapter is also consistent elsewhere about the limits: a political theory not substantiated by historical data **both in terms of rule and exception** is said to lack scientific precision, which concedes that exceptions are expected.

⚠️ **Residual.** The objection retains force about the *status* of political generalizations, and the chapter never grades them. ⚠️ The honest position is that requisite (c) is reported as the approach's aspiration, with its antecedent condition stated, and ❌ it is not asserted that political science satisfies it.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The three requisites are examinable in this topic only as the *source of the interdisciplinary demand*; ➡️ their full fact-value treatment is Topic 08's.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *How did the empirical orientation change the scope of political science?* — the two-stage traditional programme, the four-row contrast, the three requisites, and the table of section 5.
- ❌ **Trap.** Drifting into Weldon, Strauss and the fact-value debate. ⚠️ That material belongs elsewhere and will read as padding here.
- 🔑 **Answer use.** The sentence worth constructing is: *each empirical requisite demands material the political system does not itself contain.* It converts a list into an argument in one line.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Traditional political science was founded on ethics or moral philosophy.** (PDF p.138)
2. ✅ Its **chief problem** was to **determine norms of public life**, and to **evolve institutions according to those norms**.
3. ⚠️ Both owners omit this sentence; it explains why the interdisciplinary question could not arise earlier.
4. ✅ Empirical = **facts**, **descriptive**, **the real**, **what is there**.
5. ✅ Normative = **values**, **prescriptive**, **the ideal**, **what ought to be done**.
6. ✅ Requisite (a): **observable facts and data**.
7. ✅ Requisite (b): **explanation** — correlation between variables, cause-and-effect relationship, possible generalizations.
8. ✅ Requisite (c): **prediction** — when data and the **precise relationship** are known, the result could be **predicted and verified**.
9. ⚠️ The word **verified** links this passage to implication (b) of the interdisciplinary programme.
10. 🔑 No requisite after (a) can be met from political material alone — this is the lesson's argument.
11. ⚠️ The chapter says the approach **tends to** predict; ❌ it does not claim the condition is routinely met.
12. ❌ The normative question is not abolished — political philosophy later regains **renewed prominence**.
13. ➡️ The full empirical/normative controversy is Topic 08's property and is not imported here.

### Concept check

**Question:** Show how the three requisites of the empirical approach, taken in order, generate the chapter's interdisciplinary conclusion without any further premise.

**Model answer:** ✅ Requisite (a) demands **observable facts and data**; political institutions supply some, but income distribution, literacy, kinship form and attitude are held by the neighbouring disciplines. ✅ Requisite (b) demands **correlation between different variables**, **cause-and-effect relationship** and **possible generalizations**; a correlation needs at least two variables, and in the questions the chapter cares about at least one is non-political, while generalization needs variation across cases that is usually social or economic. ✅ Requisite (c) demands prediction **when certain data and the precise relationship between different variables are known**, with the result **predicted and verified** — and verification against data is exactly implication (b) of the interdisciplinary programme. ⚠️ So the conclusion follows from the method's own demands: an empirically oriented political science needs material the political system does not contain. 🔑 No separate premise about the value of breadth is required.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not use this lesson to re-run the fact-value debate. ⚠️ Topic 08 owns the refused equation, Weldon's charge and Strauss's reply; here the distinction is needed only as the engine that drives political science outward, and an answer that drifts into the controversy is answering a different question.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Analyse how the shift from a normative to an empirical orientation altered political science's need for the other social sciences. *(Analyse — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Traditional political science was **founded on ethics or moral philosophy**, and its **chief problem was to determine norms of public life, and to evolve institutions according to those norms**. ⚠️ That programme needs moral argument and generates no structural demand for outside evidence, which is why the interdisciplinary question could not arise within it. ✅ The empirical approach is instead concerned with **facts**, is **descriptive**, deals with **the real** and asks **what is there**. ✅ Its requisites are observable facts and data; explanation through correlation, cause-and-effect and possible generalizations; and prediction, where the result could be **predicted and verified**. ⚠️ None of these after the first can be satisfied from political material alone, since the second variable in most political correlations is social, economic or psychological. 🔑 The need for neighbours is therefore structural, not stylistic. ❌ The normative question survives, and political philosophy later regains prominence.

**Rubric [10]:** the two programmes separated, with the ethics-and-moral-philosophy foundation and its two-stage chief problem **2** · at least three of the four contrast pairs **2** · all three requisites with the internal structure of (b) **3** · the inference that requisites cannot be met from political material alone **2** · the qualification that the normative question survives **1**.

## Lesson 12 — What the empirical turn replaced, and what it borrowed first

Progress: 12 / 31 | Stage: Core | Part IV — the forces that pushed political science outward

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §4.3 and §8's "limit if used alone" column, with the Advanced companion's §3.4, read against the paragraph closing the chapter's sub-section on empirical orientation (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.138 = printed 123), which supplies the rejection of ideal-state images and formal institutions, the new trend of treating politics as a process within the social system, and the two-part statement of what political science sought from its neighbours.
CA search: "formal institutions operate differently in practice political process social system 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked was returned. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Two targets, not one

⚠️ When candidates describe what empirical political science rejected, they name one thing: speculation about the ideal state. ✅ The chapter names **two**, and the second is the one that matters for this topic.

> The empirical orientation in political science led to the realization that the real world of politics could not be understood through images of an ideal state, nor merely in terms of formal institutions which may operate differently in actual practice.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.138 (printed 123)

```text
   TARGET 1   IMAGES OF AN IDEAL STATE
              -- the normative programme of Lesson 11
              -- rejected as a route to understanding
                 THE REAL WORLD of politics

   TARGET 2   FORMAL INSTITUTIONS
              -- "which may operate differently in actual
                 practice"
              -- rejected as SUFFICIENT, note the word
                 "merely"

   Target 1 was already under attack from the empirical turn.
   TARGET 2 IS THE NEW ONE, and it is the one that forces
   the borrowing -- because if the written rule does not
   predict the behaviour, something outside the written rule
   is doing the work.
```

*Caption: note the word "merely" before "formal institutions". The chapter is not saying institutions do not matter; it is saying an account confined to them is insufficient. That is a much more defensible claim, and the one to reproduce.*

### 2. Why the second target produces the interdisciplinary demand

⚠️ Suppose a constitution assigns a power to one organ and in practice another exercises it. ⚠️ A purely formal account has three options: call the practice an aberration, call the text a fiction, or fall silent. ✅ The empirical account has a fourth: look for the forces that produce the divergence — and those forces are social, economic and psychological.

🔑 The gap between the rule and the practice is where the other social sciences enter. ⚠️ That is the cleanest single statement of why this chapter exists, and it is derivable from the sentence above.

### 3. What replaced the two targets

> It set a new trend of understanding politics as a process which takes place within the larger framework of the social system.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.138 (printed 123)

✅ Two components, and both do work:

| ✅ Component | ⚠️ What it rules out |
|---|---|
| Politics as a **process** | ❌ Politics as a structure or a document |
| Taking place **within the larger framework of the social system** | ❌ Politics as a self-contained domain |

⚠️ This sentence is the explicit statement of the premise that Lesson 8 showed to be load-bearing — *politics as a social process*. ⚠️ Here it is presented not as a definition but as the outcome of a methodological discovery, which is a stronger position: the premise was not stipulated, it was arrived at.

### 4. The first import, and its order

✅ The chapter now states what political science went looking for:

> Since the empirical method was first developed in other social sciences, such as economics, sociology, anthropology, psychology, etc. political science was attracted toward other social sciences not only to learn their methodology, but also to find out how far their data, theories and models could help in understanding the political phenomenon.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.138 (printed 123)

```text
   WHY was political science attracted?
        because the empirical method was FIRST DEVELOPED
        in economics, sociology, anthropology, psychology

   WHAT did it seek, in order?
        (1) METHODOLOGY            <- the first and deeper import
        (2) DATA                   <- Lessons 15-16
        (3) THEORIES               <- Lesson 17
        (4) MODELS                 <- Lessons 17-19

   The rest of the chapter is this list, worked through
   in exactly this order.
```

*Caption: the four-item list is the chapter's own table of contents for its middle section. An answer that uses it as a structuring device will track the chapter precisely.*

⚠️ The four disciplines named here — **economics, sociology, anthropology, psychology** — are the same four named in the purposefulness lock of Lesson 5, and the third time anthropology appears as a normal donor discipline without receiving a section of its own.

### 5. An example of the methodology import

⚠️ **Example.** Sampling. The idea that a well-constructed sample of a population can support claims about the whole was developed outside political science. ⚠️ Once imported, it changed what political science could ask: not merely *what does the constitution provide* but *what proportion of citizens believe the courts are independent, and how does that vary with education*. ⚠️ No political datum was borrowed; a procedure was.

⚠️ **Where the example stops.** A procedure carries assumptions. Sampling assumes a population that can be enumerated and a question that means the same thing to every respondent — assumptions that can fail badly in plural societies with multiple languages and sharply different political vocabularies. ⚠️ Importing a method is therefore never cost-free, and the chapter's own purposefulness lock is the only brake it offers.

### 6. Comparison with the institutional approach it displaces

| | ⚠️ Institutional account | ✅ Process account |
|---|---|---|
| Primary object | Offices, powers, written rules | Interactions, behaviour, outcomes |
| Treatment of divergence between rule and practice | ⚠️ Anomaly | ✅ Explanandum |
| Where explanation is sought | Inside the institution | ✅ In the social framework within which the process runs |
| Need for other social sciences | ❌ Minimal | ✅ Structural |
| ➡️ Full treatment | ➡️ Topic 08 owns the institutional approach and its four drawbacks | ✅ This topic owns only the consequence for borrowing |

### 7. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** The second target is overstated and the chapter has smuggled a conclusion into an observation. Saying that formal institutions **may operate differently in actual practice** establishes only that institutional description is sometimes wrong; it does not establish that the explanation of the divergence lies outside politics. A political explanation is readily available — party discipline, coalition arithmetic, bureaucratic incentive, judicial strategy — all of them internal to the political system.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter does not claim the explanation must be non-political; it claims the account cannot be **merely** institutional. ⚠️ The word *merely* concedes exactly the ground the objection occupies. ✅ Moreover, the political factors the objection names are themselves behavioural rather than formal — party discipline is a pattern of behaviour, not a provision — and the chapter's very next sub-section identifies that shift from institutions to **political behaviour** as the first of two developments the empirical orientation produced. ⚠️ So the objection describes the first step of the chapter's own argument and mistakes it for a refutation.

⚠️ **Residual.** A sharper version survives: even granting the behavioural turn, it is a further and separate claim that behaviour must be explained by **non-political** variables rather than by political ones. ⚠️ The chapter supports that further claim by example — mobilization, literacy, attitude, class — rather than by argument, and ⚠️ an answer should present it as an empirical wager that the examples make plausible, ❌ not as a demonstrated necessity.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** The two-target sentence and the four-item import list are both high-yield and compact.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *What did the empirical orientation change in political science?* — two targets, one replacement, four imports.
- ❌ **Trap.** Omitting **merely** and thereby attributing to the chapter the indefensible claim that institutions do not matter.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Use the four-item list — methodology, data, theories, models — as the skeleton of any long answer on this topic; it is the chapter's own order.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Two targets rejected: **images of an ideal state**, and **merely** formal institutions **which may operate differently in actual practice**. (PDF p.138)
2. 🔑 The word **merely** is essential; without it the claim is indefensible.
3. ⚠️ The gap between rule and practice is where the other social sciences enter.
4. ✅ The replacement: politics as **a process which takes place within the larger framework of the social system**.
5. ⚠️ Two components — process, and social framework — each ruling out a different error.
6. ⚠️ This is where *politics as a social process* is earned rather than stipulated.
7. ✅ The empirical method was **first developed in other social sciences** — economics, sociology, anthropology, psychology.
8. ✅ Political science sought **not only** their **methodology** **but also** their **data, theories and models**.
9. 🔑 Four imports, in the chapter's own order: methodology → data → theories → models.
10. ⚠️ The rest of the chapter follows that order exactly; use it as an answer skeleton.
11. ⚠️ Anthropology appears here for the third time as a donor discipline with no section of its own.
12. ⚠️ Imported methods carry assumptions; the purposefulness lock is the only brake the chapter supplies.
13. ⚠️ That behaviour must be explained by *non-political* variables is supported by example, ❌ not demonstrated.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter rejects an account of politics framed "merely in terms of formal institutions which may operate differently in actual practice". Explain precisely what work the word *merely* is doing, and what the sentence would claim without it.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ With **merely**, the claim is about **sufficiency**: a formal-institutional account is not enough, because the written allocation of powers may diverge from the practice and the divergence itself requires explanation. ✅ Institutions remain part of the account, and the chapter nowhere says otherwise. ⚠️ Without **merely**, the sentence would claim that formal institutions are *irrelevant* to understanding the real world of politics — an indefensible position that the chapter does not hold and that its own later treatment of the state, law and government would contradict. 🔑 The sufficiency claim is what generates the interdisciplinary demand: if the rule does not predict the practice, something outside the rule is doing the work, and the chapter's next move is to locate that something first in **political behaviour** and then in the social, economic and psychological conditions that shape it.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not conclude that the empirical turn abolished institutional study. ⚠️ The chapter's claim is that the institutional account is insufficient, and its own later sections repeatedly use institutional material — governmental agencies, trade unions, political parties — as objects of sociological analysis.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine what the empirical orientation in political science replaced, and what it borrowed first. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ It replaced two things. ✅ The real world of politics could not be understood through **images of an ideal state**, nor **merely in terms of formal institutions which may operate differently in actual practice**. ⚠️ The qualifier *merely* matters: institutional accounts are insufficient, not irrelevant, and the rule-practice divergence becomes the thing to be explained. ✅ What replaced them was a **new trend of understanding politics as a process which takes place within the larger framework of the social system** — the premise of the chapter's later conclusions. ✅ What was borrowed first was method: since the empirical method was **first developed in other social sciences**, political science turned to them **not only to learn their methodology, but also** for their **data, theories and models**. 🔑 That four-item order — methodology, data, theories, models — governs everything that follows. ⚠️ Imported methods, however, import assumptions with them.

**Rubric [10]:** both rejected targets, with *merely* preserved **3** · the process-within-the-social-system replacement **2** · the first-developed-elsewhere reason **1** · the four-item import list in the chapter's order **3** · a caution about imported assumptions **1**.

## Lesson 13 — Political behaviour, and Eulau's reason

Progress: 13 / 31 | Stage: Core | Part V — behaviour, systems and the demand for outside material

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §6 (Heinz Eulau) and §4.3, with the Advanced companion's §3.5 and §5, read against the chapter's sub-section headed "Focus on Political Behaviour and Systems Analysis" down to the end of the Eulau quotation (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF pp.138-139 = printed 123-124), which supplies the shift in emphasis, the roster of participants, the description-against-analysis contrast and Eulau's statement with its exact provenance.
CA search: "political behaviour analysis interdisciplinary social cultural personal factors 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nil — the authority examined here is a 1968 encyclopaedia article, which has no present-day counterpart. ❌ None is manufactured.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Two consequences, and this is the first

✅ The chapter announces that the empirical orientation produced a shift **from political institutions to political behaviour and political system**, and then takes the two in order. ⚠️ Behaviour is first; systems analysis is Lesson 14.

### 2. Whose behaviour

> Firstly, it was realized that politics could be better understood in terms of the behaviour of the participants, namely the electorate, legislators, bureaucrats, executive and judicial authorities, and so on.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF pp.138-139 (printed 123-124)

✅ Five categories are named, and the canonical owners compress them to four:

```text
   THE ELECTORATE          -- those who authorise
   LEGISLATORS             -- those who deliberate and enact
   BUREAUCRATS             -- those who implement
   EXECUTIVE AUTHORITIES   -- those who direct
   JUDICIAL AUTHORITIES    -- those who adjudicate
                           "... and so on"

   The list is a map of the whole political process by ROLE.
   Note that it begins OUTSIDE the state (the electorate)
   and then runs through all three organs. That ordering is
   the behavioural turn in miniature.
```

*Caption: the roster is worth reproducing in full because it shows the behavioural turn was not confined to voting studies. Judges and bureaucrats are on the list from the start.*

### 3. The contrast that defines the shift

✅ The chapter states what political analysis ceased to be and became:

> In other words, political analysis involved not a mere description of ideas and institutions, but an analysis of the behaviour of the people—the individuals, groups of various types whether they belonged to particular structures of the government or were outside it, the elite, etc.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.139 (printed 124)

| ❌ What it ceased to be | ✅ What it became |
|---|---|
| A mere **description** | An **analysis** |
| Of **ideas and institutions** | Of the **behaviour of the people** |
| — | ✅ Individuals |
| — | ✅ **Groups of various types**, whether **inside** government structures **or outside** them |
| — | ✅ **The elite** |

🔑 The inside-or-outside clause is the detail that most answers lose. ⚠️ It is what makes the behavioural turn interdisciplinary rather than merely micro-analytical: once groups outside the structures of government are admitted as objects of political analysis, the analyst is studying associations, interests and movements that sociology already studies.

### 4. Eulau's statement, with its provenance restored

✅ The chapter attributes the reason to a named scholar in a named place:

> As man's political behaviour is only one aspect of his total behaviour as a social being, political behaviour analysis must be interdisciplinary; it cannot neglect the wider context in which political action occurs. It is bound, therefore, to consider the possible effects of social, cultural and personal factors on political behaviour.
>
> — Heinz Eulau, article on 'Political Behaviour' in the *International Encyclopaedia of the Social Sciences* (1968), quoted at Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.139 (printed 124)

⚠️ Both canonical owners paraphrase this; ✅ neither carries the article title, the encyclopaedia or the year. ⚠️ All three are restored here, because a named, dated, locatable source is worth far more in an answer than an unattributed paraphrase.

⚠️ The argument inside the quotation has a clean two-step form:

```text
   PREMISE     political behaviour is ONLY ONE ASPECT of
               man's TOTAL BEHAVIOUR as a SOCIAL BEING
                             |
                             v
   CONCLUSION  political behaviour analysis MUST be
               interdisciplinary
                             |
                             v
   COROLLARY   it is bound to consider the possible effects of
               three classes of factor:
                  SOCIAL  |  CULTURAL  |  PERSONAL
```

*Caption: the three factors map onto three donor disciplines — sociology, cultural anthropology and psychology. Eulau's sentence is therefore a compressed statement of three of the chapter's five "use of" sections.*

### 5. Why the argument works, and what it assumes

⚠️ The inference is from a part-whole relation to a methodological requirement, and it is valid only on a substantive assumption: that the aspects of a person's behaviour are not sealed off from one another. ⚠️ If political behaviour were a discrete module unaffected by the rest of a person's social life, the premise would be true and the conclusion false.

✅ The chapter supplies that assumption elsewhere — the political system is an **open system**, politics is a **social process** — which is why this lesson sits after Lesson 8 and not before it. ⚠️ Eulau's argument is a corollary of the open-system premise applied to the individual rather than to the system.

### 6. Comparison: three readings of "political behaviour"

| ⚠️ Reading | ✅ Chapter's position |
|---|---|
| ❌ Voting behaviour | ❌ Too narrow — legislators, bureaucrats, executive and judicial authorities are named |
| ❌ Behaviour of individuals only | ❌ Too narrow — **groups of various types** and **the elite** are named |
| ✅ The behaviour of all participants in the political process, inside and outside government structures | ✅ The chapter's own roster |
| ➡️ Behaviouralism as a doctrine with eight intellectual foundations | ➡️ **Topic 08** owns this; ❌ it is not imported here |

❌ The boundary in the last row matters. ⚠️ This topic needs behaviour only as a *reason for borrowing*; ➡️ the tenets, the Chicago School, the post-behavioural revolution and the critics belong to Topic 08.

### 7. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** Eulau's argument proves too little. That political behaviour is one aspect of total social behaviour shows that social, cultural and personal factors *may* be relevant; it does not show they are relevant to any particular political question, and a methodological rule that says "consider everything" is not a method. On this reading the quotation licenses undisciplined breadth, which is exactly what the chapter's purposefulness lock was meant to prevent.

✅ **Reply.** Eulau's wording is careful: analysis is bound to consider the **possible** effects, which is a requirement to examine rather than a presumption of relevance. ⚠️ Considering a possible effect and finding it absent is a completed analytical step, not a failure. ✅ And the chapter's own lock supplies the discipline the objection asks for: entry into another sphere must be **purposeful and well-conceived**, with the **definite purpose of understanding the problems of politics**. ⚠️ Taken together the two produce a workable rule — the social, cultural and personal must be examined for relevance to the political question in hand, and dropped when they prove irrelevant.

⚠️ **Residual.** The combined rule is still a counsel of good practice rather than a procedure, since nothing tells the analyst how far to look before concluding that a factor is irrelevant. ⚠️ The chapter accepts breadth as a cost of accuracy and ❌ does not pretend to bound it.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** Eulau's sentence is the single best quotable authority in the chapter for the proposition that political analysis must be interdisciplinary.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Why must the study of political behaviour be interdisciplinary?* — the premise, the conclusion, the three factors, then the three donor disciplines.
- ❌ **Trap.** Reducing political behaviour to voting. ⚠️ The roster includes judges and bureaucrats and extends to groups outside government.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Cite Eulau with the encyclopaedia and the year. Precision of attribution is cheap to supply here and visibly distinguishes a prepared answer.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The empirical orientation shifted emphasis from **political institutions** to **political behaviour and political system**. (PDF p.138)
2. ✅ The participants named: the **electorate**, **legislators**, **bureaucrats**, **executive and judicial authorities**.
3. ✅ Political analysis became not a **mere description of ideas and institutions** but an **analysis of the behaviour of the people**.
4. ✅ Objects include **individuals**, **groups of various types** — ✅ **whether they belonged to particular structures of the government or were outside it** — and **the elite**.
5. 🔑 The inside-or-outside clause is what makes the behavioural turn interdisciplinary.
6. ✅ **Heinz Eulau**, article on **'Political Behaviour'**, *International Encyclopaedia of the Social Sciences*, **1968**.
7. ✅ Eulau: political behaviour is **only one aspect of his total behaviour as a social being**, therefore its analysis **must be interdisciplinary**.
8. ✅ It is bound to consider the possible effects of **social, cultural and personal** factors.
9. 🔑 Those three map to sociology, cultural anthropology and psychology.
10. ⚠️ Both owners paraphrase Eulau without the provenance; the article, encyclopaedia and year are restored here.
11. ⚠️ The inference assumes the aspects of behaviour are not sealed off — an assumption the open-system premise supplies.
12. ⚠️ **Possible** effects: a requirement to examine, ❌ not a presumption of relevance.
13. ➡️ Behaviouralism as a doctrine, with its tenets and critics, belongs to Topic 08 and is not imported.

### Concept check

**Question:** Reconstruct Eulau's argument as a premise and a conclusion, identify the assumption it needs, and say where in the chapter that assumption is supplied.

**Model answer:** ✅ Premise: **man's political behaviour is only one aspect of his total behaviour as a social being**. ✅ Conclusion: **political behaviour analysis must be interdisciplinary** and is bound to consider the possible effects of **social, cultural and personal** factors. ⚠️ The inference needs the assumption that the aspects of a person's behaviour interact — that the political aspect is not a sealed module. ⚠️ Without it, the premise would be true while the conclusion failed, since one could study a sealed module in isolation. ✅ The chapter supplies the assumption two pages earlier, where politics is studied **as a social process** and the political system is defined as an **open system** or **a system of interactions**, so that what happens in it is affected by, and affects, its environment. 🔑 Eulau's point is that same openness restated at the level of the individual rather than the system.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat Eulau's sentence as a definition of behaviouralism. ⚠️ It is an argument about what the *analysis* of political behaviour requires; the doctrine of behaviouralism, with its intellectual foundations and its critics, is owned by Topic 08 and is a different thing.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "Once political analysis turned from institutions to behaviour, interdisciplinarity ceased to be optional." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The chapter supports the proposition and supplies the reason. ✅ The behavioural turn widened the object from a **mere description of ideas and institutions** to the behaviour of the **electorate, legislators, bureaucrats, executive and judicial authorities**, and of **groups of various types whether they belonged to particular structures of the government or were outside it**. ⚠️ The moment groups outside government became political objects, the analyst entered territory sociology already occupied. ✅ **Heinz Eulau**, writing on **'Political Behaviour'** in the *International Encyclopaedia of the Social Sciences* (**1968**), gives the argument: political behaviour is **only one aspect of his total behaviour as a social being**, so its analysis **must be interdisciplinary** and is bound to weigh **social, cultural and personal** factors. ⚠️ The inference holds only because the aspects of behaviour interact, which the open-system premise supplies. 🔑 Three factors, three donor disciplines, one unavoidable conclusion.

**Rubric [10]:** the widened roster, including the inside-or-outside clause **3** · Eulau quoted or closely reported with his provenance **3** · the three factors and their mapping to donor disciplines **2** · the interaction assumption identified and sourced **1** · a closing formulation **1**.

## Lesson 14 — Systems analysis: a subsystem cannot be read alone

Progress: 14 / 31 | Stage: Core | Part V — behaviour, systems and the demand for outside material

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §4.3 and §1's map, with the Advanced companion's §3.6 and §2, read against the chapter's second development under "Focus on Political Behaviour and Systems Analysis" (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.139 = printed 124), which supplies the subsystem claim, the denial that the political system operates independently, the statement about the three formal organs and the four aspects of social organization that must be understood.
CA search: "political system subsystem of social system legislature executive judiciary demands 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked was returned. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The second development

✅ The chapter's second reason:

> In the second place, the introduction of systems analysis in political science has also given an impetus to the interdisciplinary approach.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.139 (printed 124)

⚠️ Note the modesty of the verb: systems analysis **gave an impetus**. ⚠️ It did not create the interdisciplinary approach — Marx and Engels preceded it by a century — but it supplied a formal vocabulary in which the requirement could be stated precisely.

### 2. The subsystem claim

> The political system is essentially regarded as a sub-system within the ambit of the larger social system. In other words, the political system does not operate independently or in isolation.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.139 (printed 124)

```text
   +=====================================================+
   |              THE LARGER SOCIAL SYSTEM               |
   |                                                     |
   |   economic     cultural     psychological           |
   |   aspects      aspects      aspects      sociological
   |                                          aspects    |
   |        +----------------------------+               |
   |        |   THE POLITICAL SYSTEM     |               |
   |        |   -- a SUB-system          |               |
   |        |   -- does NOT operate      |               |
   |        |      independently or in   |               |
   |        |      isolation             |               |
   |        +----------------------------+               |
   |                                                     |
   +=====================================================+

   The box is drawn INSIDE the larger box on purpose.
   A subsystem's boundary is a boundary of analysis, not a
   wall. Everything that crosses it is, by definition, the
   business of two disciplines at once.
```

*Caption: the containment relation is the whole argument. You cannot describe a subsystem's behaviour completely while treating its container as background.*

### 3. The claim about the three organs

✅ The chapter now applies the containment claim to the most formal part of the state:

> The formal organs of the government—the legislature, the executive and the judiciary—do not produce their decisions on their own initiative or at their own will, but in response to the demands emanating from the social system, and these decisions in turn have their impact on the social system.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.139 (printed 124)

⚠️ This sentence is absent from both canonical owners, and it is the most pointed claim in the first half of the chapter. ⚠️ Three things are being denied of the three organs:

| ❌ Denied | ✅ Asserted instead |
|---|---|
| That they decide **on their own initiative** | ✅ They decide **in response to the demands emanating from the social system** |
| That they decide **at their own will** | ✅ Same |
| That the decision terminates the process | ✅ The decisions **in turn have their impact on the social system** |

🔑 The sentence is a two-way claim, and the second half is as important as the first. ⚠️ An answer that uses only the first half describes a state that is a creature of society; using both halves describes a state that is a participant in a loop.

⚠️ **A necessary caution.** ❌ The chapter is not claiming that legislatures and courts have no agency, no discretion and no independent doctrine. ⚠️ Read in context, the denial is of *autarky* — of decisions originating from nowhere but the organ's own will — and it is the claim needed to justify looking outside. ⚠️ Pressed harder than that, it would contradict the chapter's own refusal to merge political science into sociology.

### 4. What therefore has to be understood

> With this framework of analysis, it becomes necessary to understand the economic, cultural, psychological and sociological aspects of the social organization in order to appreciate the full import of the political process.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.139 (printed 124)

✅ Four aspects are named, and each has an obvious donor:

| ✅ Aspect of social organization | ⚠️ Donor discipline | ✅ Where the chapter develops it |
|---|---|---|
| **Economic** | Economics | ✅ PDF pp.142-143, Lessons 23-25 |
| **Cultural** | ⚠️ Cultural anthropology and sociology | ⚠️ Distributed; ❌ no separate section |
| **Psychological** | Psychology and social psychology | ✅ PDF pp.144-145, Lesson 28 |
| **Sociological** | Sociology | ✅ PDF pp.143-144, Lessons 26-27 |

⚠️ Note that **history** is not in this list, and **philosophy** is not either — yet both receive full sections. ⚠️ The reason is visible once the list is read for what it is: these are aspects of the *social organization* as it stands, whereas history supplies the dimension of time and philosophy the dimension of evaluation. ⚠️ That is a reading, not the chapter's statement, but it makes the chapter's architecture coherent.

### 5. An example that shows the loop

⚠️ **Example.** A court interprets a statutory entitlement expansively. The interpretation responds to litigation that itself arose from a pattern of denial rooted in administrative practice and in the social position of the claimants (demand). The ruling alters what officials must do and what claimants expect (impact). The altered expectations generate the next round of litigation (the loop closing).

⚠️ **Where the example stops.** ❌ It must not be read as implying the ruling was *caused* by social demand in any strong sense. ⚠️ The doctrinal reasoning inside the judgment is the court's own, and nothing in the chapter licenses explaining it away. ⚠️ The claim is about the conditions under which the question arose and the consequences that followed — the two ends of the process, not its middle.

### 6. Comparison with the behavioural reason of Lesson 13

| | ⚠️ Behavioural reason (Lesson 13) | ⚠️ Systems reason (Lesson 14) |
|---|---|---|
| Unit | The acting individual or group | The political system as a whole |
| Argument form | Part-whole: political behaviour is one aspect of total social behaviour | Containment: the political system is a subsystem of the social system |
| Authority cited | ✅ Eulau | ⚠️ None named here; ➡️ Easton is named two pages later as the source of the concept |
| What it demands | Attention to social, cultural and personal factors | Attention to economic, cultural, psychological and sociological aspects |
| ⚠️ Relation | Complementary — openness stated at the level of the person | Complementary — the same openness stated at the level of the system |

🔑 Carrying both arguments, and saying that they are the same claim at two levels, is what turns a list into an analysis.

### 7. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** The subsystem framework begs the question. Calling the political system a subsystem of the social system builds the conclusion into the description: once you have defined politics as contained, it follows trivially that you must study the container. But the containment is a modelling choice, not a discovery, and an equally good model would treat politics and society as two interacting systems with neither inside the other — in which case the asymmetry of attention the chapter demands would disappear.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's grounds for the containment are not definitional. ✅ It argues from an observed failure: the real world of politics could not be understood **merely in terms of formal institutions which may operate differently in actual practice**, and it is that divergence between rule and practice which motivates looking outside. ⚠️ The containment model is adopted because it accounts for the divergence, not asserted in advance of it. ✅ The chapter also avoids the asymmetry the objection fears, because its relation is two-way: decisions **in turn have their impact on the social system**, and political science must **make a suitable contribution** to the other disciplines.

⚠️ **Residual.** The objection is nevertheless right that containment is a model and not a finding, and the chapter does not defend it against the two-interacting-systems alternative. ⚠️ Nothing important in the chapter turns on the difference, since both models require the same borrowing — but ⚠️ an answer that notices the difference is reading at the right level, and ❌ should not present containment as proved.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** The three-organs sentence is the sharpest single claim available for a stem about whether formal institutions can be studied in isolation.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Why is the political system studied as a subsystem?* — containment, the three organs, the four aspects, the two-way loop.
- ❌ **Trap.** Reproducing Easton's full inputs-conversion-outputs-feedback model with gatekeepers and stress. ➡️ That is Topic 08's property.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Pair this with Eulau from Lesson 13 and say explicitly that they are the same argument at two levels; examiners reward that kind of structural observation.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Systems analysis **gave an impetus** to the interdisciplinary approach — ⚠️ it did not create it.
2. ✅ The political system is **a sub-system within the ambit of the larger social system**. (PDF p.139)
3. ✅ It **does not operate independently or in isolation**.
4. ✅ The **legislature, the executive and the judiciary** do **not produce their decisions on their own initiative or at their own will**.
5. ✅ They decide **in response to the demands emanating from the social system**.
6. ✅ And those decisions **in turn have their impact on the social system**.
7. ⚠️ This sentence is absent from both canonical owners and is restored here.
8. ⚠️ The denial is of autarky, ❌ not of agency, discretion or doctrine.
9. ✅ Four aspects must therefore be understood: **economic, cultural, psychological and sociological**.
10. ⚠️ History and philosophy are not in that list yet receive full sections — ⚠️ because they supply time and evaluation rather than aspects of present social organization.
11. ⚠️ The behavioural and systems reasons are the same openness claim at two levels — person and system.
12. ⚠️ Containment is a modelling choice; the chapter adopts it because it explains the rule-practice divergence.
13. ➡️ Easton's model in detail belongs to Topic 08.

### Concept check

**Question:** State the chapter's claim about the three formal organs exactly, then state what it does **not** claim, and explain why the distinction is necessary for the chapter's own consistency.

**Model answer:** ✅ The claim is that the **legislature, the executive and the judiciary do not produce their decisions on their own initiative or at their own will, but in response to the demands emanating from the social system**, and that **these decisions in turn have their impact on the social system**. ⚠️ What it does **not** claim is that these organs lack agency, discretion or doctrine of their own; the denial is of autarky — of decisions originating from nothing but the organ's own will. ⚠️ The distinction is necessary because the chapter's conclusion refuses to **merge all social sciences into a single 'master science'** and insists that political science retains its focus. ⚠️ A strong reading, on which political decisions are simply the social system's output, would make political science a branch of sociology and contradict that conclusion. 🔑 The weak reading does all the work required — it justifies looking outside without dissolving the subject.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not use the sentence as evidence that the chapter treats courts and legislatures as passive. ⚠️ The second half of the same sentence makes them causes as well as effects, which is also what makes the chapter's demand for a contribution back to the other disciplines coherent.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Discuss how systems analysis strengthened the case for interdisciplinary political analysis. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ⚠️ It supplied a precise vocabulary for a requirement that already existed. ✅ Systems analysis **has also given an impetus** to the approach, and its core claim is that the political system is **a sub-system within the ambit of the larger social system** and **does not operate independently or in isolation**. ✅ Applied to the most formal part of the state, this yields the chapter's sharpest sentence — the **legislature, the executive and the judiciary do not produce their decisions on their own initiative or at their own will, but in response to the demands emanating from the social system**, and those decisions **in turn have their impact** on it. ✅ Hence the requirement to understand the **economic, cultural, psychological and sociological** aspects of social organization. ⚠️ The denial is of autarky, not of agency. 🔑 Containment states at system level what Eulau stated at person level.

**Rubric [10]:** the impetus formulation, with the point that systems analysis did not originate the approach **1** · the subsystem claim and the denial of independent operation **2** · the three-organs sentence in both its halves **3** · the four aspects named **2** · the autarky-not-agency qualification **1** · the link to Eulau as the same claim at another level **1**.

## Lesson 15 — Society as a totality, and the evidence a democracy question needs

Progress: 15 / 31 | Stage: Core | Part VI — what is actually borrowed: data

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §7 (the data list) and §4.4, with the Advanced companion's §4's data-and-models row, read against the chapter's sub-section headed "Use of Data of other Social Sciences" (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.139 = printed 124), which supplies the totality claim and the complete roster of data a political scientist may require to judge whether a form of government will be sustained.
CA search: "literacy income distribution ownership attitudes rural urban ratio democracy sustained data 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ no concept-linked item returned; ❌ and no contemporary statistic is introduced anywhere in this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The premise behind every borrowing of data

> The interdisciplinary approach treats society as a totality whose various aspects are closely interrelated.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.139 (printed 124)

⚠️ **Society as a totality** is absent from both canonical owners, and it is the premise that makes data-borrowing more than convenience. ⚠️ If society were a set of separable compartments, a political fact would be explicable by political facts, and reaching for an economic or sociological datum would be a confession of laziness. ⚠️ On the totality premise the compartments are an artefact of the division of academic labour, not a feature of the object.

🔑 Carry the formulation: **the disciplines are divided; the society is not.**

### 2. The question the chapter chooses

✅ The chapter then poses a single concrete question and lists what would be needed to answer it:

> you may require data on the rate of economic growth, distribution of national income, patterns of ownership of material wealth, educational levels and percentage of literacy of the population, religious and cultural attitudes of the people, their family structures, professional distribution, rural-urban ratio, etc. in order to ascertain whether a particular form of government (such as democracy) will be sustained by a given community or not.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.139 (printed 124)

⚠️ The canonical basic owner records six of these. ✅ The chapter names **nine**, and the three most often lost are the first, the fifth's first limb and the seventh.

```text
   QUESTION: will a particular form of government --
             say DEMOCRACY -- be SUSTAINED by a given
             community?

   DATA REQUIRED, as the chapter lists them:

   1  rate of ECONOMIC GROWTH                 } economics
   2  distribution of NATIONAL INCOME         }
   3  patterns of OWNERSHIP of material wealth}

   4  EDUCATIONAL LEVELS                      } sociology of
   5  percentage of LITERACY                  } education

   6  RELIGIOUS AND CULTURAL ATTITUDES        } sociology +
                                              } social psych.
   7  FAMILY STRUCTURES                       } sociology +
                                              } anthropology
   8  PROFESSIONAL DISTRIBUTION               } economics +
                                              } sociology
   9  RURAL-URBAN RATIO                       } demography /
                                              } human geography
                               ... "etc."

   NOT ONE of the nine is a political datum.
   NOT ONE of them is collected by political science.
   And the question they answer is a political question.
```

*Caption: the list is the chapter's argument in the form of a shopping list. The force of it lies in the gap between the last two lines of the caption.*

### 3. Why the chapter chose *this* question

⚠️ The choice is not accidental, and noticing why is worth marks.

- ⚠️ **It is a question only political science would ask.** No economist asks whether democracy will survive; the question belongs to political theory.
- ⚠️ **It cannot be answered by political data.** Election results tell you what happened, not whether the form will be sustained.
- ⚠️ **It is a prediction**, which is requisite (c) of the empirical approach from Lesson 11. ⚠️ The chapter is quietly demonstrating that its own requisite cannot be met without the neighbours.
- ⚠️ **It is a question with enormous practical weight**, which anticipates the later claim that political philosophy determines the **goals of public policy** while empirical study finds the facts and devises the tools.

### 4. Reading the list by discipline, and what that shows

| ⚠️ Donor | ✅ Items it supplies | ⚠️ What it tells you about the political question |
|---|---|---|
| Economics | 1, 2, 3, part of 8 | ⚠️ Whether there is a surplus to distribute, and whether its distribution creates a class with an interest in overturning the arrangement |
| Sociology | 4, 6, 7, part of 8 | ⚠️ Whether the society has the associational and educational capacity to work representative institutions, and what cleavages they must bridge |
| Social psychology | 6 | ⚠️ Whether attitudes support or corrode the legitimacy of the form |
| ⚠️ Anthropology | 7 | ⚠️ Whether the kinship form competes with or supports citizenship as a basis of claim |
| ⚠️ Demography / human geography | 9 | ⚠️ Whether the population's spatial distribution makes representation and administration feasible |

⚠️ Note that the last row returns to the straddling disciplines of Lesson 2. ⚠️ The rural-urban ratio is a human-geographical and demographic datum, and it appears here on the chapter's own list — which is one more reason the geographical approach in the conclusion is not an intrusion.

### 5. An example, and where it stops

⚠️ **Example.** Take item 3, patterns of ownership of material wealth. ⚠️ If a very small group owns most productive assets, then the formal equality of the vote coexists with a severe inequality of the means to influence outcomes — campaign resources, media access, the ability to wait out a strike or a policy. ⚠️ That makes a political prediction possible: formal democratic survival may coexist with substantive hollowing. ⚠️ Item 3 supplies the datum; the political interpretation is political science's own.

⚠️ **Where the example stops.** ❌ It does not license the claim that a given ownership pattern *determines* a given political outcome. ⚠️ The chapter's own word is that such data are **required** to ascertain whether the form will be sustained — a necessary input to a judgment, not a sufficient cause of an outcome. ⚠️ Treating it otherwise is the economic reductionism that Lesson 31 names.

### 6. Comparison: data, theories and models

| | ✅ Data | ✅ Theories | ✅ Models |
|---|---|---|---|
| What is borrowed | Measured facts about a population | ✅ Propositions with explanatory claims — elite theory, base and superstructure | ✅ Frameworks for organising analysis — the system, structural-functionalism, the open market |
| Where in the chapter | ✅ PDF p.139 — this lesson | ✅ PDF p.140 — Lesson 17 | ✅ PDF p.140 — Lessons 18-19 |
| Risk when borrowed | ⚠️ Category mismatch — the donor's definition may not be the political theorist's | ⚠️ Importing the donor's ontology with the proposition | ⚠️ Importing the donor's priorities, so the model decides what counts as a question |
| ⚠️ Reversibility | ✅ Highest — a datum can be re-described | ⚠️ Lower | ❌ Lowest — a model restructures the enquiry |

🔑 This table is worth memorising as a ladder of increasing commitment: borrowing a number is cheap, borrowing a model is expensive.

### 7. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** The totality premise proves too much and makes the purposefulness lock unworkable. If society really is a totality whose aspects are **closely interrelated**, then every datum is potentially relevant to every question, and the instruction to borrow only with the **definite purpose of understanding the problems of politics** cannot tell anyone what to leave out. The chapter's own premise defeats its own restriction.

✅ **Reply.** The premise is about the object; the lock is about the enquiry. ⚠️ That society is interrelated means no datum can be ruled out *in advance on ontological grounds*; it does not mean every datum bears on the question in hand. ✅ The chapter's nine-item list is itself the demonstration — it is a *selection*, made by asking what would bear on the sustainability of a form of government, and a different political question would generate a different list. ⚠️ Relevance is settled by the question, exactly as the lock requires.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply works but shifts the burden onto the analyst's prior judgment about which data could matter, and the chapter offers no method for forming that judgment. ⚠️ In practice the selection is theory-driven, which means the data borrowed are already shaped by the theory being tested — a circularity the chapter does not address. ⚠️ ❌ Do not attribute an answer to it; state the difficulty in your own voice.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** The nine-item list is the single most concrete illustration in the chapter and is worth reproducing in full in a 15- or 20-mark answer.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *How does political science use the data of other social sciences?* — totality premise, the question, the nine items grouped by donor, the caution against determinism.
- ❌ **Trap.** Reproducing the list without the question it answers. ⚠️ The list is only impressive because of what it is for.
- ❌ **Second trap.** Attaching contemporary figures to any of the nine items. ⚠️ The chapter is methodological and dated 2009; ❌ no statistic is asserted here.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The interdisciplinary approach treats **society as a totality whose various aspects are closely interrelated**. (PDF p.139)
2. 🔑 The disciplines are divided; the society is not.
3. ✅ The organising question: whether a particular form of government, **such as democracy**, **will be sustained by a given community**.
4. ✅ Nine data items: rate of **economic growth**; distribution of **national income**; patterns of **ownership of material wealth**; **educational levels**; **percentage of literacy**; **religious and cultural attitudes**; **family structures**; **professional distribution**; **rural-urban ratio** — ✅ followed by **etc.**
5. ⚠️ The canonical owner records six of the nine; growth, educational levels and professional distribution are the ones lost.
6. ⚠️ None of the nine is a political datum, and the question is a political question.
7. ⚠️ The question is also a **prediction** — requisite (c) of the empirical approach.
8. ⚠️ Item 9 is demographic and human-geographical, which anticipates the geographical approach of the conclusion.
9. ⚠️ Item 7 is anthropological, the fourth appearance of anthropology as a donor without its own section.
10. ⚠️ Data are **required** to ascertain; ❌ they do not determine the outcome.
11. 🔑 Ladder of commitment: data (cheapest, most reversible) → theories → models (most expensive, least reversible).
12. ⚠️ The totality premise rules nothing out ontologically; the question rules things out practically.
13. ⚠️ Selection of data is theory-driven, a circularity the chapter does not address.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter's list of nine data items contains no political datum, yet it answers a political question. Explain why that is the chapter's point rather than an embarrassment, and name the premise that makes it so.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ It is the point because the question — whether a form of government **will be sustained by a given community** — is a prediction about the relation between a political arrangement and the society that has to carry it. ⚠️ A prediction of that kind cannot be read off the arrangement itself; it requires facts about the carrier. ✅ The premise that makes this coherent is that the interdisciplinary approach treats **society as a totality whose various aspects are closely interrelated**, so that the division between economic, educational, religious, familial and spatial facts and political facts is a division of academic labour rather than of the object. ✅ Hence economics supplies growth, income distribution and ownership; sociology supplies educational levels, attitudes and family structure; demography supplies the rural-urban ratio — and political science supplies the question, the interpretation and the judgment. ⚠️ Requiring the data is not the same as being determined by them.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not conclude that political science has no data of its own. ⚠️ The chapter's behavioural section names the electorate, legislators, bureaucrats and judicial authorities as objects of political analysis; the point here is narrower — that this *particular kind of question*, a prediction about sustainability, cannot be answered from political data alone.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Comment on what political science gains by using the data of other social sciences, with reference to the chapter's own illustration. *(Comment — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The gain is the ability to answer predictive questions that political data cannot reach. ✅ The chapter's illustration asks whether a particular form of government, **such as democracy**, **will be sustained by a given community**, and lists what would be required: the **rate of economic growth**, **distribution of national income**, **patterns of ownership of material wealth**, **educational levels** and **percentage of literacy**, **religious and cultural attitudes**, **family structures**, **professional distribution** and the **rural-urban ratio**. ⚠️ Not one is a political datum, yet the question is political. ✅ The move is licensed by the premise that the approach treats **society as a totality whose various aspects are closely interrelated**. ⚠️ The data are **required** to ascertain the answer; they do not determine it, and the interpretation remains political science's own. 🔑 The disciplines are divided; the society is not.

**Rubric [10]:** a compact opening judgment on the gain **1** · the totality premise quoted or closely reported **1** · the sustainability question stated as the organising question **1** · at least seven of the nine data items **3** · the observation that none is political while the question is **2** · the required-not-determining qualification **1** · a closing formulation **1**.
