## Lesson 16 — Three interests, three donor disciplines, and Lipset

Progress: 16 / 31 | Stage: Core | Part VI — what is actually borrowed: data

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §6 (Lipset) and §7's third bullet, with the Advanced companion's §5's Lipset entry, read against the closing lines of the chapter's "Use of Data" sub-section and the block quotation that follows (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF pp.139-140 = printed 124-125), which supply the three interest-to-discipline mappings and Lipset's statement with its exact provenance.
CA search: "Lipset political development social mobilization values kinship caste legal-rational order 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked was returned. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The routing rule most answers never state

⚠️ Lesson 15 showed that political science borrows data. ⚠️ It did not say how a political scientist knows *which* discipline to go to. ✅ The chapter answers that in a single sentence, and the answer is a set of three mappings.

> The political scientists' interest in political socialization and political culture has inspired them to draw heavily from the data of psychology and sociology; their interest in political development has increased their reliance on the data provided by sociology and economics; their interest in political communication has brought them nearer to social psychology.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.139 (printed 124)

```text
   POLITICAL INTEREST              ->  DONOR DISCIPLINE(S)
   ---------------------------------------------------------
   political socialization         ->  PSYCHOLOGY + SOCIOLOGY
   political culture               ->  (drawn "heavily")

   political development           ->  SOCIOLOGY + ECONOMICS
                                       (increased "reliance")

   political communication         ->  SOCIAL PSYCHOLOGY
                                       (brought "nearer")

   READ THE VERBS. They are graded:
   "draw heavily" > "increased reliance" > "brought nearer".
   The chapter is recording three different intensities of
   dependence, not three instances of the same thing.
```

*Caption: the three mappings together constitute the chapter's only explicit routing rule — a statement of which political interest sends the analyst to which neighbour. Both canonical owners omit all three.*

### 2. Why each mapping is the right one

⚠️ Each pairing can be justified, and justifying it is what converts recall into analysis.

| ✅ Interest | ⚠️ Why those donors |
|---|---|
| **Political socialization and political culture** | ⚠️ Socialization is the acquisition of orientations by an individual — psychology's object — through institutions such as family, school and peer group — sociology's object. ⚠️ Neither discipline alone covers both the acquiring and the agency that transmits |
| **Political development** | ⚠️ Development involves a change in the social structure (mobilization, status systems, kinship) and in the material base (growth, employment, surplus). ⚠️ Sociology holds the first, economics the second |
| **Political communication** | ⚠️ Communication is the formation and movement of opinion in groups, which is exactly the object of **social** psychology as distinguished from psychology at large in Lesson 2 |

🔑 The third pairing is the sharpest evidence that the chapter's Lesson 2 taxonomy is operative throughout. ⚠️ The chapter says **social psychology**, not psychology, because the object is opinion in groups.

### 3. Lipset's statement, with its provenance

✅ The chapter illustrates the second mapping with a block quotation:

> The political scientist interested in political development has learned that he cannot treat his topic without looking for the conditions of social mobilization; men cannot become citizens in a political sense without changes in their values and personality orientations . . . Politics will be different in caste and non-caste societies. It will vary among systems which have different concepts of kinship ties, and the nature of class and status relationships in various traditional societies will affect the possibilities for acceptance of a legal-rational political order.
>
> — Seymour Martin Lipset, *Introduction to Politics and the Social Sciences* (1969), quoted at Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 (printed 125)

⚠️ The canonical owners paraphrase this; ✅ neither carries the book title or the year. ⚠️ Both are restored.

### 4. Unpacking the quotation into its four claims

⚠️ Lipset's passage is dense and contains four separable propositions. ⚠️ Separating them is what an answer should do.

```text
   CLAIM 1   political development cannot be treated without
             the conditions of SOCIAL MOBILIZATION

   CLAIM 2   men cannot become CITIZENS IN A POLITICAL SENSE
             without changes in their VALUES and PERSONALITY
             ORIENTATIONS
             -- note: citizenship here is a psychological
                achievement as well as a legal status

   CLAIM 3   politics will be different in CASTE and NON-CASTE
             societies
             -- a comparative-sociological claim

   CLAIM 4   the nature of CLASS AND STATUS RELATIONSHIPS in
             traditional societies will affect the
             POSSIBILITIES FOR ACCEPTANCE of a LEGAL-RATIONAL
             POLITICAL ORDER
             -- the most consequential claim in the passage
```

*Caption: claim 4 is the one to carry into an answer. It says that whether a rule-based, impersonal, office-bound political order can take root depends on the pre-existing structure of class and status — which converts a question about institutional design into a question about social structure.*

⚠️ **On the phrase "legal-rational".** ⚠️ The term belongs to the Weberian typology of legitimate authority, and ➡️ its full treatment belongs to **Topic 15**, which owns power, authority and legitimacy. ⚠️ Here it is used as Lipset uses it — as a label for an impersonal, rule-governed political order — and ❌ nothing more is claimed about it.

### 5. Why claim 3 is the chapter's most India-relevant sentence

⚠️ **Politics will be different in caste and non-caste societies** is the chapter's own, source-grounded, comparative claim about caste. ⚠️ That matters for this module, because the canonical owner's Indian applications are all marked as inference. ✅ This one is on the page.

⚠️ What it licenses and what it does not:

| ✅ Licensed | ❌ Not licensed |
|---|---|
| ⚠️ That the presence of caste makes a difference to political process, so a comparative political analysis must treat it as a variable | ❌ Any specific claim about how caste operates in a particular polity |
| ⚠️ That class and status relationships affect the acceptance of an impersonal legal order | ❌ Any assertion about electoral outcomes, party systems or contemporary data |
| ⚠️ That political development requires changes in values and personality orientations | ❌ Any evaluative claim about particular communities |

⚠️ ❌ No contemporary Indian statistic, party, movement or episode is named anywhere in this file.

### 6. Comparison: Eulau and Lipset

| | ⚠️ Eulau (Lesson 13) | ⚠️ Lipset (this lesson) |
|---|---|---|
| Object | The individual's political behaviour | Political development as a process |
| Argument form | Part-whole | ⚠️ Conditional necessity — development *cannot be treated without* mobilization |
| Factors named | Social, cultural, personal | ⚠️ Mobilization, values, personality orientations, caste, kinship, class and status |
| Donor disciplines implied | Sociology, anthropology, psychology | ⚠️ Sociology and economics, with social psychology for values and personality |
| ⚠️ Status in the chapter | ⚠️ The general argument | ⚠️ The worked illustration of one of the three mappings |

⚠️ Using both, and saying that Lipset instantiates the second mapping while Eulau states the general principle, is how to show the chapter has been read structurally.

### 7. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** The three mappings are a sociology of the discipline, not a methodology. They report where political scientists have in fact gone when pursuing certain interests; they do not show that those were the right places to go, and they offer no guidance for an interest not on the list. A list of three habits cannot do the work of a rule.

✅ **Reply.** The mappings are indeed descriptive in form, but they are not arbitrary, and the chapter supplies the principle that generates them. ⚠️ Each interest is routed to the discipline that owns the *object* implicated in it — socialization implicates both an acquiring individual and a transmitting institution, development implicates both social structure and material base, communication implicates opinion in groups. ✅ That is the chapter's own definition of a social science by its three objects, applied. ⚠️ So the mappings are instances of a rule that can be extended: identify the objects implicated in the political interest, and route to the disciplines that own them.

⚠️ **Residual.** The chapter never states that rule; it is reconstructed here, and ⚠️ an answer should present it as a reconstruction. ⚠️ The objection also retains force where an interest implicates an object that no single discipline owns cleanly — political geography is the obvious case, and the chapter gives it no mapping at all.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** The three mappings are the chapter's only routing rule and are entirely absent from the owners; reproducing them is a visible mark of close reading.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *How does a political scientist decide which discipline to draw on?* — the three mappings, the generating principle, then Lipset as the worked case.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing *psychology* where the chapter writes **social psychology** in the third mapping. ⚠️ The qualifier is the Lesson 2 taxonomy in operation.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Lipset's claim 4 — class and status affecting the acceptance of a legal-rational order — is a high-yield sentence for development, modernisation and institution-building stems.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Political socialization and political culture** → ✅ data of **psychology and sociology**, drawn **heavily**. (PDF p.139)
2. ✅ **Political development** → ✅ **increased reliance** on data provided by **sociology and economics**.
3. ✅ **Political communication** → ✅ brought political scientists **nearer to social psychology**.
4. 🔑 The verbs are graded: heavily > increased reliance > nearer.
5. ⚠️ All three mappings are absent from both canonical owners.
6. ✅ **Seymour Martin Lipset**, *Introduction to Politics and the Social Sciences* (**1969**), quoted at PDF p.140.
7. ✅ Claim 1: political development cannot be treated without the **conditions of social mobilization**.
8. ✅ Claim 2: men cannot become **citizens in a political sense** without changes in their **values and personality orientations**.
9. ✅ Claim 3: **politics will be different in caste and non-caste societies**.
10. ✅ Claim 4: **class and status relationships** in traditional societies affect the **possibilities for acceptance of a legal-rational political order**.
11. ⚠️ Claim 3 is the chapter's own source-grounded comparative statement about caste; ❌ no contemporary Indian claim follows from it here.
12. ➡️ *Legal-rational* belongs doctrinally to Topic 15 and is used here only as Lipset's label.
13. ⚠️ The generating principle behind the mappings — route to the discipline that owns the implicated object — is a reconstruction, ❌ not the chapter's stated rule.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter routes political communication to **social** psychology rather than to psychology. Explain why that is consistent with the chapter's opening taxonomy, and state the general rule the three mappings instantiate.

**Model answer:** ✅ The opening taxonomy separates **clinical psychology**, which belongs to the physical sciences, from **social psychology**, which **obviously falls in the domain of social sciences**, because the latter studies the individual as a member of society. ⚠️ Political communication is the formation and movement of opinion within groups, so its object is behaviour-in-association, not the individual mind as such; routing it to social psychology applies the taxonomy rather than merely using a longer name. ⚠️ The general rule the three mappings instantiate is that a political interest is routed to the discipline that owns the object implicated in it: socialization implicates both an acquiring individual and transmitting institutions, hence **psychology and sociology**; development implicates both social structure and material base, hence **sociology and economics**. ⚠️ The rule is reconstructed from the chapter's practice, not stated by it.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the three mappings as an exhaustive list of permissible routes. ⚠️ They are the chapter's worked instances; the conclusion names seven partial approaches, including legal and geographical, for which no mapping is given anywhere in the text.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Discuss how the chapter determines which social science a political enquiry should draw upon, using its own illustrations. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The chapter supplies three routings. ✅ Interest in **political socialization and political culture** has inspired political scientists to draw **heavily** from the data of **psychology and sociology**; interest in **political development** has increased reliance on **sociology and economics**; interest in **political communication** has brought them **nearer to social psychology**. ⚠️ The verbs grade the dependence, not repeat it. ⚠️ Underlying them is a reconstructable rule: route the enquiry to the discipline owning the object implicated in the political interest. ✅ **Lipset** illustrates the second — political development cannot be treated without the **conditions of social mobilization**, men become **citizens in a political sense** only through changes in **values and personality orientations**, **politics will be different in caste and non-caste societies**, and **class and status relationships** affect acceptance of a **legal-rational political order**. 🔑 Interest selects object; object selects discipline. ⚠️ No routing is offered for legal or geographical enquiry.

**Rubric [10]:** all three mappings with their correct donors **3** · the graded verbs noticed **1** · the object-selects-discipline rule, marked as reconstruction **2** · at least three of Lipset's four claims, with attribution **3** · the gap for legal and geographical routes **1**.

## Lesson 17 — Borrowed models I: elite theory and base-superstructure

Progress: 17 / 31 | Stage: Core | Part VII — what is actually borrowed: theories and models

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §14 (named borrowed models) and §6, with the Advanced companion's §3.7 and §4's data-and-models row, read against the opening of the chapter's sub-section headed "Use of Theories and Models of Other Social Sciences" (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 = printed 125), which supplies the elite theory with its parenthetical definition, its three founders with their dates, its donor discipline and its four applications, and the base-and-superstructure framework with its parenthetical definition and its donor disciplines.
CA search: "elite theory Pareto Mosca Michels base superstructure borrowed into political science 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nil — the two models examined were formulated between the 1890s and the 1930s and attract no current reporting. ❌ None is manufactured.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Moving up the ladder of commitment

⚠️ Lesson 15 ended with a ladder: data are cheap to borrow, models are expensive. ✅ The chapter now moves to the expensive end, and it opens with a general statement before giving instances:

> The interdisciplinary orientation has also encouraged political scientists to make an abundant use of the theories and models evolved by other social sciences.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 (printed 125)

⚠️ **Abundant** is a strong word and it is the chapter's own. ⚠️ What follows is not a handful of curiosities but a claim that a large part of the analytical apparatus of political science was built elsewhere.

### Part A — elite theory, and what it denies political science

### 2. Elite theory: the definition the owners omit

✅ The chapter defines elite theory inside a parenthesis, and the definition is examinable:

> the elite theory (that in every society, irrespective of its forms of social and political institutions, a small group of the chosen few always assumes a dominant position in relation to the rest of the community)
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 (printed 125)

⚠️ Three features of this definition are worth isolating, and none appears in either canonical owner, which name elite theory only as a label.

| ✅ Element | ⚠️ What it commits the theory to |
|---|---|
| **In every society** | ⚠️ A universal claim, not a historical generalisation |
| **Irrespective of its forms of social and political institutions** | ⚠️ Institutional design is explicitly declared irrelevant — which is a direct challenge to constitutional political science |
| **A small group of the chosen few** | ⚠️ A claim about size and selection |
| **Always assumes a dominant position** | ⚠️ A claim about outcome, and the word **always** makes it exceptionless |

🔑 The second row is the reason elite theory mattered so much to political science: it says that the thing political science traditionally studied — the form of institutions — does not determine who actually rules.

### 3. The three founders, and the donor discipline

✅ The chapter names them with dates and places the theory's origin:

> was first systematically developed by Pareto (1848-1923), Mosca (1858-1941) and Michels (1876-1936) in the field of sociology.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 (printed 125)

⚠️ **Pareto 1848-1923**, **Mosca 1858-1941**, **Michels 1876-1936**, and the donor field is **sociology**. ⚠️ All of this is absent from the owners.

### 4. The four applications

✅ The chapter then states what political science did with the import:

> It was then applied to the analysis of political institutions, leadership and group behaviour, as also for giving new interpretations of democracy.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 (printed 125)

```text
   SOCIOLOGY
   (Pareto, Mosca, Michels)
        |
        |  ELITE THEORY exported
        v
   POLITICAL SCIENCE applies it to:

   1  analysis of POLITICAL INSTITUTIONS
   2  analysis of LEADERSHIP
   3  analysis of GROUP BEHAVIOUR
   4  NEW INTERPRETATIONS OF DEMOCRACY   <-- the big one

   Application 4 is the reason this import mattered.
   A theory that says a small group always dominates,
   whatever the institutions, forces a re-description of
   what democracy can possibly mean.
```

*Caption: the fourth application is where a borrowed sociological proposition reaches into normative political theory. Note that the chapter says "new interpretations", not "refutation" — the theory reshaped the concept rather than destroying it.*

⚠️ ➡️ What those new interpretations are — competitive elitism, the elitist-pluralist debate, democratic elitism — belongs to **Topic 22 and Topic 23**, which own democracy and its contemporary variants. ❌ This topic owns only the fact and manner of the import.

### Part B — base and superstructure, and the comparison

### 5. Base and superstructure: the second instance

✅ The chapter's second model, again with a parenthetical definition:

> Similarly, the Marxian framework of 'base and superstructure' (which postulates that politics, culture, morals, ideas and institutions in any society are shaped by the prevailing mode of material production) was first evolved in the field of economics and sociology, and then adopted as a tool of political analysis.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 (printed 125)

⚠️ Two things to notice, both absent from the owners.

- ✅ The parenthetical definition names **five** items shaped by the mode of material production: **politics, culture, morals, ideas and institutions**. ⚠️ Not politics alone.
- ✅ The donor is **economics and sociology** — two fields, not one. ⚠️ That is unusual and worth remarking: the chapter treats the base-superstructure framework as having been evolved jointly, which fits its own account of Marx and Engels as developing an approach **in social sciences** rather than in any one of them.

### 6. Comparison: two models, two different kinds of challenge

| | ✅ Elite theory | ✅ Base and superstructure |
|---|---|---|
| Donor field | ✅ Sociology | ✅ Economics **and** sociology |
| Founders named | ✅ Pareto, Mosca, Michels, with dates | ⚠️ Marxian; ✅ Marx and Engels dated earlier in the chapter |
| Core proposition | ✅ A small group of the chosen few always dominates, **irrespective of institutions** | ✅ Politics, culture, morals, ideas and institutions are shaped by the **prevailing mode of material production** |
| ⚠️ What it denies political science | ⚠️ That institutional form determines who rules | ⚠️ That politics has an independent source |
| ⚠️ Severity | ⚠️ Serious — the object of study loses explanatory primacy | ❌ More serious — the object of study loses autonomy altogether |
| ✅ Application recorded | ✅ Four, including new interpretations of democracy | ✅ **Adopted as a tool of political analysis** |

⚠️ The right-hand column is why Topic 08 treats Marxian analysis as **political analysis by proxy**. ➡️ That verdict belongs there; here the point is narrower — that a model can be imported even when its own premises are hostile to the importing discipline's autonomy.

### 7. The charge of self-defeat, and the reply

⚠️ **Objection.** Importing a model whose central claim denies the importer's subject matter is incoherent. Elite theory says institutions are irrelevant to domination; base-superstructure says politics is shaped elsewhere. A political science that adopts either has adopted a reason not to exist, and calling this *interdisciplinarity* disguises what is really surrender.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's own framework separates the model from the thesis. ⚠️ A model is an apparatus for organising analysis; the thesis is a substantive claim made with it. ⚠️ Political science can use the elite framework to analyse **political institutions, leadership and group behaviour** — three objects it continues to own — without accepting that institutional form is uniformly irrelevant; the import gives it a hypothesis to test, which is exactly implication (b). ✅ And the chapter's conclusion forecloses the surrender reading: the approach **does not seek to merge all social sciences into a single 'master science'**. ⚠️ The borrowed proposition becomes a candidate explanation, not a constitution.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply is sound in principle and fragile in practice, because a model carries its donor's ontology with it, and the categories it supplies shape what the importer can see. ⚠️ If one analyses leadership exclusively through the elite framework, the finding of elite domination is substantially guaranteed by the apparatus. ⚠️ The chapter has no safeguard against this beyond the purposefulness lock, and ⚠️ Lesson 31 names it as the family of reductionisms.

### 8. Using the two imports in an answer

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** The two parenthetical definitions, the three founders with dates and the four applications are the highest-density factual material in the chapter.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Illustrate the use of theories and models of other social sciences in political analysis* — elite theory in full, then base-superstructure, then Lesson 18's relay.
- ❌ **Trap.** Naming elite theory without its definition. ⚠️ The phrase **irrespective of its forms of social and political institutions** is what makes the import significant.
- ➡️ **Boundary.** Competitive elitism and the elitist-pluralist debate belong to the democracy topics; historical materialism belongs to the Marxism topic. ⚠️ Keep this lesson to the import itself.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Political scientists make **abundant use** of theories and models evolved by other social sciences. (PDF p.140)
2. ✅ **Elite theory**: in **every society**, **irrespective of its forms of social and political institutions**, a **small group of the chosen few always assumes a dominant position** in relation to the rest of the community.
3. 🔑 **Irrespective of institutions** is the clause that mattered to political science.
4. ✅ First systematically developed by **Pareto (1848-1923)**, **Mosca (1858-1941)** and **Michels (1876-1936)**.
5. ✅ Donor field: **sociology**.
6. ✅ Four applications: **political institutions**, **leadership**, **group behaviour**, and **new interpretations of democracy**.
7. ⚠️ The definition, the three founders with dates, the donor field and the four applications are all absent from both owners.
8. ✅ **Base and superstructure**: politics, **culture, morals, ideas and institutions** in any society are shaped by the **prevailing mode of material production**.
9. ✅ Donor fields: **economics and sociology** — two, not one.
10. ✅ It was **then adopted as a tool of political analysis**.
11. ⚠️ Elite theory denies institutional primacy; base-superstructure denies political autonomy — a difference of severity.
12. ⚠️ A model can be imported even when its premises are hostile to the importer's autonomy, because model and thesis are separable.
13. ⚠️ But a model carries its donor's ontology, which is the standing risk named at Lesson 31.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the chapter's parenthetical definition of elite theory matter more to political science than the names of its founders?

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Because the definition contains the clause that bears on political science's own subject matter. ✅ Elite theory holds that in **every society**, **irrespective of its forms of social and political institutions**, a **small group of the chosen few always assumes a dominant position**. ⚠️ The italicised middle clause declares institutional design irrelevant to who actually dominates — and institutional design is what traditional political science principally studied. ⚠️ That is why the import could not be absorbed quietly: it forced a re-description of political institutions as possibly epiphenomenal, and it produced, on the chapter's own list, **new interpretations of democracy**. ✅ The founders — **Pareto (1848-1923)**, **Mosca (1858-1941)** and **Michels (1876-1936)**, working **in the field of sociology** — establish provenance and are worth citing, but provenance does not generate the challenge; the universal-and-institution-indifferent form of the proposition does.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the import of elite theory as political science accepting that democracy is a sham. ⚠️ The chapter records that it was applied **for giving new interpretations of democracy** — a reinterpretation of the concept, not its abandonment — and the substantive debate belongs to the democracy topics, not here.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine what political science borrowed when it borrowed elite theory and the base-superstructure framework. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ⚠️ It borrowed two propositions that each demote something political science had treated as primary. ✅ **Elite theory**, first systematically developed by **Pareto (1848-1923)**, **Mosca (1858-1941)** and **Michels (1876-1936)** in **sociology**, holds that in every society, **irrespective of its forms of social and political institutions**, a **small group of the chosen few always assumes a dominant position**; it was applied to **political institutions, leadership and group behaviour**, and to **new interpretations of democracy**. ✅ The Marxian **base and superstructure** framework, evolved in **economics and sociology**, postulates that **politics, culture, morals, ideas and institutions** are shaped by the **prevailing mode of material production**, and was **adopted as a tool of political analysis**. ⚠️ The first denies institutional primacy; the second denies political autonomy. 🔑 Each arrived as a hypothesis to be tested, not as a constitution — though each carries its donor's ontology with it.

**Rubric [10]:** elite theory's definition, with the institution-indifference clause **3** · the three founders with dates and the donor field **2** · the four applications **2** · the base-superstructure definition with its five shaped items and two donor fields **2** · the contrast in what each demotes **1**.

## Lesson 18 — Borrowed models II: Easton's system and the functional relay

Progress: 18 / 31 | Stage: Core | Part VII — what is actually borrowed: theories and models

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §14 (Easton's framework as a borrowed model) and §6, with the Advanced companion's §5's Easton entry and §3.7, read against the chapter's paragraph on contemporary approaches that originated in other social sciences (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 = printed 125), which supplies the derivation of Easton's political-system concept and the complete three-stage, four-name transmission of the structural-functional approach.
CA search: "structural functional approach Malinowski Radcliffe-Brown Parsons Almond Powell transmission 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked was returned. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. A stronger claim than the last lesson's

⚠️ Lesson 17 showed political science borrowing propositions. ✅ The chapter now makes a stronger claim:

> Some contemporary approaches to the study of politics in fact originated in other social sciences.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 (printed 125)

⚠️ Note **approaches**, not theories. ⚠️ In the vocabulary of the sibling chapter, an approach supplies criteria of selection — it decides what counts as data before any investigation begins. ⚠️ To say that contemporary political **approaches** originated elsewhere is to say that the discipline's criteria of selection were, in part, written by its neighbours.

### 2. Easton's concept, and what it was derived from

> David Easton's concept of the political system is derived from the concept of the social system originally developed in the field of sociology.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 (printed 125)

✅ Both canonical owners carry this; ⚠️ it is the one model-import they do record.

⚠️ What they do not draw out is the consequence. The containment argument of Lesson 14 — the political system as a subsystem of the social system — is not an independent political discovery. ⚠️ It is a direct inheritance: if the **political** system is modelled on the **social** system, then the relation between them is built into the model's vocabulary from the outset. 🔑 The chapter's most-used premise is therefore itself a borrowing, and saying so in an answer shows the chapter has been read as a single argument rather than as a sequence of sections.

### 3. The structural-functional relay, restored in full

✅ The chapter traces one approach across three disciplines and four names:

> The structural-functional approach was originally developed in the field of cultural anthropology by Malinowski and Radcliffe-Brown, then adopted in sociology by Talcott Parsons, and subsequently introduced in political science by Almond and Powell.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 (printed 125)

```text
   CULTURAL ANTHROPOLOGY
   Malinowski · Radcliffe-Brown
             |
             |  adopted in
             v
   SOCIOLOGY
   Talcott Parsons
             |
             |  introduced in
             v
   POLITICAL SCIENCE
   Almond · Powell

   THREE disciplines. FOUR names. TWO transfers.
   This is the chapter's single most complete demonstration
   that an approach can travel -- and that political science
   is at the END of a chain, not at its head.
```

*Caption: the relay is the chapter's best evidence for its central thesis, and both canonical owners reduce it to the phrase "structural-functionalism (from sociology/anthropology)" — which loses all four names, both transfers and the direction of travel.*

⚠️ Why the relay matters more than a single transfer:

- ⚠️ It shows the apparatus was **twice adapted** before it reached politics, so what political science received had already been reshaped by a discipline that was not its source.
- ⚠️ It shows interdisciplinary traffic is not bilateral. ⚠️ Anthropology and political science have, on this account, no direct relation here at all; sociology mediated.
- ⚠️ It supplies a concrete answer to the question of what political science contributes back — the reshaping it performs becomes available to the next discipline in the chain, which is implication (c) in action.

### 4. What the approach brought with it

⚠️ The chapter does not expound structural-functionalism here, and ➡️ **Topic 08** owns its full treatment — functions, structures, the claim that the same function may be performed by different structures, the measurement of political development and the charge of conservative bias.

⚠️ What this topic needs is only the import's shape:

| ⚠️ Element imported | ⚠️ Donor's original purpose | ⚠️ Political use |
|---|---|---|
| The idea of a **function** | ⚠️ Anthropology: explaining the persistence of a practice in a society without written records or formal offices | ⚠️ Comparing political systems that have no institution in common |
| The idea of a **structure** | ⚠️ Anthropology and sociology: the arrangement that performs the function | ⚠️ Separating what a political system does from the offices through which it does it |
| ⚠️ The pairing of the two | ⚠️ Sociology, in Parsons's systematising hands | ⚠️ A comparative framework usable across very different polities |

⚠️ The first row is the key to why the approach had to come from anthropology. ⚠️ Anthropology faced the problem of describing order in societies without the familiar institutional furniture; that is precisely the problem comparative politics faced when it extended beyond Western constitutional states.

### 5. An example, and its limit

⚠️ **Example.** Two polities both manage the articulation of demands. One does it through organised interest associations; the other through kin networks, local notables and informal patronage. ⚠️ An institutional comparison finds no common object. ⚠️ A functional comparison finds the same function discharged by different structures, and the comparison becomes possible.

⚠️ **Where the example stops.** ❌ It must not be taken to show that the two arrangements are equivalent. ⚠️ Functional equivalence establishes that a task is performed, not that it is performed on the same terms, with the same inclusiveness or with the same consequences for those excluded. ⚠️ The conservative-bias criticism of the approach is exactly the charge that functional language flattens those differences, and ➡️ it is treated where the approach itself is owned.

### 6. Comparison: three ways a model can arrive

| ⚠️ Route | ✅ Instance in the chapter | ⚠️ What political science received |
|---|---|---|
| Direct import of a **proposition** | ✅ Elite theory, from sociology | ⚠️ A hypothesis about who dominates |
| Direct import of a **concept** | ✅ Easton's political system, from the sociological social system | ⚠️ A vocabulary that fixed the political system's relation to its environment |
| ⚠️ **Relayed** import of a whole **approach** | ✅ Structural-functionalism: anthropology → sociology → political science | ⚠️ Criteria of selection, already twice adapted |

🔑 The three rows are a usable taxonomy of borrowing, and the chapter supplies an instance of each on a single page.

### 7. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** If the discipline's contemporary approaches originated elsewhere, then political science has no methodological identity. Its criteria of selection — what counts as a question, what counts as data — were set by anthropologists and sociologists. Whatever the chapter says about retaining focus, a discipline that does not write its own criteria of selection is a client discipline.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's claim is **some** contemporary approaches, not all, and the reply has three parts. ⚠️ First, adoption is adaptation: what Almond and Powell introduced into political science was not what Radcliffe-Brown developed, precisely because it passed through Parsons and then through a political problem. ✅ Second, the chapter insists the traffic runs outward too, requiring a **suitable contribution** to the understanding of the donor disciplines. ⚠️ Third, and decisively, the chapter's test of identity is **focus** — the political question — not the provenance of the apparatus. ⚠️ A borrowed instrument pointed at one's own question remains one's own enquiry.

⚠️ **Residual.** The third part of the reply is the vulnerable one, and it is the same vulnerability identified at Lessons 5 and 8: identity resting on focus is identity resting on intention. ⚠️ Where a borrowed approach substantially determines which questions can be asked — and an approach, by definition, supplies criteria of selection — the distinction between instrument and identity becomes thin. ⚠️ The chapter does not confront this, and an answer that names it is adding something real.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** The four-name relay is the chapter's most quotable single demonstration and is almost never reproduced correctly.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Illustrate with examples how models from other social sciences have been adopted in political science* — Easton, then the relay, then Lesson 19's economic models.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing "structural-functionalism came from sociology" and stopping. ⚠️ Its origin is **cultural anthropology**; sociology was the intermediate stage.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** Observing that Easton's borrowing supplies the chapter's own subsystem premise is a high-value structural remark.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Some **contemporary approaches** to the study of politics **in fact originated in other social sciences**. (PDF p.140)
2. ⚠️ **Approaches**, not merely theories — criteria of selection, not only propositions.
3. ✅ **David Easton's** concept of the **political system** is derived from the concept of the **social system** originally developed in **sociology**.
4. 🔑 The chapter's own subsystem premise is therefore itself a borrowing.
5. ✅ The **structural-functional approach** was originally developed in **cultural anthropology** by **Malinowski** and **Radcliffe-Brown**.
6. ✅ Then **adopted in sociology** by **Talcott Parsons**.
7. ✅ Then **introduced in political science** by **Almond and Powell**.
8. ⚠️ Three disciplines, four names, two transfers — the owners reduce this to a parenthetical phrase.
9. ⚠️ The relay shows traffic is not bilateral: anthropology reached politics only through sociology.
10. ⚠️ The function idea came from anthropology because anthropology faced order without familiar institutions — the same problem comparative politics later faced.
11. ⚠️ Functional equivalence shows a task is performed, ❌ not that it is performed on equal terms.
12. 🔑 Three routes of arrival: proposition (elite theory), concept (Easton's system), relayed approach (structural-functionalism).
13. ➡️ The doctrine of structural-functional analysis and its conservative-bias criticism belong to Topic 08.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is the structural-functional relay better evidence for the chapter's thesis than Easton's single borrowing, and what does it reveal that a bilateral transfer could not?

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Because it shows an entire **approach** — criteria of selection, not a single concept — crossing two disciplinary boundaries and being adapted at each. ✅ It was **originally developed in the field of cultural anthropology by Malinowski and Radcliffe-Brown**, **then adopted in sociology by Talcott Parsons**, and **subsequently introduced in political science by Almond and Powell**. ⚠️ Three things follow that a bilateral transfer cannot show: that what political science received had already been reshaped by an intermediate discipline, so the import was not the original; that interdisciplinary traffic can be indirect, since anthropology and political science have no direct relation in this chain; and that political science stands at the end of a chain rather than at its head. ✅ Easton's case, by contrast, is a single concept — the **political system** derived from the sociological **social system** — important because it supplies the chapter's own subsystem premise, but a one-step transfer.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not attribute structural-functionalism to sociology as its origin. ⚠️ Sociology was the middle stage; the origin is **cultural anthropology**, and dropping that stage removes the chapter's best evidence that an approach can travel across more than one boundary.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "Political science stands at the end of a chain of methodological transmission, not at its head." Discuss with reference to the structural-functional approach. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The chapter's own relay supports the proposition. The structural-functional approach was **originally developed in the field of cultural anthropology by Malinowski and Radcliffe-Brown**, **then adopted in sociology by Talcott Parsons**, and **subsequently introduced in political science by Almond and Powell**. ⚠️ Political science is the third discipline in the chain and received an apparatus already twice adapted. ⚠️ The same holds for the chapter's own governing premise: **David Easton's concept of the political system is derived from the concept of the social system originally developed in the field of sociology**, so the subsystem framework on which the chapter relies is itself an import. ✅ The chapter nonetheless denies that this costs the discipline its identity, since adoption is adaptation and the traffic must run outward as a **suitable contribution**. ⚠️ Identity then rests on focus rather than on provenance — ⚠️ a defensible but not an unassailable position.

**Rubric [10]:** the relay stated with all four names and both transfers **3** · the point that the import was already adapted **1** · Easton's derivation and its consequence for the chapter's own premise **3** · the adaptation-and-contribution reply **2** · an honest note on the limits of identity-by-focus **1**.

Book context: Canonical Core owner §14 (Lasswell's problem-solving model, market/bargaining analogies) and §6, with the Advanced companion's §5's Lasswell entry, read against the closing lines of the chapter's "Use of Theories and Models" sub-section (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 = printed 125), which supply Lasswell's model and its donor discipline, the derivation of bargaining from the theories of competition, the two named forms of competition, and Schumpeter and Downs on the economist's model of the open market.

### 1. The last three imports, and the most ambitious of them

⚠️ The chapter closes its models sub-section with three further instances, and the last of them is the boldest in the chapter: an attempt to analyse democratic politics with an economist's model of the market.

### 2. Lasswell's problem-solving model

> Similarly, Harold Lasswell's model of the 'problem-solving' approach was derived from psychology.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 (printed 125)

✅ Both owners record this. ⚠️ One point of care is needed, and it is a provenance point rather than a doctrinal one.

⚠️ The canonical basic owner adds that the model reframes political inquiry **around identifying problems, generating alternatives and evaluating outcomes**. 🔶 That gloss does **not** appear in the chapter, which says only that the model was derived from psychology. 🔶 It is owner-supplied content and is marked as such here. ❌ Do not attribute the three-stage gloss to Gauba.

⚠️ What the chapter does supply is the donor discipline, and that is the examinable fact: **psychology**, not organisational theory, not management science.

### 3. Bargaining, and the theories it rests on

> Understanding of politics as a process of bargaining is based on the theories of competition (e.g. perfect competition and monopolistic competition) originally evolved in the field of economics.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 (printed 125)

⚠️ Two named forms of competition — **perfect competition** and **monopolistic competition** — are absent from both owners, which record only "market/bargaining analogies (from economics)".

⚠️ Why naming them matters:

| ✅ Named form | ⚠️ What it assumes | ⚠️ What it suggests about politics when transferred |
|---|---|---|
| **Perfect competition** | ⚠️ Many participants, none able to set terms alone; comparable products; open entry and exit; adequate information | ⚠️ A politics of many comparable actors where no one dictates, and outcomes emerge from the interaction — the idealised pluralist picture |
| **Monopolistic competition** | ⚠️ Many participants, but each offering a **differentiated** product, so each has some power over its own niche | ⚠️ A politics of parties and groups each with a captive constituency and some pricing power over it — a far better description of actual party competition |

🔑 The second row is the high-yield observation. ⚠️ A candidate who notices that the chapter names *monopolistic* competition and not merely competition has a ready-made analytical point: party systems look much more like differentiated-product markets than like perfect ones. ⚠️ This is an inference from the named model, ❌ not a claim the chapter makes.

### 4. Schumpeter and Downs

> Schumpeter and Anthony Downs have sought to analyse democratic politics using the economist's model of the open market.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 (printed 125)

⚠️ **Neither name appears anywhere in either canonical owner.** ⚠️ They are restored here, and they are the most examinable names in the models sub-section after the relay of Lesson 18.

```text
   THE ECONOMIST'S MODEL OF THE OPEN MARKET
                     |
                     |  applied to DEMOCRATIC POLITICS by
                     v
          SCHUMPETER  ·  ANTHONY DOWNS
                     |
                     v
   What the transfer supplies:
   -- VOTERS modelled as choosers
   -- PARTIES/LEADERS modelled as competitors seeking
      something analogous to custom
   -- the ELECTION modelled as the mechanism of competitive
      selection
   -- DEMOCRACY redescribed as a METHOD rather than as a
      set of ends

   NOTE WHAT THE CHAPTER DOES AND DOES NOT SAY.
   It records THAT they used the model. It does not
   expound either theory. The expansion above is an
   explanation of what such a transfer involves, not an
   attribution to the chapter.
```

*Caption: the chapter's sentence is a single clause. The diagram shows what that clause commits a transfer to; it is marked as explanation, and no doctrine is attributed to Gauba beyond the clause itself.*

➡️ **Boundary.** The substantive theories — competitive elitism, the economic theory of democracy, the median-voter reasoning and their criticisms — are owned by the democracy topics in this module and, for the Optional, by the socio-political owners. ❌ They are not expounded here. ⚠️ What this topic owns is the fact of the transfer and what it shows about interdisciplinarity.

### 5. The chapter's own closing verdict on models

> Thus, the use of theories and models of other social sciences in the realm of political science is quite common.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.140 (printed 125)

⚠️ **Quite common** is deliberately flat. ⚠️ After a page that has named elite theory, base-superstructure, Easton's system, the structural-functional relay, Lasswell's model, competition theory and the open-market model, the chapter declines to draw a dramatic conclusion. ⚠️ That restraint is itself the chapter's position: borrowing is ordinary practice, not a crisis and not a triumph.

✅ And the transition that follows sets up the whole second half: it would be **interesting to illustrate the usefulness of other social sciences in the study of politics** — which is the sentence that opens the five "use of" sections.

### 6. Comparison: six imports on one page

| ✅ Import | ✅ Donor | ✅ What kind of thing it is |
|---|---|---|
| Elite theory | Sociology | ⚠️ A universal proposition |
| Base and superstructure | Economics and sociology | ⚠️ A framework of determination |
| Easton's political system | Sociology | ⚠️ A concept |
| Structural-functional approach | ⚠️ Cultural anthropology → sociology → political science | ⚠️ A whole approach, relayed |
| Lasswell's problem-solving model | Psychology | ⚠️ A model of how enquiry should proceed |
| Bargaining and the open market | Economics | ⚠️ An analogy carrying a theory of competition |

🔑 Six imports, five donor fields, one page. ⚠️ Reproducing this table is the fastest way to evidence the chapter's claim that such borrowing is **abundant**.

### 7. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** The market analogy is the clearest case of a borrowing that distorts its object. Markets presuppose divisible goods, voluntary exchange and the possibility of exit; politics involves indivisible collective decisions, coercive authority and no exit for those bound by the outcome. A model built on the first set of assumptions cannot describe the second without systematically misrepresenting it — and the misrepresentation is not random but ideological, since it makes a competitive, aggregative conception of democracy look like the natural one.

✅ **Reply.** Three things can be said from the chapter's own resources. ⚠️ First, the chapter reports the transfer as something that has been attempted — Schumpeter and Downs **have sought to analyse** democratic politics this way — and does not endorse it. ⚠️ Second, the chapter's naming of **monopolistic** competition alongside perfect competition shows the imported apparatus is not confined to the idealised case, which blunts the charge that it must flatter the market picture. ✅ Third, the purposefulness lock applies with full force here: a model is admissible for understanding a political problem and is not a licence to re-describe politics in the donor's own terms.

⚠️ **Residual.** The objection's strongest limb survives. ⚠️ A model does not merely answer questions; it shapes which questions are askable, and an analysis conducted entirely in market terms will struggle to formulate questions about coercion, obligation and the position of those who lose. ⚠️ The chapter supplies no method for detecting this, and ⚠️ its only general answer — that philosophy determines the **goals of public policy** while empirical study finds facts and devises tools — arrives six pages later and is not connected to this passage.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** Schumpeter and Downs are the most valuable restored names in this lesson; the two named forms of competition are the most valuable restored concept.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Can politics be analysed as a market?* — the transfer, what it supplies, the three disanalogies, and the chapter's restraint.
- ❌ **Trap.** Attributing to Gauba the three-stage gloss on Lasswell's model. 🔶 That is owner-supplied.
- ➡️ **Boundary.** Do not expound the economic theory of democracy; the democracy topics own it.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Harold Lasswell's** model of the **'problem-solving'** approach was **derived from psychology**. (PDF p.140)
2. 🔶 The gloss about identifying problems, generating alternatives and evaluating outcomes is owner-supplied; ❌ it is not in the chapter.
3. ✅ Politics as a **process of bargaining** rests on the **theories of competition**, **originally evolved in the field of economics**.
4. ✅ Two forms are named: **perfect competition** and **monopolistic competition**.
5. ⚠️ Both named forms are absent from the owners; the second is the analytically richer transfer.
6. ✅ **Schumpeter** and **Anthony Downs** sought to analyse democratic politics using the **economist's model of the open market**.
7. ⚠️ Neither name appears in either canonical owner; both are restored here.
8. ✅ The chapter's verdict: such use is **quite common** — ⚠️ deliberately flat.
9. ✅ The transition: it would be **interesting to illustrate the usefulness of other social sciences in the study of politics**.
10. 🔑 Six imports on one page, from five donor fields.
11. ⚠️ Market disanalogies: indivisible decisions, coercive authority, no exit.
12. ⚠️ A model shapes which questions are askable — the standing risk the chapter does not address here.
13. ➡️ The economic theory of democracy belongs to the democracy topics, not to this one.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter names *monopolistic competition* alongside *perfect competition* as the economic theories on which the bargaining conception of politics rests. What analytical difference does the second name make, and what must be said about the status of that observation?

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Perfect competition assumes many participants, comparable offerings and no single actor able to set terms; transferred to politics it yields an idealised picture in which outcomes emerge from interaction among equivalent competitors. ⚠️ **Monopolistic competition** assumes many participants each offering a **differentiated** product, so each enjoys some power within its own niche. ⚠️ Transferred, it describes party and group competition far better: parties are not interchangeable, each holds a constituency with some loyalty, and competition runs between differentiated offerings rather than identical ones. ⚠️ The analytical gain is a model that can accommodate partial insulation from competitive pressure, which the perfect-competition analogy cannot. ❌ The status of this observation must be stated honestly: the chapter names both forms but draws no such contrast, so the inference is a reading of the named model, not a claim of the chapter.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not attribute the economic theory of democracy's substance to this chapter. ⚠️ It records only that **Schumpeter and Anthony Downs have sought to analyse democratic politics using the economist's model of the open market**; the doctrines themselves, and their criticisms, belong to the democracy owners.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Critically examine the transfer of economic models into the analysis of democratic politics. *(Critically examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The chapter records that understanding politics as a **process of bargaining** is based on the **theories of competition**, naming **perfect competition** and **monopolistic competition**, **originally evolved in the field of economics**, and that **Schumpeter and Anthony Downs have sought to analyse democratic politics using the economist's model of the open market**. ⚠️ The transfer supplies a vocabulary of choosers, competitors and a selection mechanism, and redescribes democracy as a method rather than a set of ends. ⚠️ Its limits are structural: political decisions are indivisible and binding, authority is coercive, and those who lose cannot exit the outcome as a consumer leaves a market. ⚠️ The named inclusion of **monopolistic** competition softens but does not remove the difficulty, since a model still shapes which questions can be asked. 🔑 The chapter's own restraint is instructive — it calls such borrowing **quite common** and endorses nothing.

**Rubric [10]:** the bargaining derivation with both named forms of competition **3** · Schumpeter and Downs named with the open-market model **2** · what the transfer supplies **2** · at least two structural disanalogies **2** · the chapter's non-endorsement noticed **1**.

## Lesson 20 — The use of history I: two contexts, not one

Progress: 20 / 31 | Stage: Core | Part VIII — the five uses expounded, discipline by discipline

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §8's history row and §5, with the Advanced companion's §6's historical perspective and §8's historical-verification route, read against the opening of the chapter's section headed "THE USE OF HISTORY" (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.141 = printed 126), which supplies the two lettered contexts, the three uses inside the first, and the laws-of-historical-development clause with its named instance.
CA search: "history as storehouse of data verification of hypotheses about politics laws of historical development 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nil — a methodological claim about how past evidence is used generates no datable present event. ❌ None is manufactured.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Why history comes first among the five

⚠️ The chapter has promised to **illustrate the usefulness of other social sciences in the study of politics**, and it begins with history — which, as Lesson 1 noted, it had not listed among the social sciences. ⚠️ The chapter does not explain the order. ⚠️ Two readings are available and both are defensible: history is the oldest companion of political study, and history is the only discipline that supplies the dimension of **time**, without which no claim about development, sustainability or causation across periods can be tested at all.

### 2. The two contexts, lettered

> The use of history for the study of politics may be considered in two important contexts: (a) history is used as a rich storehouse of data for an understanding and interpretation of political phenomena, a comparative study of political situations and verification of hypotheses about politics; and (b) when history gives us not only an account of events but also cause-and-effect relationships, or rather, laws of historical development (such as Marx's materialist interpretation of history), this knowledge can be used for understanding the course of politics in a given situation.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.141 (printed 126)

```text
   (a) HISTORY AS A STOREHOUSE OF DATA
       three distinct uses inside one limb:
         (i)   understanding and INTERPRETATION of
               political phenomena
         (ii)  COMPARATIVE STUDY of political situations
         (iii) VERIFICATION OF HYPOTHESES about politics

   (b) HISTORY AS A SOURCE OF LAWS
       not merely an account of events, but
         -- CAUSE-AND-EFFECT RELATIONSHIPS
         -- "or rather, LAWS OF HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT"
       named instance: MARX'S MATERIALIST INTERPRETATION
                       OF HISTORY
       used for: understanding THE COURSE OF POLITICS in a
                 given situation

   (a) treats history as EVIDENCE.
   (b) treats history as THEORY.
   These are different relationships, and only (b) is
   controversial.
```

*Caption: both canonical owners compress this to "past cases, sequences, roots and patterns", which merges the two limbs and loses all three uses inside limb (a). The lettered structure is restored here.*

### 3. Limb (a), use by use

| ✅ Use | ⚠️ What it does | ⚠️ What it needs from history |
|---|---|---|
| **Understanding and interpretation of political phenomena** | ⚠️ Supplies the antecedents that make a present arrangement intelligible | ⚠️ Narrative and context |
| **Comparative study of political situations** | ⚠️ Supplies cases that are not available in the present, extending the comparative range backwards | ⚠️ Documented instances with enough detail to compare |
| **Verification of hypotheses about politics** | ⚠️ Supplies the test — this is implication (b) of Lesson 4, applied to history | ⚠️ Cases that can confirm **and** disconfirm |

🔑 The third use is the most important and the one the owners lose entirely. ⚠️ It makes history a *tribunal* and not merely a *library*, and it is what Lesson 22's rule-and-exception requirement operationalises.

### 4. Limb (b), and why it is the risky one

⚠️ Limb (b) is a much stronger claim: that history can yield **laws of historical development**, which can then be used to understand the course of politics.

⚠️ Three observations:

- ✅ The chapter names an instance — **Marx's materialist interpretation of history** — and names no other. ⚠️ The example is offered as an illustration of the *type*, not as an endorsement of the content.
- ⚠️ The phrase **or rather** in the chapter's sentence signals an upgrade: from cause-and-effect relationships to laws. ⚠️ Those are not the same thing; a causal relation can be local and contingent, whereas a law claims generality.
- ➡️ The critical assessment of this move — **historicism**, Popper's objection that historical laws cannot be established, and the distinction between the two senses of the historical approach — is owned by **Topic 08**, which teaches it at length. ❌ It is not re-taught here. ⚠️ What this topic must record is that the chapter offers limb (b) without a warning attached at this point.

### 5. An example of each limb, and where each stops

⚠️ **Limb (a) example.** A claim that federal arrangements are durable where regional identities pre-date the federation can be tested against documented federations — their formation, their crises and their survivals or failures. ⚠️ History supplies both the confirming and the disconfirming cases.

⚠️ **Where it stops.** ⚠️ The historical record is not a controlled experiment: cases differ in a hundred respects at once, and the selection of cases is made by the researcher. ⚠️ Verification by history is therefore defeasible — which is precisely why the chapter later insists on testing **both in terms of rule and exception**.

⚠️ **Limb (b) example.** A law of development, if available, would allow the analyst to say not merely what has happened but what is likely to happen next in a given situation.

⚠️ **Where it stops.** ⚠️ The transition from established causal relations to laws is exactly where the objection bites, and ➡️ the chapter's own module places the critique elsewhere. ❌ An answer should not present limb (b) as uncontroversial merely because this chapter states it without qualification.

### 6. Comparison: history against the four aspects of Lesson 14

⚠️ Recall that the systems passage named four aspects to be understood — economic, cultural, psychological, sociological — and history was not among them.

| | ⚠️ The four aspects | ⚠️ History |
|---|---|---|
| What they describe | ⚠️ The present state of the social organization | ⚠️ The sequence by which that state arose |
| What they enable | ⚠️ Explanation of a current political process | ⚠️ Testing a political theory across time and comparison beyond the present |
| ⚠️ Relation | ⚠️ Without the four, a political process has no present context | ⚠️ Without history, a political claim has no test beyond the present |

🔑 This is the reconstruction offered at Lesson 14 and it earns its keep here: **history supplies time, philosophy supplies evaluation, and the four aspects supply the present.** ⚠️ It is a reading of the chapter's architecture, ❌ not a statement the chapter makes.

### 7. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** Limb (a)'s third use is incompatible with limb (b). If history can yield **laws of historical development**, then history is itself a theoretical discipline with its own commitments, and using it to **verify hypotheses about politics** becomes circular — the historian's theory is doing the verifying, and the test is only as good as a theory that political science did not examine. The chapter cannot have history as both neutral tribunal and theoretical rival.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's two limbs are stated as alternative contexts, not as simultaneous roles in a single operation. ⚠️ In limb (a) the historian's records function as evidence; in limb (b) the historian's generalisations function as theory. ⚠️ A political scientist who uses the first is not thereby committed to the second, and the chapter's **when** in limb (b) — *when history gives us not only an account of events but also cause-and-effect relationships* — marks it as conditional. ✅ The chapter also acknowledges the theoretical turn in historical writing itself, noting the **increasing realization of the importance of socio-economic factors** among modern historians, so the entanglement is recognised even if not resolved.

⚠️ **Residual.** The objection's core survives: there is no theory-free historical record, and the selection and description of past events is already shaped by historiographical commitments. ⚠️ The chapter does not address this, and ⚠️ an answer should state limb (a) as the chapter states it while adding the caution in its own voice.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** The lettered two-context structure is the correct opening for any stem on history and political science.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Examine the contribution of history to political analysis* — two contexts, three uses inside the first, the named instance in the second, then Lessons 21-22.
- ❌ **Trap.** Reducing history's contribution to "background". ⚠️ The chapter's strongest use is **verification of hypotheses**, which is a testing role.
- ➡️ **Boundary.** Historicism and Popper's critique belong to Topic 08; name the issue, do not expound it.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ History's use is considered in **two important contexts**, lettered (a) and (b). (PDF p.141)
2. ✅ Limb (a): history as a **rich storehouse of data**.
3. ✅ Three uses inside limb (a): **understanding and interpretation** of political phenomena; **comparative study of political situations**; **verification of hypotheses about politics**.
4. 🔑 Verification makes history a tribunal, not merely a library — and both owners lose it.
5. ✅ Limb (b): when history gives **not only an account of events but also cause-and-effect relationships, or rather, laws of historical development**.
6. ✅ Named instance: **Marx's materialist interpretation of history**.
7. ✅ Limb (b)'s use: **understanding the course of politics in a given situation**.
8. ⚠️ **Or rather** marks an upgrade from causal relations to laws; the two are not equivalent.
9. ⚠️ Limb (a) treats history as evidence; limb (b) treats it as theory.
10. ➡️ Historicism and Popper's objection belong to Topic 08 and are named, not expounded.
11. ⚠️ History supplies **time**; the four aspects of Lesson 14 supply the **present** — ⚠️ a reading, not the chapter's statement.
12. ⚠️ Verification by history is defeasible, since cases are selected and never controlled.
13. ⚠️ There is no theory-free historical record; state the caution in your own voice.

### Concept check

**Question:** Separate the three uses the chapter packs into limb (a), and explain why the third changes history's standing relative to political science.

**Model answer:** ✅ Limb (a) treats history as a **rich storehouse of data** for three purposes: **understanding and interpretation of political phenomena**; **a comparative study of political situations**; and **verification of hypotheses about politics**. ⚠️ The first supplies antecedents and the second extends the comparative range backwards, and both leave history in a supporting role. ⚠️ The third changes the standing, because a source used to verify can also falsify: history becomes a tribunal before which political claims are tested rather than a library from which they are illustrated. ✅ The chapter develops exactly this later, requiring that a political theory be **substantiated and verified by historical data, both in terms of rule and exception**, failing which it will **crumble**. ⚠️ The verification is nonetheless defeasible, since the cases are selected rather than controlled.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat limb (b) as the chapter's main claim about history. ⚠️ Limb (b) is conditional — it applies **when** history yields cause-and-effect relationships or laws — and its one named instance is offered as an illustration of a type, with the critical assessment of such claims belonging to the approaches topic.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the two contexts in which the chapter holds history to be useful for the study of politics. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ In the first, history is a **rich storehouse of data** serving three purposes: **understanding and interpretation of political phenomena**, **a comparative study of political situations**, and **verification of hypotheses about politics**. ⚠️ The third is the strongest, because a source that can verify can also refute, and it converts history from illustration into test. ✅ In the second, history supplies **not only an account of events but also cause-and-effect relationships, or rather, laws of historical development (such as Marx's materialist interpretation of history)**, which can be used for **understanding the course of politics in a given situation**. ⚠️ The second limb is conditional and far stronger: it treats history as theory rather than as evidence, and the claim that historical laws can be established is contested where the approaches to the study of politics are examined. 🔑 Evidence in the first, theory in the second.

**Rubric [10]:** limb (a) named with all three internal uses **3** · the verification point developed as a change of standing **2** · limb (b) with its cause-and-effect-to-laws upgrade **2** · the named instance **1** · the conditional *when* noticed **1** · a contested-status caution routed correctly **1**.

## Lesson 21 — The use of history II: Seeley, Freeman and the oversimplification

Progress: 21 / 31 | Stage: Core | Part VIII — the five uses expounded, discipline by discipline
