## Lesson 26 — The use of sociology I: the discipline, and the sociologists who turned to politics

Progress: 26 / 31 | Stage: Core | Part VIII — the five uses expounded, discipline by discipline

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §8's sociology row and §3's political-sociology definition, with the Advanced companion's §6's political-sociological perspective, read against the opening of the chapter's section headed "THE USE OF SOCIOLOGY" (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.143 = printed 128), which supplies the definition of sociology, its account of institutions, the near-universal scope claim, and the roster of early sociologists who turned to political analysis, with their dates.
CA search: "Weber Michels Pareto Durkheim Bentley Giddings voting behaviour 1920s sociology politics 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked was returned. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Sociology defined by a relation, not by a subject matter

> Sociology is chiefly concerned with the behaviour of men in an associative process.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.143 (printed 128)

⚠️ **The associative process** is the chapter's phrase and it is absent from both owners, which describe sociology by its topics — group life, institutions, status, participation. ⚠️ Defining it by a *process* rather than by a list is stronger: it explains why sociology's scope is so wide, and it links directly to the social-science definition of Lesson 1, whose third limb was **interpersonal relationships of individuals as members of society**.

### 2. Institutions, defined

> It inquires into how institutions, stabilized systems of expectations and actions, fulfil the varying needs of man in society, particularly in a complex society.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.143 (printed 128)

🔑 **Institutions = stabilized systems of expectations and actions.** ⚠️ This definition is absent from both owners and it is one of the most useful single formulations in the whole chapter, because it is usable far beyond this topic.

⚠️ Why it is useful:

| ⚠️ Element | ⚠️ What it explains |
|---|---|
| **Stabilized** | ⚠️ Why institutions persist and why they are costly to change |
| **Systems** | ⚠️ Why an institution cannot be altered one part at a time without unintended consequences |
| **Expectations** | ⚠️ Why institutions operate even where enforcement is absent — people act on what they expect others to do |
| **And actions** | ⚠️ Why an institution that nobody acts on ceases to exist, whatever the text says |

⚠️ Read against Lesson 12, this definition is the precise reason a formal-institutional account is insufficient: the written rule is a text, while the institution is the stabilized pattern of expectation and action. ⚠️ When they diverge, it is the second that is the institution.

✅ The chapter adds the scope consequence: sociology **includes within its domain almost every aspect of human behaviour**.

### 3. Why sociology arrived in politics before political science went to sociology

✅ The chapter's account is that the traffic began from the other side:

> The early sociologists, both in Europe and America, in fact, exhibited considerable interest in politics, as they could not ignore as important a set of institutions as the political ones or the formation of attitudes and group norms in politics.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.143 (printed 128)

⚠️ Two reasons are given, and they correspond to the two halves of sociology's own definition:

```text
   WHY EARLY SOCIOLOGISTS COULD NOT IGNORE POLITICS

   reason 1   political institutions are "as important a set
              of institutions" as any
              -> follows from sociology's interest in
                 STABILIZED SYSTEMS OF EXPECTATIONS AND
                 ACTIONS

   reason 2   the FORMATION OF ATTITUDES AND GROUP NORMS
              in politics
              -> follows from sociology's interest in the
                 ASSOCIATIVE PROCESS

   CONSEQUENCE: the relationship did not begin with political
   science borrowing. It began with sociologists arriving.
```

*Caption: this inverts the usual story. On the chapter's account, political sociology was built partly by sociologists moving into political territory, which is why its parentage is genuinely joint.*

### 4. The roster, with dates

✅ The chapter names six scholars, four in one sentence and two more individually. ⚠️ **None of the six appears in either canonical owner for this topic.**

| ✅ Name | ✅ Dates as printed | ✅ What the chapter says |
|---|---|---|
| **Max Weber** | 1864-1920 | ✅ Among **famous sociologists** who **turned to political analysis as a part of their sociological inquiry** |
| **Robert Michels** | 1876-1936 | ✅ Same |
| **Vilfredo Pareto** | 1848-1923 | ✅ Same |
| **Emile Durkheim** | 1858-1917 | ✅ Same |
| **Arthur Bentley** | 1870-1957 | ✅ **Trained as a sociologist in Europe**, **eventually turned out to be a major intellectual force in political science in America** |
| **Franklin Giddings** | 1855-1931 | ✅ **Another American sociologist of repute**, who **encouraged his students to undertake empirical studies of voting behaviour in the early 1920s** |

⚠️ Three observations worth making in an answer:

- ⚠️ **Pareto and Michels appear twice in this chapter** — once in Lesson 17 as founders of elite theory and again here as sociologists who turned to political analysis. ⚠️ The chapter does not remark on the repetition; noticing it shows that the elite-theory import and the sociological migration are the same movement seen twice.
- ⚠️ **Bentley is the clearest case of a person, not a theory, crossing a boundary.** ⚠️ Trained in one discipline in one continent, he became a force in another discipline on another continent.
- ✅ **Giddings supplies a date**: empirical studies of **voting behaviour in the early 1920s**. ⚠️ That is the earliest dated empirical political research in this chapter, and it was encouraged by a sociologist.

### 5. An example of the institutions definition at work

⚠️ **Example.** Consider the convention that a defeated incumbent leaves office. ⚠️ In many systems no provision compels it mechanically; what operates is a stabilized system of expectations — officials expect it, successors expect it, and each acts on the expectation that others will. ⚠️ The institution is the expectation-plus-action pattern, not the text.

⚠️ **Where the example stops.** ⚠️ It does not follow that texts are unimportant. ⚠️ Texts are among the devices that stabilise expectations, and a written rule can create an expectation where none existed. ⚠️ The chapter's definition locates the institution in the stabilized pattern; ❌ it does not say the pattern is independent of the rules that help produce it.

### 6. Comparison: three disciplines' definitions in the chapter

| ✅ Discipline | ✅ Chapter's defining formula | ⚠️ What the formula puts at the centre |
|---|---|---|
| **Economics** | ✅ Factors affecting human behaviour in **material production, distribution and exchange**; decisions about **investment or use** of capital, labour and goods | ⚠️ Allocation under scarcity |
| **Sociology** | ✅ Behaviour of men in an **associative process**; institutions as **stabilized systems of expectations and actions** | ⚠️ Association and stabilisation |
| **Psychology** | ✅ **Motives of human behaviour** and the **causes of variations in individual and group behaviour** | ⚠️ Motive and variation |

🔑 All three are defined by **behaviour**. ⚠️ That is not a coincidence: it is the shared inheritance of the empirical turn described in Lesson 10, and it is what makes the three mutually intelligible.

### 7. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** If sociology **includes within its domain almost every aspect of human behaviour**, then political science is a proper subset of sociology, and the chapter's defence of political science's identity collapses. A discipline whose entire subject matter falls inside another's domain is a branch, not a neighbour.

✅ **Reply.** Domain and discipline are not the same thing. ⚠️ Sociology's domain is wide because its organising question — how association produces stabilized patterns — can be asked of almost anything. ⚠️ Political science's organising question is different: how authoritative decisions are made, by whom, with what justification, and with what consequences for those bound by them. ✅ The chapter's test of identity throughout is **focus**, and the fact that two questions can be asked of the same material does not make one question a subset of the other. ⚠️ The chapter's conclusion is explicit that the point of interdisciplinarity is not to merge the social sciences into a single master science.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply repeats the chapter's standing move — identity by focus — and inherits its standing weakness, noted at Lessons 5, 8 and 18. ⚠️ Here there is an additional wrinkle: political sociology, as the next lesson shows, is a field defined precisely by asking sociology's question about politics' material, and the chapter calls it a joint enterprise thriving on contributions from **sociology as well as political science**. ⚠️ Joint fields blur the boundary the identity claim needs, and the chapter treats that as a gain rather than as a problem.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** The institutions definition is transferable to almost any institutional question in any topic.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Examine the contribution of sociology to political analysis* — the associative process, the institutions definition, the sociologists who arrived, then Lesson 27's political sociology.
- ❌ **Trap.** Describing sociology by a list of topics. ⚠️ The chapter defines it by a process, which is both more accurate and more usable.
- 🔑 **Answer use.** The Giddings date — empirical voting studies encouraged in the **early 1920s** — is a precise, verifiable anchor that very few candidates will have.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Sociology is **chiefly concerned with the behaviour of men in an associative process**. (PDF p.143)
2. ✅ It inquires how **institutions, stabilized systems of expectations and actions**, fulfil **the varying needs of man in society**, **particularly in a complex society**.
3. 🔑 **Stabilized systems of expectations and actions** — the chapter's definition of an institution, absent from both owners.
4. ⚠️ The definition explains why a formal-institutional account is insufficient: text and stabilized pattern can diverge.
5. ✅ Sociology **includes within its domain almost every aspect of human behaviour**.
6. ✅ Early sociologists **both in Europe and America** exhibited considerable interest in politics.
7. ✅ Two reasons: political institutions are **as important a set of institutions** as any; and the **formation of attitudes and group norms** in politics.
8. ✅ **Max Weber (1864-1920)**, **Robert Michels (1876-1936)**, **Vilfredo Pareto (1848-1923)** and **Emile Durkheim (1858-1917)** turned to political analysis **as a part of their sociological inquiry**.
9. ✅ **Arthur Bentley (1870-1957)**, **trained as a sociologist in Europe**, became **a major intellectual force in political science in America**.
10. ✅ **Franklin Giddings (1855-1931)** encouraged **empirical studies of voting behaviour in the early 1920s**.
11. ⚠️ None of the six appears in either canonical owner for this topic.
12. ⚠️ Pareto and Michels appear twice in the chapter — as elite theorists and as migrating sociologists.
13. ⚠️ The relationship began with sociologists arriving, ❌ not only with political scientists borrowing.
14. ⚠️ All three donor disciplines are defined by **behaviour** — the shared inheritance of the empirical turn.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is the chapter's definition of an institution — "stabilized systems of expectations and actions" — more useful to political analysis than a definition in terms of offices and rules?

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Because it locates the institution in a pattern rather than in a text, and it is the pattern that political analysis has to explain. ⚠️ **Stabilized** accounts for persistence and for the cost of change; **systems** explains why an institution resists piecemeal alteration; **expectations** explains why institutions operate even without active enforcement, since each participant acts on what others are expected to do; and **actions** explains why an institution that nobody enacts has ceased to exist whatever the rules provide. ✅ This is the precise reason the chapter holds that the real world of politics cannot be understood **merely in terms of formal institutions which may operate differently in actual practice** — when text and pattern diverge, the institution is the pattern. ⚠️ Texts remain among the devices that stabilise expectations; the definition does not make them irrelevant.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not infer from sociology's near-universal domain that political science is a branch of sociology. ⚠️ The chapter individuates disciplines by focus, not by the extent of the material each can address, and it refuses any merger into a single master science.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the chapter's account of how sociology and political science came into relation. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ⚠️ The chapter's account inverts the usual story: the traffic began with sociologists arriving rather than with political scientists borrowing. ✅ Sociology is **chiefly concerned with the behaviour of men in an associative process** and inquires how **institutions, stabilized systems of expectations and actions**, meet needs in a complex society — a domain covering **almost every aspect of human behaviour**. ✅ Early sociologists in **Europe and America** could not ignore **as important a set of institutions as the political ones** or the **formation of attitudes and group norms** in politics. ✅ **Max Weber (1864-1920)**, **Robert Michels (1876-1936)**, **Vilfredo Pareto (1848-1923)** and **Emile Durkheim (1858-1917)** turned to political analysis within their sociological inquiry; **Arthur Bentley (1870-1957)**, trained in Europe, became a force in American political science; and **Franklin Giddings (1855-1931)** encouraged **empirical studies of voting behaviour in the early 1920s**. 🔑 Joint parentage, not one-way borrowing.

**Rubric [10]:** the associative-process definition **1** · the institutions definition in full **2** · the two reasons sociologists could not ignore politics **2** · at least four named scholars with dates **3** · Bentley's or Giddings's specific case **1** · the inversion of the usual borrowing story **1**.

## Lesson 27 — The use of sociology II: political sociology and its agenda

Progress: 27 / 31 | Stage: Core | Part VIII — the five uses expounded, discipline by discipline

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3's political-sociology definition and §8's sociology row, with the Advanced companion's §6 and §8's political-sociology route, read against the remainder of the chapter's sociology section and the boxed definition that follows it (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF pp.143-144 = printed 128-129), which supply the emergence of political sociology, the transfer of bureaucratic analysis to four kinds of institution, the public-decision argument with its five-item inquiry, the two-way thriving claim and the box's full definition and roster.
CA search: "political sociology political socialization political culture consensus cleavage legitimacy leadership 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked was returned. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. How a joint field is born

> The application of various concepts and methods of sociology to the study of political behaviour and institutions has given rise to a new venture called 'political sociology'.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.143 (printed 128)

⚠️ Note the formula. ⚠️ A hyphenated field arises when **one discipline's concepts and methods** are applied to **another discipline's objects**. ⚠️ The same formula generated political economy in Lesson 25 and will generate political psychology in Lesson 28 — and in each case the chapter gives the field a boxed definition.

🔑 Four such fields are named across the chapter: **political economy, political sociology, political psychology, political philosophy.** ⚠️ Listing them together, with the formula that produces them, is a compact way to show the chapter's architecture.

### 2. The transfer that political sociology performs

✅ The chapter is specific about what gets transferred:

> In fact, the practitioners of political sociology not only seek to explain political behaviour but also apply the analyses of bureaucratic structures developed in the works of Max Weber and Robert Michels to a variety of institutional structures, such as governmental agencies, trade unions, political parties, and even economic institutions.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.143 (printed 128)

```text
   SOURCE OF THE ANALYSIS
   analyses of BUREAUCRATIC STRUCTURES developed in the
   works of MAX WEBER and ROBERT MICHELS
             |
             |  applied to
             v
   FOUR KINDS OF INSTITUTIONAL STRUCTURE
      1  GOVERNMENTAL AGENCIES
      2  TRADE UNIONS
      3  POLITICAL PARTIES
      4  "and even" ECONOMIC INSTITUTIONS

   The phrase "and even" marks the fourth as the surprising
   one -- an analysis developed for public administration
   being applied to firms.

   AND NOTE: items 2, 3 and 4 are NOT organs of the state.
   Political sociology's objects extend well beyond
   government.
```

*Caption: this passage is absent from both owners. Its value is that it shows precisely what "applying sociology to politics" consists of — a specific body of analysis, from two named authors, extended to four classes of organisation.*

⚠️ ➡️ Weber's bureaucracy and Michels's law of oligarchy are owned doctrinally by **Topic 15** and, for the Optional, by the socio-political owners. ❌ Neither is expounded here; the point is the transfer.

### 3. Why public decisions require sociology

✅ The chapter then gives a practical argument:

> The practice of politics involves taking public decisions. Any such decisions, to be effective, must take into account the social motivations of relevant groups. An inquiry into value-orientations, attitudes, habits, beliefs and prejudices of the people becomes indispensable to ascertain the possible response and chances of success of any social measure.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.143 (printed 128)

| ✅ Step | ⚠️ What it adds |
|---|---|
| Politics involves **taking public decisions** | ⚠️ The argument is about effectiveness, not about explanation |
| Decisions **to be effective** must take account of the **social motivations of relevant groups** | ⚠️ Effectiveness is the criterion, which makes this an argument about implementation |
| An inquiry into **value-orientations, attitudes, habits, beliefs and prejudices** is **indispensable** | 🔑 Five named objects of inquiry, ❌ absent from both owners |
| To ascertain the **possible response** and **chances of success** of any **social measure** | ⚠️ Two outputs: prediction of response, and prediction of success |

🔑 This is the chapter's clearest statement that sociology matters for **policy feasibility**, not only for explanation. ⚠️ It also anticipates the chapter's closing division of labour: empirical study **devises tools for achieving the goals of public policy**, while philosophy determines the goals.

### 4. The scope claim and the two-way conclusion

> In fact, the study of politics today extends its scope beyond formal procedures and institutions for which reliance on sociology becomes essential. In short, the discipline of political sociology thrives on the contributions from sociology as well as political science.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF pp.143-144 (printed 128-129)

⚠️ **Thrives on the contributions from sociology as well as political science** is the two-way claim again, and it is the chapter's own evidence for implication (c). ⚠️ Political sociology is not sociology colonising politics; it is a field with two parents.

### 5. The boxed definition, restored in full

> The branch of learning which examines political institutions, processes and ideologies in the light of corresponding social structures, processes and modes of thought. It also examines the impact of political domination and decisions on social life. It treats political system as a subsystem within social system and inquires into their interrelationship.
>
> In a broader sense, political sociology focuses on those concepts and issues which are the common concern of sociology and politics. Examples of such concepts and issues are: political socialization, political culture, political participation, social basis of political behaviour, political ideologies, political consensus and cleavage, power, authority and legitimacy, patterns of political leadership, etc.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.144 (printed 129), boxed definition "Political Sociology"

| ✅ Part of the box | ⚠️ Owner status |
|---|---|
| ✅ Examines **political institutions, processes and ideologies** in the light of **social structures, processes and modes of thought** | ✅ Recorded by the basic owner |
| ✅ **It also examines the impact of political domination and decisions on social life** | ❌ Absent from both owners — ⚠️ and it is the reverse-direction limb |
| ✅ **It treats political system as a subsystem within social system and inquires into their interrelationship** | ❌ Absent — ⚠️ and it links the box directly to Lesson 14 |
| ✅ The nine-item roster of common concepts and issues | ❌ Absent |

```text
   POLITICAL SOCIOLOGY -- the nine named concepts and issues

      1  political socialization
      2  political culture
      3  political participation
      4  social basis of political behaviour
      5  political ideologies
      6  political consensus and cleavage
      7  power
      8  authority
      9  legitimacy
     10  patterns of political leadership
                                        ... "etc."

   (The chapter's list runs 7-9 together as a single
   compound entry -- "power, authority and legitimacy" --
   so it is read here as a ten-item list containing one
   compound of three.)
```

*Caption: this roster is effectively a syllabus for half of this subject's later topics. Items 7-9 are owned by the power topic, item 5 by the ideology topic, and items 1-4 and 6 by political sociology proper.*

⚠️ ➡️ **Routing.** Power, authority and legitimacy belong to **Topic 15**; political ideologies to **Topic 02**; and the broader state-society relation to **Topic 10**. ❌ This topic owns only the fact that the chapter lists them as the common concern of the two disciplines.

### 6. Comparison: the two boxed fields so far

| | ✅ Political economy (Lesson 25) | ✅ Political sociology (this lesson) |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Traditional form given? | ✅ Yes — **revenue and expenditure of the state** | ❌ No traditional form given |
| ✅ Defining test or relation | ✅ **Economically efficient** and **politically acceptable** | ✅ Political phenomena read **in the light of** social structures, processes and modes of thought |
| ✅ Reverse-direction limb | ✅ **Impact of political decisions on economic life** | ✅ **Impact of political domination and decisions on social life** |
| ✅ Model/technique noted | ✅ **Rational choice theory** | ✅ Weber's and Michels's analyses of bureaucratic structures |
| ✅ Variant named | ✅ **Marxist political economy** | ❌ None named |

🔑 Every one of these boxed fields contains an explicit reverse-direction limb. ⚠️ That is the chapter's most consistent structural signal: each hyphenated field is defined as two-way, which is why the conclusion can demand a **suitable contribution** back.

### 7. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** Political sociology, so defined, leaves political science with nothing of its own. Its roster includes power, authority, legitimacy, ideology, participation and leadership — the core vocabulary of political theory — and claims them as the **common concern of sociology and politics**. If the central concepts are shared, the discipline is not merely borrowing evidence; it is sharing its subject matter.

✅ **Reply.** Sharing a concept is not sharing a question. ⚠️ Sociology asks what social structures sustain a given distribution of authority; political theory asks whether that authority is legitimate, by what standard, and what obligations follow. ✅ The chapter's own closing division makes this explicit: scientific and behavioural studies **find facts and devise tools for achieving the goals of public policy**, whereas **these goals are largely determined with the help of political philosophy**. ⚠️ The normative half of the vocabulary is not on political sociology's roster at all.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply draws the line at the normative boundary, which means the empirical study of power, authority and legitimacy really is shared territory, and the chapter says so. ⚠️ Whether a discipline whose empirical core is shared and whose distinctiveness is normative is thereby safe is a question the chapter does not ask. ⚠️ Lesson 30's conclusion gives the chapter's answer — integration without merger — and ⚠️ Lesson 31 records what that answer leaves unsettled.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** The box's nine-item roster is a ready-made list of what political sociology covers, and the bureaucratic-transfer sentence is the best concrete instance of sociology applied to politics.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *What is political sociology and what does it contribute?* — the generating formula, the transfer, the policy-feasibility argument, the box.
- ❌ **Trap.** Reproducing only the first sentence of the box. ⚠️ The reverse-direction limb and the subsystem sentence are both inside it and both are absent from the owners.
- ⚠️ **Answer use.** The five-item inquiry — **value-orientations, attitudes, habits, beliefs and prejudices** — is the sharpest available answer to any stem about why policy fails in implementation.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Applying sociology's **concepts and methods** to political behaviour and institutions produced **'political sociology'**. (PDF p.143)
2. 🔑 The same formula produces political economy, political psychology and political philosophy — four boxed fields in one chapter.
3. ✅ Practitioners apply the **analyses of bureaucratic structures developed in the works of Max Weber and Robert Michels**.
4. ✅ To four kinds of structure: **governmental agencies, trade unions, political parties, and even economic institutions**.
5. ⚠️ Three of those four are not organs of the state.
6. ✅ Public decisions, **to be effective**, must take account of the **social motivations of relevant groups**.
7. ✅ The five-item inquiry: **value-orientations, attitudes, habits, beliefs and prejudices**.
8. ✅ Its purpose: to ascertain the **possible response** and **chances of success** of any **social measure**.
9. ✅ The study of politics **extends its scope beyond formal procedures and institutions**, making reliance on sociology **essential**.
10. ✅ Political sociology **thrives on the contributions from sociology as well as political science** — the two-way claim.
11. ✅ Box: examines political institutions, processes and ideologies **in the light of** corresponding social structures, processes and modes of thought.
12. ✅ Box, reverse limb: **it also examines the impact of political domination and decisions on social life**.
13. ✅ Box: **treats political system as a subsystem within social system and inquires into their interrelationship**.
14. ✅ Box roster: political socialization; political culture; political participation; social basis of political behaviour; political ideologies; political consensus and cleavage; power, authority and legitimacy; patterns of political leadership.
15. ➡️ Power, authority and legitimacy are owned by Topic 15; ideologies by Topic 02.

### Concept check

**Question:** The boxed definition of political sociology contains a limb that reverses the direction of explanation. Identify it, and explain what the chapter would lose if it were dropped.

**Model answer:** ✅ The limb is **it also examines the impact of political domination and decisions on social life**. ⚠️ The box's first sentence runs from society to politics — political institutions, processes and ideologies examined **in the light of** social structures, processes and modes of thought — and the second sentence runs the other way. ⚠️ Dropping it would reduce political sociology to the sociological explanation of politics, which would make political science a recipient rather than a partner. ✅ The chapter would then lose its own conclusion that political sociology **thrives on the contributions from sociology as well as political science**, and more seriously it would lose implication (c), under which political science develops a broader perspective **with a view to making contribution to the other social sciences**. 🔑 Every hyphenated field the chapter defines contains such a reverse limb, and that consistency is what licenses the conclusion's demand for a **suitable contribution**.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat political sociology as a sub-branch of sociology. ⚠️ The chapter calls it a field with two parents, and its boxed definition includes both directions of influence and the subsystem relation that Lesson 14 derived from Easton's borrowed concept.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Analyse what political sociology contributes to the practice as well as to the study of politics. *(Analyse — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ To study, it supplies a method and a roster. ✅ Practitioners apply the **analyses of bureaucratic structures developed in the works of Max Weber and Robert Michels** to **governmental agencies, trade unions, political parties, and even economic institutions**, and the field's common concerns include **political socialization, political culture, political participation, social basis of political behaviour, political ideologies, political consensus and cleavage, power, authority and legitimacy**, and **patterns of political leadership**. ✅ To practice, it supplies feasibility: public decisions, **to be effective, must take into account the social motivations of relevant groups**, and an inquiry into **value-orientations, attitudes, habits, beliefs and prejudices** is **indispensable to ascertain the possible response and chances of success of any social measure**. ⚠️ The field runs both ways, examining also **the impact of political domination and decisions on social life**. 🔑 Explanation for the student, feasibility for the policy-maker.

**Rubric [10]:** study and practice separated as distinct contributions **1** · the bureaucratic transfer with both named authors and at least three target structures **2** · the effectiveness argument with the five-item inquiry **3** · at least five items from the box's roster **2** · the reverse-direction limb **1** · a closing that separates study from practice **1**.

## Lesson 28 — The use of psychology: from attitudes to propaganda

Progress: 28 / 31 | Stage: Core | Part VIII — the five uses expounded, discipline by discipline

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3's political-psychology definition, §7's fourth bullet and §8's psychology row, with the Advanced companion's §6's political-psychological perspective and §8's political-psychology route, read against the chapter's section headed "THE USE OF PSYCHOLOGY" and the boxed definition that follows it (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF pp.144-145 = printed 129-130), which supply the definition of psychology, the derivation of social psychology, the authoritarian-personality episode with its dating, Lasswell with his dates, the four-item roster of political interests and the box's scope and influencing clause.
CA search: "authoritarian personality propaganda charismatic leadership mass communication political culture psychology 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked was returned. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Psychology, defined by two things

> Psychology is primarily interested in learning the motives of human behaviour and the causes of variations in individual and group behaviour.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.144 (printed 129)

✅ Two objects: **motives**, and **the causes of variations**. ⚠️ The second is the one that matters for political analysis, because politics is full of variation that institutional accounts cannot explain — why two citizens in identical circumstances behave differently, why the same appeal succeeds in one place and fails in another.

### 2. The derivation of social psychology

✅ The chapter then derives the branch it actually needs:

> Some human actions are, of course, the product of man's mental make-up as such. But some of his actions are also shaped by the pattern of man's participation in varying social situations. One of the key sources of personality formation and of individual behaviour is seen in the pattern of his interaction with other individuals in a given social setting. Psychology, therefore, also inquires into the effects of different group involvements on individual behaviour. This has given rise to a special branch of psychology, namely social psychology.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.144 (printed 129)

```text
   SOME ACTIONS      <- man's MENTAL MAKE-UP as such
                        (psychology proper -- the clinical
                         side of Lesson 2's split)

   SOME ACTIONS      <- the pattern of PARTICIPATION IN
                        VARYING SOCIAL SITUATIONS

   PERSONALITY FORMATION itself <- the pattern of
                        INTERACTION WITH OTHER INDIVIDUALS
                        in a given SOCIAL SETTING

                             |
                             v
   psychology therefore inquires into the effects of
   DIFFERENT GROUP INVOLVEMENTS on individual behaviour
                             |
                             v
            >>> SOCIAL PSYCHOLOGY <<<

   This is Lesson 2's taxonomy being DERIVED rather than
   asserted. The social side of psychology exists because
   personality is itself partly social in origin.
```

*Caption: the derivation is absent from both owners. It matters because it shows the chapter's taxonomy is argued rather than stipulated — social psychology is a branch because personality formation has a social source.*

### 3. The authoritarian personality, with its trigger and its method

> An inquiry into the formation of attitudes has inspired psychologists to examine political values and behaviour. The growth of totalitarian political movements in the thirties and forties of the twentieth century aroused interest in the study of the 'authoritarian personality', which involved application of insights gained from psycho-analytic theory and social psychological methods to understanding extremist political appeals.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.144 (printed 129)

| ✅ Element | ⚠️ Owner status |
|---|---|
| ✅ Inquiry into the **formation of attitudes** inspired the examination of political values and behaviour | ⚠️ Partly recorded |
| ✅ The trigger: **growth of totalitarian political movements in the thirties and forties of the twentieth century** | ❌ Absent — ⚠️ a dated historical cause |
| ✅ The object: the **'authoritarian personality'** | ✅ Recorded by the advanced owner as a use |
| ✅ The method: **psycho-analytic theory** and **social psychological methods** | ❌ Absent — ⚠️ two distinct methodological sources |
| ✅ The target: understanding **extremist political appeals** | ⚠️ Partly recorded |

🔑 The dating matters. ⚠️ The chapter is recording that a political event produced a psychological research programme, which is implication (c) running the other way: politics set psychology's agenda. ⚠️ That is a point few candidates will make and it is directly supported by the sentence.

✅ The chapter then names the scholar:

> Similar orientations influenced the work of Harold Lasswell (1902-78) and his disciples in their study of the influence of psychological factors on the appeal of extremist movements particularly, and on political behaviour generally.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.144 (printed 129)

⚠️ **Lasswell 1902-78** — the dates are absent from both owners. ⚠️ Note also that Lasswell now appears twice in the chapter: once in Lesson 19 for the problem-solving model derived from psychology, and here for the study of extremist appeal. ⚠️ He is the chapter's single most recurrent contemporary figure.

### 4. The four political interests psychology serves

> Besides, the study of politics involves an interest in the process of the formation and expression of public opinion, patterns of leadership including charismatic leadership, impact of propaganda and the role of mass communication in inculcating political culture.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.144 (printed 129)

```text
   1  the FORMATION AND EXPRESSION of PUBLIC OPINION
      -- note both verbs: how opinion forms, and how it
         gets expressed

   2  PATTERNS OF LEADERSHIP, including CHARISMATIC
      LEADERSHIP

   3  the IMPACT OF PROPAGANDA

   4  the role of MASS COMMUNICATION in INCULCATING
      POLITICAL CULTURE
      -- "inculcating" is the chapter's verb: culture is
         not merely transmitted but instilled

   "Social psychology proves particularly helpful in
    understanding these important aspects of the political
    process."
```

*Caption: item 4 connects directly to political sociology's roster, where political culture and political socialization appear. The disciplines overlap at exactly the point the chapter said they would.*

⚠️ ➡️ Charismatic leadership as a **type of authority** belongs to Topic 15. ❌ Here it is named only as an object that political psychology helps explain.

### 5. The boxed definition, with the clause that should give a reader pause

> A branch of learning which uses the models and techniques of psychology for analysing psychological aspects of political behaviour. It may also be used for influencing political behaviour of a group. It may be regarded as a branch of social psychology which focuses on political aspect of social life. It may include such problems within its scope: relation between individual's personality and his political behaviour; sources of similarities and dissimilarities between political attitudes; psychological bases of political learning, political socialization and leadership styles; techniques and impact of political propaganda, etc.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.145 (printed 130), boxed definition "Political Psychology"

⚠️ **It may also be used for influencing political behaviour of a group.** ❌ This clause is absent from both canonical owners, and it is the most ethically loaded sentence in the chapter.

⚠️ Why it matters:

- ⚠️ Every other hyphenated field in the chapter is defined by what it **explains**. ⚠️ This one is defined by what it explains **and** by what it can be used to **do**.
- ⚠️ The box's own scope list ends with **techniques and impact of political propaganda** — ⚠️ note **techniques**, which is knowledge of how to do it, not only of what it does.
- ⚠️ The chapter offers no evaluation of this capacity. ❌ It neither warns against it nor endorses it. ⚠️ An answer may legitimately observe the silence; ❌ it must not attribute a position to the chapter that the chapter does not take.

✅ The box's four-item scope:

| ✅ Scope item |
|---|
| ✅ Relation between an **individual's personality** and his **political behaviour** |
| ✅ Sources of **similarities and dissimilarities between political attitudes** |
| ✅ **Psychological bases of political learning, political socialization and leadership styles** |
| ✅ **Techniques and impact of political propaganda** |

✅ And the placement: political psychology **may be regarded as a branch of social psychology which focuses on political aspect of social life** — ⚠️ which is Lesson 2's taxonomy applied once more, and which the basic owner's definition omits.

### 6. Comparison: the three hyphenated fields so far

| | ✅ Political economy | ✅ Political sociology | ✅ Political psychology |
|---|---|---|---|
| ✅ Parent branch named | ⚠️ Economics | ⚠️ Sociology | ✅ **A branch of social psychology** |
| ✅ Reverse-direction limb | ✅ Impact of political decisions on economic life | ✅ Impact of political domination and decisions on social life | ⚠️ Implicit — ✅ politics set psychology's agenda in the 1930s-40s |
| ✅ Applied capacity noted | ⚠️ Policy design | ⚠️ Feasibility of a social measure | ❌ **Influencing political behaviour of a group** |
| ✅ Technique named | ✅ Rational choice theory | ✅ Weber's and Michels's bureaucratic analyses | ✅ Psycho-analytic theory; social psychological methods |

🔑 The third row is the sharpest contrast in the chapter: only political psychology is defined as a field that can be used on people rather than only about them.

### 7. An ethical objection the chapter does not answer

⚠️ **Objection.** A discipline defined partly by its capacity to influence the behaviour of groups cannot be a neutral source of evidence for political analysis. If political psychology supplies both the explanation of propaganda's impact and the **techniques** of propaganda, then borrowing from it imports an instrument of manipulation into a discipline whose conclusion claims that goals are determined by **political philosophy**. The chapter notes the capacity and says nothing about it.

✅ **Reply.** Three things can be said from within the chapter. ⚠️ First, the capacity is an accurate description rather than a recommendation: knowledge of attitude formation **is** usable in both directions, and concealing that would be worse than recording it. ⚠️ Second, the purposefulness lock applies — material is admitted into political analysis for the **definite purpose of understanding the problems of politics**, which is an explanatory purpose. ✅ Third, the chapter's final division of labour is precisely the safeguard the objection asks for: empirical studies **find facts and devise tools**, while the **goals** are **largely determined with the help of political philosophy**. ⚠️ A technique is a tool, and tools do not select their own ends.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply is the best available but it is assembled by the reader; ⚠️ the chapter does not connect the influencing clause to its own closing division of labour, and it offers no ethical treatment of the point anywhere. ⚠️ ❌ An answer should record the silence honestly rather than supplying the chapter with a position it never takes.

### 8. Taking psychology into the answer

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** The authoritarian-personality episode with its dating and the box's four-item scope are the densest material here.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Examine the contribution of psychology to political analysis* — definition, derivation of social psychology, the 1930s-40s episode, the four interests, the box.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating political psychology as a branch of political science. ⚠️ The chapter calls it **a branch of social psychology**.
- 🔑 **Answer use.** The observation that a political event — the growth of totalitarian movements — set a psychological research agenda is an unusually good illustration of two-way traffic.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Psychology is primarily interested in the **motives of human behaviour** and the **causes of variations in individual and group behaviour**. (PDF p.144)
2. ✅ Some actions come from **man's mental make-up as such**; others are shaped by **participation in varying social situations**.
3. ✅ **Personality formation** itself has a key source in the **pattern of interaction with other individuals in a given social setting**.
4. ✅ Hence psychology inquires into **the effects of different group involvements on individual behaviour**, giving rise to **social psychology**.
5. ⚠️ The derivation is absent from both owners and shows Lesson 2's taxonomy being argued rather than asserted.
6. ✅ Inquiry into the **formation of attitudes** led psychologists to examine political values and behaviour.
7. ✅ The **growth of totalitarian political movements in the thirties and forties of the twentieth century** aroused interest in the **'authoritarian personality'**.
8. ✅ Its method: **psycho-analytic theory** and **social psychological methods**, applied to **extremist political appeals**.
9. ✅ **Harold Lasswell (1902-78)** and his disciples studied psychological factors in the appeal of extremist movements and in political behaviour generally.
10. ⚠️ Lasswell appears twice in the chapter — the problem-solving model and this.
11. ✅ Four political interests psychology serves: **formation and expression of public opinion**; **patterns of leadership including charismatic leadership**; **impact of propaganda**; **the role of mass communication in inculcating political culture**.
12. ✅ Box: political psychology uses the **models and techniques** of psychology for analysing psychological aspects of political behaviour.
13. ❌ Box: **It may also be used for influencing political behaviour of a group** — ❌ absent from both owners; ⚠️ the chapter offers no evaluation.
14. ✅ Box: it **may be regarded as a branch of social psychology**.
15. ✅ Box scope: personality and political behaviour; similarities and dissimilarities between political attitudes; psychological bases of political learning, socialization and leadership styles; **techniques and impact of political propaganda**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The boxed definition says political psychology "may also be used for influencing political behaviour of a group". Why is that clause unlike anything in the chapter's other field definitions, and what is the most defensible way to handle it in an answer?

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Because every other hyphenated field in the chapter is defined by what it explains, whereas this one is defined by what it explains **and** by what it can be used to do. ⚠️ The box reinforces this by listing **techniques and impact of political propaganda** within its scope — technique being knowledge of how to produce an effect, not only of what effects occur. ⚠️ The defensible handling is threefold: report the clause accurately, since suppressing it would misrepresent the source; note that the chapter offers **no** evaluation of the capacity, neither warning nor endorsement; and connect it, in one's own voice, to the chapter's closing division of labour, under which empirical studies **find facts and devise tools for achieving the goals of public policy** while **these goals are largely determined with the help of political philosophy**. ❌ Do not attribute that connection to the chapter, which never makes it.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not classify political psychology as a branch of political science. ⚠️ The box places it as **a branch of social psychology which focuses on political aspect of social life**, which is Lesson 2's taxonomy operating once more and which the basic owner's definition leaves out.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the contribution of psychology to political analysis, and identify what is distinctive about the field it has generated. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Psychology studies the **motives of human behaviour** and the **causes of variations in individual and group behaviour**, and because personality formation depends on **interaction with other individuals in a given social setting**, it generates **social psychology**. ✅ Politically, it supplied the study of the **'authoritarian personality'**, prompted by the **growth of totalitarian political movements in the thirties and forties of the twentieth century** and pursued through **psycho-analytic theory and social psychological methods**, notably by **Harold Lasswell (1902-78)**. ✅ It serves four political interests: **formation and expression of public opinion**; **patterns of leadership including charismatic leadership**; the **impact of propaganda**; and the role of **mass communication in inculcating political culture**. ⚠️ Distinctive is the resulting field: political psychology is **a branch of social psychology**, and uniquely among the chapter's fields **it may also be used for influencing political behaviour of a group**. ⚠️ The chapter evaluates that capacity nowhere.

**Rubric [10]:** the two-object definition of psychology **1** · the derivation of social psychology **2** · the authoritarian-personality episode with its dating and method **3** · at least three of the four political interests **2** · the influencing clause identified as distinctive **1** · the chapter's silence recorded honestly **1**.

## Lesson 29 — The use of philosophy: the transcendental method, Comte, and the revival

Progress: 29 / 31 | Stage: Advanced | Part VIII — the five uses expounded, discipline by discipline

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §3's political-philosophy definition, §5's third distinction and §9's second trap, with the Advanced companion's §4's political-science/political-philosophy row, §6 and §7's second bullet, read against the chapter's section headed "THE USE OF PHILOSOPHY" and the boxed definition that follows it (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF pp.145-146 = printed 130-131), which supply philosophy's agenda, the empirical/transcendental contrast, Comte with his dates and positive philosophy, the dating of behaviouralism and post-behaviouralism, the facts-and-tools division and the box's full content.
CA search: "political philosophy revival theory of justice liberty equality community post-behaviouralism 1970s 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nil — the revival dated here belongs to the early 1970s, and ❌ no honest present-day anchor exists for it.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The section that closes the loop

⚠️ The chapter opened by saying that the traditional study of politics was **characterized by its close link with philosophy** and that other social sciences **spun off** from philosophy. ⚠️ It now returns to philosophy as the last of the five uses, and the return is not nostalgic: it is the chapter's answer to the question of what the empirical turn cannot do.

### 2. Philosophy's agenda, in six items

> Philosophy is primarily concerned with an inquiry into the essence of reality, limits of knowledge, nature of truth, relation between man and universe, the purpose of universe, the foundations of morality and beauty, and so on.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.145 (printed 130)

```text
   1  the ESSENCE OF REALITY              -- metaphysics
   2  the LIMITS OF KNOWLEDGE             -- epistemology
   3  the NATURE OF TRUTH                 -- epistemology
   4  the RELATION BETWEEN MAN AND        -- metaphysics
      UNIVERSE
   5  the PURPOSE OF UNIVERSE             -- metaphysics /
                                             teleology
   6  the FOUNDATIONS OF MORALITY AND     -- ethics and
      BEAUTY                                 aesthetics
                                 ... "and so on"
```

*Caption: the six-item agenda is absent from both owners. Reproducing it shows why philosophy's contribution is of a different kind from the other four disciplines': it is the only donor whose questions are not about observable states of affairs.*

### 3. What philosophy does with empirical results

> Philosophy makes use of the conclusions of empirical data, but it focuses on clarification of the conceptual problems which may lie beyond the limits of empirical knowledge in all branches of learning. It is an attempt at investigation of the substantive aspects of nature, man, society and politics. When it touches the problems of politics, it is described as political philosophy.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.145 (printed 130)

⚠️ Three claims, and the first is often missed: ✅ philosophy **makes use of the conclusions of empirical data**. ⚠️ The chapter does not set philosophy against empirical inquiry; it has philosophy taking empirical conclusions as input and then doing something else with them — namely **clarification of the conceptual problems** that lie **beyond the limits of empirical knowledge**.

### 4. The distinction, and the chapter's refusal of it

✅ The chapter first states the usual contrast in full:

> Philosophy is sometimes distinguished from science on the ground that science exclusively relies on empirical method, i.e. the method based on sense-experience whereas philosophy uses transcendental method, i.e. the method based on speculation of what is beyond sense-experience; science is descriptive whereas philosophy is critical and evaluative.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.145 (printed 130)

| | ✅ Science | ✅ Philosophy |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Method | **Empirical method** — based on **sense-experience** | **Transcendental method** — based on **speculation of what is beyond sense-experience** |
| ✅ Character | **Descriptive** | **Critical and evaluative** |

⚠️ **Transcendental method** appears nowhere in either canonical owner, and it is the technical term in this section most likely to be rewarded in a Mains answer.

✅ And then the chapter declines to accept the contrast as decisive:

> However, it should not be forgotten that science itself is the product of philosophy. Science not only owes its origin to philosophy, it derives its validity from philosophy itself.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.145 (printed 130)

🔑 Two distinct claims, and the second is much stronger than the first:

```text
   ORIGIN   science owes its origin to philosophy
            -- a HISTORICAL claim, already supported by the
               spin-off thesis of Lesson 10

   VALIDITY science derives its VALIDITY from philosophy
            itself
            -- a LOGICAL claim: the grounds on which
               scientific method is justified are not
               themselves scientific findings

   The second claim is why the chapter can refuse to let the
   descriptive/evaluative split become a hierarchy.
```

*Caption: the origin claim is history; the validity claim is philosophy of science. An answer that separates them is making a distinction the chapter runs together in one sentence.*

### 5. Comte

> Auguste Comte (1798-1857), a French philosopher, traced the foundation of science to 'positive philosophy'. Comte recognized sociology as the 'mother social science' which was based on the development of 'positive philosophy'.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.145 (printed 130)

⚠️ Four restorations here: ✅ the dates **1798-1857**, ✅ **a French philosopher**, ✅ **'positive philosophy'**, and ✅ sociology as the **'mother social science'**. ⚠️ The owners name Comte and record the general point, but none of these four.

⚠️ The sociology claim repays attention. ⚠️ Earlier the chapter described philosophy as the **parent discipline** from which the social sciences spun off; here Comte is reported as treating sociology as the **mother social science**. ⚠️ These are claims about two different levels — philosophy as parent of all learning, sociology as mother among the social sciences — and the chapter does not reconcile them. ❌ Do not present them as a single hierarchy.

### 6. The fashion, the eclipse and the revival — with dates

> With the growing craze for the use of scientific method in the study of politics in modern times, it became fashionable to distinguish 'political science' from 'political philosophy'. Rise of behaviouralism in 1950s and 1960s was particularly responsible for this attitude. However, since early 1970s with the advent of 'post-behaviouralism' and the revival of interest in theory of justice and other related principles (liberty, equality, community, etc.), political philosophy received a renewed prominence.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.145 (printed 130)

```text
   PHASE 1   "growing craze" for scientific method
             -> it became FASHIONABLE to distinguish
                'political science' from 'political
                philosophy'

   PHASE 2   BEHAVIOURALISM, 1950s and 1960s
             -> "particularly responsible for this attitude"

   PHASE 3   since the EARLY 1970s
             -> POST-BEHAVIOURALISM
             -> revival of interest in the THEORY OF JUSTICE
                and related principles:
                   LIBERTY · EQUALITY · COMMUNITY
             -> political philosophy received a RENEWED
                PROMINENCE
```

*Caption: the two dates — 1950s-60s and early 1970s — are absent from both owners, as is the explicit four-item list of revived principles. Note also the chapter's dismissive vocabulary for phase 1: a "craze", and a "fashionable" distinction.*

⚠️ ➡️ Behaviouralism and post-behaviouralism as doctrines belong to **Topic 08**. ❌ They are dated here, not expounded.

### 7. The division of labour the chapter settles on

> Now scientific and behavioural studies of politics are undertaken to find facts and devise tools for achieving the goals of public policy whereas these goals are largely determined with the help of political philosophy.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF pp.145-146 (printed 130-131)

🔑 This is the chapter's settlement, and it is the single most useful sentence in the chapter for a Mains conclusion.

| ✅ Empirical and behavioural study | ✅ Political philosophy |
|---|---|
| ✅ **Find facts** | ✅ **Determine the goals** of public policy |
| ✅ **Devise tools** for achieving the goals | ⚠️ — |
| ⚠️ Means | ⚠️ Ends |

⚠️ Note the hedge: goals are **largely** determined with the help of political philosophy. ⚠️ Not wholly, and **with the help of** rather than *by*. ⚠️ The chapter is careful not to hand ends over to philosophers as a monopoly.

### 8. The boxed definition of political philosophy

> A branch of learning which is primarily concerned with moral and substantive dimensions of politics. Its central problems include the pursuit of good life, questions pertaining to norms and values, good and evil, virtue and vice, means and ends, right and wrong, and visions of an ideal state and society. Political philosophy inquires into the foundations of political life, grounds of political obligation, and moral worthiness of different political systems. It makes use of philosophical and historical method in determining the goals of public life. A major part of political philosophy coincides with moral and social philosophy.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.146 (printed 131), boxed definition "Political Philosophy"

| ✅ Part | ⚠️ Owner status |
|---|---|
| ✅ Primarily concerned with **moral and substantive dimensions of politics** | ✅ Recorded |
| ✅ Central problems: **pursuit of good life**; **norms and values**; **good and evil**; **virtue and vice**; **means and ends**; **right and wrong**; **visions of an ideal state and society** | ❌ The five value-pairs are absent |
| ✅ Inquires into **foundations of political life**, **grounds of political obligation**, **moral worthiness of different political systems** | ✅ Recorded |
| ✅ **It makes use of philosophical and historical method in determining the goals of public life** | ❌ Absent — ⚠️ note **historical**, which ties this box back to the history section |
| ✅ **A major part of political philosophy coincides with moral and social philosophy** | ❌ Absent |

🔑 The method clause is the quiet surprise. ⚠️ Political philosophy, on this definition, does not operate by pure reflection: it uses a **historical** method alongside a philosophical one, which is consistent with the chapter's earlier insistence that political theory be verified against historical data.

### 9. Does the division of labour survive its own evidence?

⚠️ **Objection.** The facts-and-tools division of labour is untenable and the chapter half-knows it. Choosing which facts to find is itself a value-laden decision; the design of a tool embodies assumptions about acceptable costs and about whose interests count; and the chapter's own history section demands that political theory be verified against evidence, which makes the determination of goals partly empirical. The neat division collapses from both directions.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's formulation anticipates both pressures. ⚠️ It says goals are **largely** determined **with the help of** political philosophy — a formulation that concedes both that the determination is not exclusively philosophical and that philosophy assists rather than dictates. ✅ And the box has political philosophy using **philosophical and historical method**, so the chapter has already denied that goal-determination is purely a priori. ⚠️ What the division does claim is narrower and defensible: that the question *what should be achieved* is not answered by the methods that answer *what is the case*.

⚠️ **Residual.** The narrow claim survives, but the chapter does not address the reverse pressure — that the selection of facts and the design of tools carry evaluative commitments. ⚠️ ➡️ That is exactly the post-behavioural objection, which **Topic 08** owns. ⚠️ ❌ Do not import its full treatment; ⚠️ note that this chapter's settlement is stated without engaging it.

### 10. Answer-booklet use

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** The facts-and-tools sentence is the best available conclusion for any answer on the relation between empirical and normative political study.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Did the scientific study of politics make political philosophy redundant?* — the fashion, the 1950s-60s eclipse, the early-1970s revival, the settlement.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that behaviouralism "destroyed" political philosophy. ⚠️ The chapter's word is that it became **fashionable** to distinguish the two, and that the attitude was a **craze**.
- 🔑 **Answer use.** **Transcendental method** and **positive philosophy** are two precise technical terms available here and almost never used by candidates.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Philosophy's agenda: **essence of reality**, **limits of knowledge**, **nature of truth**, **relation between man and universe**, **purpose of universe**, **foundations of morality and beauty**. (PDF p.145)
2. ✅ Philosophy **makes use of the conclusions of empirical data** but focuses on **clarification of the conceptual problems** beyond the limits of empirical knowledge.
3. ✅ When it touches the problems of politics it is **political philosophy**.
4. ✅ The usual contrast: science uses the **empirical method** based on **sense-experience**; philosophy uses the **transcendental method**, based on **speculation of what is beyond sense-experience**.
5. ✅ Science is **descriptive**; philosophy is **critical and evaluative**.
6. ⚠️ **Transcendental method** appears in neither owner.
7. ✅ **Science itself is the product of philosophy** — it owes its **origin** to philosophy and derives its **validity** from philosophy.
8. 🔑 Origin is a historical claim; validity is a logical one.
9. ✅ **Auguste Comte (1798-1857)**, **a French philosopher**, traced the foundation of science to **'positive philosophy'**.
10. ✅ Comte recognised **sociology as the 'mother social science'**.
11. ✅ A **growing craze** for scientific method made it **fashionable** to distinguish political science from political philosophy.
12. ✅ **Behaviouralism in the 1950s and 1960s** was particularly responsible.
13. ✅ **Since the early 1970s**, with **post-behaviouralism** and the revival of interest in the **theory of justice** and related principles — **liberty, equality, community** — political philosophy received **renewed prominence**.
14. ✅ Settlement: empirical and behavioural studies **find facts and devise tools for achieving the goals of public policy**, whereas **these goals are largely determined with the help of political philosophy**.
15. ✅ Box: political philosophy uses **philosophical and historical method** in determining the goals of public life, and **a major part** of it **coincides with moral and social philosophy**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter states the standard science-philosophy contrast and then refuses to let it stand as a hierarchy. Reconstruct both moves precisely, and identify which half of the refusal is the stronger claim.

**Model answer:** ✅ The contrast is that science **exclusively relies on empirical method, i.e. the method based on sense-experience**, whereas philosophy uses the **transcendental method, i.e. the method based on speculation of what is beyond sense-experience**, and that science is **descriptive** while philosophy is **critical and evaluative**. ✅ The refusal is that **science itself is the product of philosophy**: it **not only owes its origin to philosophy**, it **derives its validity from philosophy itself**. ⚠️ The origin claim is historical and is already supported elsewhere in the chapter by the account of the social sciences spinning off from philosophy at the end of the nineteenth century. ⚠️ The **validity** claim is the stronger and is logical rather than historical: the grounds on which the empirical method is justified are not themselves products of that method. ✅ The chapter supports it by reporting **Auguste Comte (1798-1857)** tracing the foundation of science to **'positive philosophy'**.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the chapter as claiming that political philosophy was destroyed by behaviouralism and then restored. ⚠️ Its wording is that a **growing craze** made the distinction **fashionable**, that behaviouralism in the **1950s and 1960s** was **particularly responsible for this attitude**, and that since the **early 1970s** philosophy received **renewed prominence** — a change of standing, not an extinction and resurrection.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "The scientific study of politics did not make political philosophy redundant." Examine with reference to the chapter's account. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The chapter supports it on historical and logical grounds. ⚠️ Historically, a **growing craze** for scientific method made it **fashionable** to distinguish political science from political philosophy, and **behaviouralism in the 1950s and 1960s** was **particularly responsible for this attitude**; but **since the early 1970s**, with **post-behaviouralism** and the revival of interest in the **theory of justice** and related principles — **liberty, equality, community** — political philosophy received **renewed prominence**. ⚠️ Logically, it denies the contrast on which the eclipse rested: although science uses the **empirical method** and philosophy the **transcendental method**, **science itself is the product of philosophy**, owing it both **origin** and **validity**, as **Auguste Comte (1798-1857)** held in tracing science's foundation to **'positive philosophy'**. ✅ Hence the settlement: empirical studies **find facts and devise tools**, while **goals are largely determined with the help of political philosophy**. 🔑 Means and ends, not winner and loser.

**Rubric [10]:** the dating of the eclipse and the revival **2** · the four revived principles **1** · the empirical/transcendental contrast with both terms **2** · the origin-and-validity refusal **2** · Comte with positive philosophy **1** · the facts-and-tools settlement with its hedge **2**.
