## Lesson 30 — The conclusion: integration without merger, and seven partial approaches

Progress: 30 / 31 | Stage: Advanced | Part IX — the chapter's verdict

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Canonical Core owner §4.5, §5's fourth distinction, §9's fourth trap and §11's closing bullet, with the Advanced companion's §1, §3.9 and §12's third bullet, read against the chapter's section headed "CONCLUSION" in full (Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.146 = printed 131), which supplies the departure from the classical approach, Aristotle's concession, the nineteenth-century separation, the dating of interdisciplinarity's importance, the refusal of merger, the two-way requirement and the complete list of partial approaches.
CA search: "interdisciplinary approach integrated view of political science partial approaches 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing concept-linked was returned. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson; 📰 the single dated anchor in this file is carried at Lesson 10.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Why the conclusion is a lesson of its own

⚠️ The chapter's conclusion is eleven sentences long and contains four separable claims, one of which is the most frequently misreported sentence in this topic. ⚠️ It also contains a concession about Aristotle that neither canonical owner records, and a list whose length both owners get wrong in different ways.

### 2. Claim 1 — departure from the classical approach, and the concession

> Interdisciplinary approach to the study of politics is a modern approach which marks a departure from the classical approach. The classical approach, typified by Aristotle's description of politics as the 'master science' failed to distinguish between normative and empirical aspects of the study of politics. Although it was Aristotle himself who originally identified 'politics' as an independent discipline, yet he could not discern political phenomenon from other aspects of man's social life. In Aristotle's times all knowledge was covered by philosophy, and Aristotle only sought to distinguish 'political philosophy' from other branches of philosophy.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.146 (printed 131)

```text
   THE CHARGE
   the classical approach, typified by the 'MASTER SCIENCE'
   description, FAILED TO DISTINGUISH between NORMATIVE and
   EMPIRICAL aspects
             |
             |  BUT -- and this is the part the owners omit
             v
   THE CONCESSION
   "it was ARISTOTLE HIMSELF who ORIGINALLY IDENTIFIED
    'politics' as an INDEPENDENT DISCIPLINE"
             |
             v
   THE SECOND CHARGE
   "yet he COULD NOT DISCERN political phenomenon from
    OTHER ASPECTS OF MAN'S SOCIAL LIFE"
             |
             v
   THE EXCUSE
   "in Aristotle's times ALL KNOWLEDGE WAS COVERED BY
    PHILOSOPHY, and Aristotle ONLY SOUGHT TO DISTINGUISH
    'political philosophy' from OTHER BRANCHES OF
    PHILOSOPHY"
```

*Caption: four moves in three sentences — charge, concession, second charge, excuse. The structure is a model of how to evaluate a classical thinker in an answer: credit the achievement, state the limit precisely, and explain the limit by the conditions of the period.*

🔑 The concession is the restoration that matters. ⚠️ Both owners record only that the classical approach blurred the normative and empirical. ✅ The chapter also credits Aristotle with having **originally identified 'politics' as an independent discipline** — which is a remarkable thing to concede in the course of criticising him, and it is precisely why the criticism is not crude.

⚠️ And note the exact content of the second charge. ⚠️ It is **not** that Aristotle failed to separate politics from other disciplines; the disciplines did not exist. ✅ It is that he **could not discern political phenomenon from other aspects of man's social life** — a failure of analytical separation within an undivided field of knowledge, which the chapter immediately excuses by the state of knowledge in his time.

### 3. Claim 2 — the nineteenth-century separation, and the dating

> It was only in the nineteenth century that different aspects of man's social life became independent subjects of empirical study as distinct from their philosophical foundations. Accordingly, psychology, sociology, economics, political science, etc. emerged as independent scientific disciplines.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.146 (printed 131)

> The interdisciplinary approach in social sciences is of recent origin which assumed special importance in the latter part of the twentieth century.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.146 (printed 131)

⚠️ Two dates, both absent from the owners:

| ✅ Event | ✅ Dating |
|---|---|
| Different aspects of social life become **independent subjects of empirical study** | ✅ **The nineteenth century** |
| **Psychology, sociology, economics, political science** emerge as **independent scientific disciplines** | ✅ Same |
| The interdisciplinary approach **assumed special importance** | ✅ **The latter part of the twentieth century** |

⚠️ Note that **political science is in the list this time**, which the p.138 spin-off list did not include — there it was the late-starter standing outside. ⚠️ The conclusion folds it in, which is consistent: by the twentieth century it too had become an independent empirical discipline.

🔑 The chronological shape of the whole chapter is therefore: **one field → division in the nineteenth century → reintegration of evidence in the latter twentieth century, without reunification of the fields.**

### 4. Claim 3 — the refusal of merger, and what replaces it

> It does not seek to merge all social sciences into a single 'master science'. On the contrary, it insists that the results of our inquiry in any sphere should be based on and verified from the findings of all relevant social sciences. Thus political science should not only make use of data, theories and models of other social sciences, but should also make a suitable contribution to an understanding of those disciplines.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.146 (printed 131)

| ❌ What the approach does **not** seek | ✅ What it insists on instead |
|---|---|
| To **merge all social sciences into a single 'master science'** | ⚠️ That results **in any sphere** be **based on** and **verified from** the findings of **all relevant social sciences** |
| — | ⚠️ That political science **make use of** data, theories and models — implication (a) |
| — | ⚠️ That it **also make a suitable contribution** to understanding those disciplines — implication (c) |

🔑 Three observations that lift an answer:

- ⚠️ **The phrase 'master science' returns.** It was Aristotle's description of politics at p.138; here it names the thing interdisciplinarity refuses to build. ⚠️ The chapter has closed a loop: the classical error was to treat one discipline as master, and the modern temptation would be to fuse them all into one. ⚠️ Both are refused by the same argument.
- ⚠️ **In any sphere.** The requirement is symmetrical, as Lesson 8's reply noted; it is not a special burden on political science.
- ⚠️ **Based on and verified from** — two relations again, exactly implications (a) and (b) from Lesson 4, restated at the close.

### 5. Claim 4 — the seven partial approaches

✅ The chapter's final sentences:

> Moreover, some approaches to the study of politics cover a limited range of inquiry by relying on a single discipline. Thus philosophical, historical, legal, economic, sociological, psychological and geographical approaches turn out to be partial approaches. On the contrary, the interdisciplinary approach seeks to overcome this shortcoming by relying on all other relevant disciplines for the purpose of evolving an integrated view of the subject.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.5, PDF p.146 (printed 131)

```text
   THE SEVEN PARTIAL APPROACHES, in the chapter's order

     1  PHILOSOPHICAL      -- taught at Lesson 29
     2  HISTORICAL         -- taught at Lessons 20-22
     3  LEGAL              -- NO "use of" section in the
                              chapter; named only here
     4  ECONOMIC           -- taught at Lessons 23-25
     5  SOCIOLOGICAL       -- taught at Lessons 26-27
     6  PSYCHOLOGICAL      -- taught at Lesson 28
     7  GEOGRAPHICAL       -- NO "use of" section in the
                              chapter; named only here

   WHY THEY ARE PARTIAL: each "covers a LIMITED RANGE OF
   INQUIRY by relying on a SINGLE DISCIPLINE".

   WHAT THE REMEDY IS: relying on ALL OTHER RELEVANT
   DISCIPLINES for the purpose of EVOLVING AN INTEGRATED
   VIEW OF THE SUBJECT.
```

*Caption: five of the seven received a section; two — legal and geographical — are named here and nowhere else in the chapter. They are nonetheless the chapter's own names, printed in the chapter's own final sentence.*

### 6. ⚠️ The disagreement between the canonical owners, stated openly

⚠️ The two owners treat this sentence differently, and they cannot both be right.

| ⚠️ Source | ⚠️ What it says | ⚠️ Status |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Basic owner §9, fourth trap | ⚠️ Reports the conclusion as holding that **philosophical, historical, legal, economic, sociological and psychological** approaches are partial when used alone — ❌ **six names; geographical is omitted** | ❌ Incomplete against the page |
| ⚠️ Basic owner §13 | ⚠️ Headed *Extending the discipline list: anthropology, law and geography*, and states that the chapter **foregrounds history, economics, sociology, psychology and philosophy**, so that a complete answer **should also** be able to place anthropology, law and geography on the same map | ⚠️ Mis-types **law** and **geography** as additions beyond the chapter |
| ⚠️ Advanced owner §12, third bullet | ⚠️ Warns: **do not present Gauba's disciplinary map as if it abolishes legal or geographical approaches; his conclusion is that single approaches are partial, not useless** | ✅ Correctly treats legal and geographical as belonging to the chapter's conclusion |

✅ **The page settles it.** The sentence at PDF p.146 names **seven** approaches and includes both **legal** and **geographical**. ⚠️ So:

- ✅ The Advanced owner is right that legal and geographical approaches are in the chapter's conclusion.
- ❌ The Basic owner's §9 is incomplete: it drops **geographical** from a seven-name list.
- ⚠️ The Basic owner's §13 is mis-typed: **law** and **geography** are **chapter-named**, not owner-added. ⚠️ What §13 genuinely adds is the *developed treatment* — political anthropology as a field, law as a source of evidence distinct from the legal approach, and political geography as a spatial analysis of politics. ⚠️ That developed treatment is an owner extension and is marked 🔶 throughout Lesson 31.
- ⚠️ **Anthropology** occupies a third position, different from both: ✅ it is named repeatedly in the chapter as a discipline and a donor — in the five-item social-science list at p.136, in the purposefulness warning at p.137, among the disciplines that spun off from philosophy at p.138, and as the origin of structural-functionalism at p.140 — ❌ but it is **not** in the seven-name list of partial approaches. ⚠️ Treating it as chapter-named *as a discipline* and owner-developed *as an approach* is the accurate position.

⚠️ ❌ No canonical or sibling-topic file is edited to record any of this; the disagreement is disclosed here and the page is cited.

### 7. How to teach all seven without over-claiming

| ✅ Approach | ✅ Chapter status | ⚠️ What a candidate may safely say |
|---|---|---|
| **Philosophical** | ✅ Named at p.146; ✅ full section pp.145-146 | ⚠️ Clarifies concepts, determines goals; ❌ alone, it cannot explain behaviour |
| **Historical** | ✅ Named at p.146; ✅ full section p.141 | ⚠️ Supplies data, comparison and verification; ❌ alone, it cannot settle present moral worth |
| **Legal** | ✅ **Named at p.146**; ❌ no section in this chapter | ⚠️ Reads politics through law, command and formal allocation; ❌ alone, it misses the gap between rule and practice — ⚠️ the chapter's own p.138 objection |
| **Economic** | ✅ Named at p.146; ✅ full section pp.142-143 | ⚠️ Supplies production, distribution, demands and incentives; ❌ alone, it cannot settle legitimacy — ⚠️ and even economic decisions are shaped by non-economic factors |
| **Sociological** | ✅ Named at p.146; ✅ full section pp.143-144 | ⚠️ Supplies association, institutions, attitudes and feasibility; ❌ alone, it risks reducing politics to social structure |
| **Psychological** | ✅ Named at p.146; ✅ full section pp.144-145 | ⚠️ Supplies motive, personality, opinion and propaganda; ❌ alone, it cannot explain structures or economic conflict |
| **Geographical** | ✅ **Named at p.146**; ❌ no section in this chapter | ⚠️ Supplies the spatial dimension — ⚠️ note that the chapter's own data list at p.139 includes the **rural-urban ratio**, and that **human geography** is placed among the social sciences at p.136; 🔶 the developed treatment is the owner's |

🔑 The honest formula for an answer: **seven are named; five are expounded; two are named without a section, and the developed account of those two is supplied from beyond the chapter.** ⚠️ Saying this explicitly is better than silently padding the chapter.

### 8. The strongest objection, the reply, and what is left over

⚠️ **Objection.** The conclusion is in tension with itself. It refuses to merge the social sciences into a master science, yet it requires that results **in any sphere** be **based on and verified from the findings of all relevant social sciences**. A discipline whose results must be grounded in and checked against every other relevant discipline is not an independent discipline; it is a department of an unacknowledged master science. The chapter has rejected the name while accepting the thing.

✅ **Reply.** The distinction between merger and integration is doctrinally precise. ⚠️ A master science would have one set of concepts, one method and one problem-agenda; what the chapter requires is that **separate** disciplines, retaining their own questions, use one another's evidence and submit their results to one another's findings. ⚠️ The disciplines remain distinct because their **focus** differs, and the chapter keeps that focus requirement alive to the end by insisting the contribution run both ways — a master science would not need a contribution from its own department. ✅ The verb pair is decisive: **based on** and **verified from** are relations between distinct bodies of enquiry, not within a single one.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply once again rests on focus, and the objection's force is proportional to how robust that notion is. ⚠️ This chapter never defines focus independently of the researcher's purpose, so the autonomy it defends is procedural rather than substantive. ⚠️ The honest verdict is that the chapter's refusal of merger is coherent, is argued, and is not fully secured — and an answer that says exactly that is giving the strongest available reading.

### 9. Using this in an answer

- 🔑 **Syllabus relevance.** This lesson contains the chapter's final position and the seven-name list, which together form the mandatory conclusion of any answer on this topic.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** *Why does the interdisciplinary approach not lead to a single master science?* — the four claims, in order.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing **six** partial approaches. ⚠️ The sentence names **seven**, and **geographical** is the one most often dropped.
- ❌ **Second trap.** Claiming that the chapter devotes a section to the legal or geographical approach. ✅ It names them; it does not expound them.
- 🔑 **Answer use.** The closing phrase — **evolving an integrated view of the subject** — is the right last line for almost any Mains answer on this topic.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The interdisciplinary approach is a **modern approach** marking a **departure from the classical approach**. (PDF p.146)
2. ✅ The classical approach, **typified by Aristotle's description of politics as the 'master science'**, **failed to distinguish between normative and empirical aspects**.
3. ✅ **Although it was Aristotle himself who originally identified 'politics' as an independent discipline** — ⚠️ the concession both owners omit.
4. ✅ **Yet he could not discern political phenomenon from other aspects of man's social life.**
5. ✅ **In Aristotle's times all knowledge was covered by philosophy**, and he sought only to distinguish **'political philosophy' from other branches of philosophy**.
6. ✅ **Only in the nineteenth century** did different aspects of social life become **independent subjects of empirical study**.
7. ✅ **Psychology, sociology, economics, political science** then emerged as **independent scientific disciplines** — ⚠️ political science is in this list, unlike the p.138 list.
8. ✅ The interdisciplinary approach is **of recent origin** and **assumed special importance in the latter part of the twentieth century**.
9. ✅ **It does not seek to merge all social sciences into a single 'master science'.**
10. ✅ It insists that results **in any sphere** be **based on and verified from the findings of all relevant social sciences**.
11. ✅ Political science must also **make a suitable contribution to an understanding of those disciplines**.
12. ✅ **Seven** partial approaches: **philosophical, historical, legal, economic, sociological, psychological and geographical**.
13. ✅ They are partial because each **covers a limited range of inquiry by relying on a single discipline**.
14. ✅ The remedy: relying on **all other relevant disciplines** for the purpose of **evolving an integrated view of the subject**.
15. ⚠️ Owner disagreement disclosed: the Basic owner's §9 lists six and drops geographical; its §13 treats law and geography as additions; the Advanced owner's §12 correctly places both in the conclusion. ✅ The page supports the Advanced owner.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter's conclusion names seven partial approaches but devotes a section to only five. State what follows, and what does not follow, from that asymmetry.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ What follows is that the **legal** and **geographical** approaches are the chapter's own names for ways of studying politics — they appear in its final sentence alongside the philosophical, historical, economic, sociological and psychological — but that the chapter supplies no developed account of either. ⚠️ Their presence is nonetheless prepared earlier: **human geography** is placed among the social sciences at the opening, and the **rural-urban ratio** appears in the chapter's own data list. ❌ What does **not** follow is that the chapter expounds them, or that anything it says about them beyond the naming may be attributed to it. ❌ It also does not follow that they are negligible: the chapter's verdict is that single-discipline approaches are **partial**, which is a limitation, not a dismissal. ⚠️ A developed treatment of law as evidence and of political geography is available, but it comes from beyond the chapter and must be labelled as such.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not reproduce the list as six approaches. ⚠️ The sentence at PDF p.146 names **philosophical, historical, legal, economic, sociological, psychological and geographical** — seven — and **geographical** is the one most frequently dropped, including by one of this topic's own canonical owners.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "Integration, not merger." Critically examine the chapter's final position on the interdisciplinary approach. *(Critically examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The conclusion is explicit. ✅ Negatively, the approach **does not seek to merge all social sciences into a single 'master science'** — the phrase it used for Aristotle's conception of politics, so classical and modern temptations are refused by one argument. ✅ Positively, results **in any sphere** must be **based on and verified from the findings of all relevant social sciences**, and political science must **make a suitable contribution to an understanding of those disciplines**. ⚠️ The requirement is symmetrical, which is why it is integration rather than absorption. ✅ Its necessity follows from the closing list: **philosophical, historical, legal, economic, sociological, psychological and geographical** approaches each **cover a limited range of inquiry by relying on a single discipline** and **turn out to be partial approaches**. 🔑 The remedy is **evolving an integrated view of the subject**. ⚠️ Autonomy rests on focus, never independently defined.

**Rubric [10]:** the refusal of merger, quoted or closely reported **2** · the based-on-and-verified-from requirement with *in any sphere* **2** · the contribution-back limb **1** · all seven partial approaches **3** · the reason they are partial and the integrated-view remedy **1** · an honest residual about autonomy by focus **1**.

## Lesson 31 — Owner extensions, reductionism by name, and the answer architecture

### 1. What this lesson is, and what it is not

⚠️ Lessons 1-30 stayed inside the chapter. ⚠️ This lesson does two different things, and keeping them apart matters: it develops the three approaches the chapter names or uses without expounding, and it converts the whole topic into an answer architecture.

❌ Nothing in sections 2-5 below carries a page anchor in the chapter's range, and every claim in them is marked 🔶.

### 2. 🔶 The three developed approaches

#### 2a. 🔶 Political anthropology

⚠️ Anthropology is named repeatedly across the chapter — in the social-science list and the straddling pairs at p.136, in the purposefulness warning at p.137, among the disciplines that left philosophy and again among those in which the empirical method was first developed at p.138, and as the home of structural-functionalism at p.140 — ❌ but it has no section, and it is **not** among the seven partial approaches.

🔶 The owner's developed treatment:

| 🔶 Element | 🔶 Content |
|---|---|
| 🔶 **Claim** | 🔶 Political order does not require a centralised state |
| 🔶 **Evidence** | 🔶 Kinship- and lineage-based authority and dispute-resolution structures documented across non-state and pre-state societies — lineage systems, tribal councils, chieftaincy |
| 🔶 **Significance** | 🔶 Tests and refines political theory's core concepts — authority, legitimacy, sovereignty — against cases in which the modern state is not the unit of analysis |
| 🔶 **Limit** | 🔶 Findings from small-scale societies cannot be mechanically transplanted onto large, complex modern states |

⚠️ Two anchors connect this to the chapter without over-claiming. ✅ First, the structural-functional approach, which the chapter says **originated in cultural anthropology**, was built precisely to describe order where familiar institutions are absent. ✅ Second, Lipset's quoted statement at p.140 holds that politics varies among systems with **different concepts of kinship ties** — which is an anthropological variable on the chapter's own page.

#### 2b. 🔶 Law as a source of evidence, distinct from the legal approach

⚠️ The chapter names a **legal** approach among the seven partial approaches but never describes it. ➡️ Topic 08 owns the legal approach as a way of studying politics.

🔶 The owner's extension is a different thing, and the distinction is the point:

```text
   THE LEGAL APPROACH             LAW AS A SOURCE OF
   (Topic 08's property)          EVIDENCE (this extension)
   ----------------------         -------------------------
   a way of STUDYING politics     a BODY OF MATERIAL used
   through law and command        inside any approach

   selects what counts as data    supplies constitutional
                                  text, statutes and
                                  judicial reasoning as
                                  primary evidence of how
                                  authority is formally
                                  allocated and constrained

   LIMIT (shared): legal text alone cannot explain informal
   power, bargaining or non-compliance -- which is why
   sociology and psychology must stand beside it.
```

*Caption: a candidate who conflates these two will either repeat Topic 08 or claim that this chapter expounds the legal approach. Separating them keeps both topics honest.*

⚠️ Note that the shared limit is the chapter's own: political reality cannot be understood **merely in terms of formal institutions which may operate differently in actual practice**.

#### 2c. 🔶 Political geography

✅ The chapter's own preparation, already noted: ✅ **human geography** falls in the domain of the social sciences at p.136; ✅ the **rural-urban ratio** is among the data a political scientist may require at p.139; ✅ and a **geographical** approach is named among the seven at p.146.

🔶 The owner's developed treatment:

| 🔶 Element | 🔶 Content |
|---|---|
| 🔶 **Claim** | 🔶 Where power and resources are located in space shapes political conflict and institutional design |
| 🔶 **Evidence** | 🔶 Boundary disputes; regional imbalance between resource-rich and resource-poor areas; federal arrangements responding to territorial diversity |
| 🔶 **Significance** | 🔶 Explains regionalism, federalism and centre-state or inter-state conflict that a purely institutional or economic account would miss |
| 🔶 **Limit** | 🔶 Geography constrains but does not determine; it must be combined with sociology for identity and economics for resource conflict, and must not be treated as destiny |

⚠️ ➡️ Specific boundary arrangements, tribunals, reorganisations and cases belong to the Polity, Geography and History owners. ❌ None is named here.

### 3. 🔶 The reductionisms, named one by one

⚠️ The chapter warns generally that borrowing must stay politics-centred. 🔶 The owner names the specific failure modes, and naming them is what converts a vague warning into a usable critical vocabulary.

| 🔶 Reductionism | 🔶 The move | 🔶 What it misses |
|---|---|---|
| 🔶 **Economic** | 🔶 Treating all politics as market bargaining or class interest | 🔶 Legitimacy, identity and normative claims not reducible to material interest |
| 🔶 **Psychological** | 🔶 Treating all politics as individual attitude or personality | 🔶 Structural, institutional and economic constraints operating independently of any actor's psychology |
| 🔶 **Sociological or functional** | 🔶 Treating all politics as social-structure maintenance | 🔶 Conflict, agency and deliberate institutional change — ⚠️ a conservative bias |
| 🔶 **Legal** | 🔶 Treating politics as fully captured by formal rules | 🔶 Informal power and non-compliance |
| ⚠️ **Historical** — ⚠️ added here by extension of the same logic | ⚠️ Treating present arrangements as fully explained by their antecedents | ⚠️ Present choice, and the chapter's own insistence on testing by **rule and exception** |
| ⚠️ **Geographical** — ⚠️ added here by extension | ⚠️ Treating spatial position as destiny | ⚠️ The owner's own stated limit: geography constrains but does not determine |

🔑 The structure is identical in every row: each reductionism takes one of the five borrowed contributions and promotes it from *a* source of explanation to *the* source. ⚠️ The chapter's corrective is the purposefulness lock — the borrowed model sharpens political analysis only so long as the political question, and not the donor discipline's priorities, remains in view.

⚠️ The last two rows are marked as extensions of the owner's own logic; ❌ neither the owner nor the chapter names them.

### 4. 🔶 The two objection-reply chains

🔶 **Chain 1 — dissolution of subject matter.**

- 🔶 **Objection:** if political science borrows core models from economics, sociology and psychology, it risks losing any distinct subject-matter of its own and dissolving into social science in general.
- ✅ **Reply, and it is the chapter's own:** interdisciplinary borrowing integrates evidence and models but **does not seek to merge all social sciences into a single 'master science'**; the political question remains the organising centre even when the evidence used to answer it is economic, sociological or psychological.
- ⚠️ **Residual, stated in this file's voice:** the reply secures autonomy by focus, and focus is defined by the researcher's governing purpose rather than by any external test. ⚠️ The autonomy is therefore real but procedural.

🔶 **Chain 2 — over-generalisation from distant evidence.**

- 🔶 **Objection:** using anthropological or geographical evidence risks importing findings from very different contexts — small-scale societies, or purely spatial patterns — and over-generalising them to large modern democratic states.
- 🔶 **Reply:** such evidence is used for testing and illustrating a political claim, not for asserting that small-scale or spatial patterns transfer mechanically; each discipline's evidence is weighed for relevance rather than imported wholesale. ⚠️ The owner itself marks this as a reasonable extension of the chapter's purposeful-borrowing standard, ❌ not a direct quotation.
- ✅ **Support available from the chapter:** the requirement that theory be verified **both in terms of rule and exception** is exactly a discipline against over-generalisation, and the purposefulness lock requires that borrowing serve the political problem.

### 5. 🔶 Cautious Indian application

⚠️ ❌ **Three rules apply to everything in this section.** ❌ No figure, scheme, party, leader, case, tribunal or dated episode is named. ❌ Nothing is asserted as a current fact. ⚠️ Each item is offered as a **type** of application.

| 🔶 Lens | 🔶 Type of application | ⚠️ What must be routed elsewhere |
|---|---|---|
| 🔶 **Political economy** | 🔶 Debates on economic liberalisation, welfare-scheme design and federal fiscal transfers illustrate political theory using economic evidence to analyse policy conflict | ❌ Specific figures, scheme names, years — ➡️ the Economy and Polity owners |
| 🔶 **Political sociology** | 🔶 The role of caste, community and regional identity in political mobilisation and political culture is a standard illustrative anchor for the sociology-politics link | ❌ Electoral statistics and particular mobilisations — ➡️ the Polity and Social Issues owners; ✅ note that the chapter's own Lipset quotation already licenses caste as a comparative **variable** |
| 🔶 **Political geography** | 🔶 Federal reorganisation and continuing regional and resource-sharing disputes illustrate how territory and resource distribution shape political conflict | ❌ Exact dates, tribunal names, case detail — ➡️ the Polity, Geography and History owners |
| ✅ **Chapter-grounded** | ✅ **Any study of the Indian political system must start with its historical antecedents** — ✅ a methodological instruction, on the page at p.141 | ❌ Which antecedents, which period, what they explain |

🔑 Only the last row is source-grounded. ⚠️ The distinction between it and the three above it is exactly the ✅/🔶 distinction, and an answer that observes it is demonstrating evidential discipline.

### 6. The executable answer architecture

#### 6a. Directive decoding

| ⚠️ Stem shape | ⚠️ What it is really asking | ⚠️ Minimum content |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ *Discuss the interdisciplinary character of political science* | ⚠️ Definition plus demonstration plus conclusion | ⚠️ Two-limb definition; at least three disciplines with one contribution each; **integration without merger** |
| ⚠️ *Examine the contribution of [a named discipline] to political analysis* | ⚠️ Single-discipline depth | ⚠️ That discipline's specific contribution, one borrowed model or worked instance from the chapter, and its limit if used alone |
| ⚠️ *Can political science be studied independently of other social sciences?* | ⚠️ The autonomy question | ⚠️ The dissolution objection at full strength, the chapter's reply, and the residual |
| ⚠️ *Why are single-cause theories of politics analytically weak?* | ⚠️ The reductionism question | ⚠️ The seven partial approaches, two or three named reductionisms, and the integrated-view remedy |
| ⚠️ *Trace the development of the interdisciplinary approach* | ⚠️ Chronology | ⚠️ Rudimentary ancestry; Marx and Engels; the empirical turn; the latter twentieth century |

#### 6b. The 10-mark architecture — about 150 words

```text
   1  THESIS         political science needs other disciplines'
                     evidence and models but keeps the political
                     question central
   2  BODY           2-3 disciplines, each as
                     CLAIM -> NAMED EVIDENCE -> SIGNIFICANCE
   3  LIMIT          one named reductionism risk
   4  CONCLUSION     one line: integration, not merger
```

#### 6c. The 15-mark architecture — about 200-250 words

```text
   1  THESIS + directive decoding
   2  DOCTRINE       two-limb definition
                     -> why empirical/behavioural/systems
                        analysis pushed political science
                        outward
                     -> 4-5 disciplines, each with
                        CLAIM -> EVIDENCE -> SIGNIFICANCE -> LIMIT
                        (include at least one of
                         anthropology / law / geography)
   3  MODEL          name one borrowed model: Easton's system,
                     or Lasswell's problem-solving model, or
                     the structural-functional relay
   4  OBJECTION      one objection-reply chain
   5  CONCLUSION     integration without merger, reasoned
```

#### 6d. The 20-mark architecture — about 250-300 words

```text
   1  THESIS + directive decoding
   2  DOCTRINE       full reconstruction across history,
                     economics, sociology, psychology and
                     philosophy, PLUS legal and geographical
                     as chapter-named, each with
                     CLAIM -> EVIDENCE -> SIGNIFICANCE -> LIMIT
   3  MODELS         at least two named: Easton's system;
                     the Malinowski/Radcliffe-Brown ->
                     Parsons -> Almond and Powell relay;
                     elite theory with Pareto, Mosca,
                     Michels; Schumpeter and Downs on the
                     open market
   4  REDUCTIONISM   the specific risk for each donor
   5  OBJECTIONS     both chains -- dissolution of subject
                     matter; over-generalisation from
                     distant evidence
   6  INDIA          one cautiously hedged application
   7  CONCLUSION     graded: integration deepens explanation;
                     merger is neither possible nor desirable
```

#### 6e. The quotation method

⚠️ Prefer paraphrased, named propositions over unverified quotations. ⚠️ Where the chapter's own wording is distinctive and short, it carries well — **integrated view of the subject**, **partial approaches**, **politics cannot deliver the goods**, **rule and exception**, **stabilized systems of expectations and actions**. ❌ Do not manufacture a longer quotation around them.

### 7. The strongest objection to this lesson itself

⚠️ **Objection.** A lesson that adds three approaches, six reductionisms and an answer template is padding a short chapter with material it does not contain, and the 🔶 marking is a licence to do so.

✅ **Reply.** Two of the three approaches are named in the chapter's own final sentence and are therefore required by it, not added to it; the third — anthropology — is named repeatedly in the chapter, at pp.136, 137, 138 and 140, as a discipline, as a donor and as the origin of a borrowed approach. ⚠️ The reductionisms are a critical vocabulary for a warning the chapter gives in general terms, and the architecture is a method of deployment, not a claim about content. ⚠️ The 🔶 marking exists precisely so that a reader can strip this lesson out and still have the chapter intact.

⚠️ **Residual.** The objection retains force against the last two rows of the reductionism table and against any Indian illustration, which is why those are marked twice — ⚠️ once as extensions and ❌ once as carrying no particulars. ⚠️ A reader who wants only the chapter should read Lessons 1-30.

### Revision notes

1. 🔶 **Political anthropology**: political order does not require a centralised state; evidence from kinship and lineage authority; it tests authority, legitimacy and sovereignty against non-state cases; ❌ it does not transfer mechanically to large modern states.
2. ✅ Anthropology is named in the chapter at pp.136, 137, 138 and 140 — ❌ but not among the seven partial approaches.
3. ✅ Structural-functionalism **originated in cultural anthropology**, and Lipset's **different concepts of kinship ties** is an anthropological variable — the chapter's two strongest anthropological anchors.
4. 🔶 **Law as evidence** is distinct from the **legal approach**: the first is material used inside any approach; ➡️ the second is a way of studying politics owned by Topic 08.
5. ✅ Shared limit: formal rules alone cannot explain informal power, bargaining or non-compliance — ⚠️ the chapter's own p.138 objection.
6. 🔶 **Political geography**: the spatial location of power and resources shapes conflict and institutional design; ❌ geography constrains but does not determine.
7. ✅ The chapter prepares geography at p.136 (**human geography**) and p.139 (**rural-urban ratio**) before naming it at p.146.
8. 🔶 Four named reductionisms: **economic**, **psychological**, **sociological or functional**, **legal**.
9. ⚠️ Two further ones — **historical** and **geographical** — are extensions of the same logic, named in this file's voice only.
10. 🔑 Every reductionism promotes one contribution from *a* source of explanation to *the* source.
11. 🔶 Objection-reply chain 1: dissolution of subject matter → ✅ the chapter's refusal of merger → ⚠️ autonomy by focus is procedural.
12. 🔶 Objection-reply chain 2: over-generalisation from small-scale or spatial evidence → 🔶 evidence is weighed for relevance → ✅ supported by **rule and exception** and the purposefulness lock.
13. ⚠️ Indian applications are offered as **types**; ❌ no figure, scheme, party, case or date is named anywhere.
14. ✅ The one chapter-grounded Indian instruction is that **any study of the Indian political system must start with its historical antecedents**.
15. ⚠️ Answer ceilings: 10-mark within 150 words; 15- and 20-mark within 250 words — 🔑 and close any answer with **evolving an integrated view of the subject**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Gauba adds anthropology, law and geography to the five disciplines he discusses." Correct the statement precisely, distinguishing the three cases from one another.

**Model answer:** ❌ The statement is wrong in three different ways. ✅ **Law** and **geography** are not additions at all: **legal** and **geographical** approaches are named in the chapter's own closing sentence, which lists **philosophical, historical, legal, economic, sociological, psychological and geographical** approaches as partial — ⚠️ what the chapter omits is not the names but any developed treatment of those two. ✅ **Anthropology** is named repeatedly in the chapter, at pp.136, 137, 138 and 140 — among the social sciences and the straddling pairs, in the purposefulness warning, among the disciplines that left philosophy and those in which the empirical method was first developed, and as the origin of structural-functionalism — ❌ but it is **not** in the seven-name list. ⚠️ So the accurate formulation is: seven approaches are chapter-named and five are expounded; anthropology is chapter-named as a discipline and donor but not as an approach; and the developed accounts of political anthropology, law-as-evidence and political geography come from beyond the chapter and must be labelled as extensions.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the 🔶 extensions as the chapter's content merely because they are useful. ⚠️ The chapter's authority attaches to what is on its pages; an extension's value is that it answers a question the chapter raises and leaves open, and saying so explicitly is stronger than silent assimilation.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Why are single-discipline approaches to the study of politics analytically weak? *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Because each **covers a limited range of inquiry by relying on a single discipline**, and the chapter accordingly judges **philosophical, historical, legal, economic, sociological, psychological and geographical** approaches to be **partial approaches**. ⚠️ Partiality has a specific pathology: each becomes a reductionism when its contribution is promoted from *a* source of explanation to *the* source. ⚠️ Economic reductionism loses legitimacy and identity; psychological reductionism loses structural and institutional constraint; sociological or functional reductionism loses conflict and deliberate change; legal reductionism loses informal power and non-compliance. ✅ The chapter's own corrective is twofold: borrowing must be **purposeful and well-conceived**, and results **in any sphere** must be **based on and verified from the findings of all relevant social sciences**. 🔑 The remedy is not to prefer one lens but to evolve **an integrated view of the subject**. ⚠️ The named reductionisms extend the chapter's general warning rather than quoting it.

**Rubric [10]:** the limited-range formulation with all seven approaches **3** · the structure common to every reductionism **2** · at least three named reductionisms with what each loses **2** · both correctives, quoted or closely reported **2** · the integrated-view conclusion **1**.

## The honest position, stated before anything else

⚠️ ⚠️ **No examined stem in the locally held Philosophy Optional Paper-II, Section-A record, 2018 through 2026, names the interdisciplinary approach, the relation between political science and any other social science, or any of the seven partial approaches.** ⚠️ All one hundred and twenty-six Section-A parts across the nine examined years were read for this file — the one hundred and twelve parts of the 2018-2025 bank and the fourteen parts of the 2026 supplement — and the result is a clean negative. ❌ **This topic therefore has no primary PYQ anchor, and none is manufactured here.**

❌ **No General Studies PYQ is claimed for this topic.** ⚠️ Political Theory is supplementary conceptual support across the General Studies papers; it is **not** a directly named GS paper owner, and constructing a GS route in order to manufacture coverage would be a fabrication. ❌ **No synthetic question, no reconstructed stem and no inferred ownership appears below.**

✅ What is offered instead is a curated set of **nine adjacent application anchors** — one for each examined year — each owned by a socio-political file other than this one and used only to show **what an interdisciplinary frame contributes to an answer whose subject belongs elsewhere**. ⚠️ Every stem below preserves the ledger wording, paper code, question number and marks. ❌ No model answer is supplied for any of them, and ❌ no answer, elimination cue or corrective mapping appears anywhere in this section.
