## Anchor 1 — 2026, `KVMS-B-PHL`, Q4(c) [15] ✅ strong fit

> ✅ "Examine the relationship between democracy and development. Does democracy promote or hinder development ?"

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Development and Social Progress. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What this topic contributes:** the stem asks for a **relationship** between a political form and a socio-economic process, and then a verdict on it. ⚠️ That is precisely the question the chapter uses to justify data-borrowing: whether a particular form of government **will be sustained by a given community** cannot be settled from political material alone. ✅ Lesson 15 supplies the evidence roster — **rate of economic growth, distribution of national income, patterns of ownership of material wealth, educational levels and percentage of literacy, religious and cultural attitudes, family structures, professional distribution, rural-urban ratio** — and Lesson 16 supplies the routing: interest in **political development** increases reliance on **sociology and economics**. ⚠️ Lipset's statement is directly on point, since political development **cannot be treated without looking for the conditions of social mobilization**.
- ❌ **Boundary:** the conception of development, the growth/development distinction, capability accounts and the substantive verdict belong wholly to the owner. ⚠️ This topic supplies the evidential frame, not the content.

## Anchor 2 — 2025, `SLPM-B-PHL`, Q3(c) [15] ⚠️ moderate fit

> ✅ "Is it possible to reconcile the concept of development with tribal values to bring social and economic progress? Discuss."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Development and Social Progress. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What this topic contributes:** the stem turns on whether evidence drawn from one kind of society can be used to judge a process defined in another. ✅ The chapter's own anthropological anchors bear on this: **cultural anthropology** is among the five social sciences; **politics will be different in caste and non-caste societies**, and will vary among systems with **different concepts of kinship ties**; and structural-functional analysis, which **originated in the field of cultural anthropology**, was built precisely to describe order where familiar institutions are absent. 🔶 Lesson 31's second objection-reply chain supplies the caution against transplanting small-scale findings mechanically.
- ❌ **Boundary:** the substantive account of development, of tribal values and of the reconciliation belongs to the owner. ❌ No community, region or policy is named here.

## Anchor 3 — 2024, `PHKM-B-PHL`, Q3(c) [10+5=15] ✅ strong fit

> ✅ "Is economic development a necessary condition, sufficient condition, both or neither, in order to achieve social progress? Give reasons and justifications for your answer."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Development and Social Progress. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What this topic contributes:** a conditions stem is an invitation to misuse one discipline. ✅ The chapter supplies both halves of the disciplined answer. ⚠️ On one side it holds that **politics cannot deliver the goods until it is able to tackle the economic problem effectively** and that an **adequate knowledge of economics becomes essential**. ⚠️ On the other it holds that economic decisions are themselves **greatly affected by 'non-economic factors', such as cultural values, personality, political needs, and considerations of status**, and that modern political economy requires policy to be **not only economically efficient but also politically acceptable**. 🔶 The named economic reductionism of Lesson 31 is the explicit warning against answering a sufficiency question in economic terms alone.
- ❌ **Boundary:** the concept of social progress, its criteria and the final verdict belong to the owner. ⚠️ The ledger records this part as **10+5=15**; ⚠️ the two-part marking is the examiner's, not this topic's concern.

## Anchor 4 — 2023, `SKYC-B-PHLY`, Q3(a) [20] ⚠️ moderate fit

> ✅ "Explain Historical Materialism and discuss its relevance in the context of social development and change."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Political Ideologies. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What this topic contributes:** the chapter treats the doctrine as a **borrowed model** and supplies a compact, quotable statement of it. ✅ The **Marxian framework of 'base and superstructure'** postulates that **politics, culture, morals, ideas and institutions in any society are shaped by the prevailing mode of material production**, and it was **first evolved in the field of economics and sociology, and then adopted as a tool of political analysis**. ✅ The chapter also supplies the provenance — Marx **(1818-83)** and Engels **(1820-95)**, around the middle of the nineteenth century, locating the primary source of political behaviour in the **level of technological development**, the **mode of material production** and the **prevailing class structure** — and, in the political-economy box, **Marxist political economy**'s focus on the relation between mode of production and **social formations**.
- ❌ **Boundary:** the full doctrinal exposition, the forces-and-relations dynamic and the assessment of relevance belong to the owner and to the Marxism topic. ⚠️ This topic owns the model's status as an import and its exact formulation.

## Anchor 5 — 2022, `CRNA-S-PHLY`, Q4(a) [20] ✅ strong fit

> ✅ "Discuss propaganda as a challenge to democratic form of government."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Forms of Government. ❌ Not this topic.
- 🔑 **What this topic contributes:** propaganda is an object the chapter assigns explicitly to political psychology. ✅ The study of politics involves an interest in **the impact of propaganda** and in **the role of mass communication in inculcating political culture**, and the boxed definition of political psychology includes **techniques and impact of political propaganda** within its scope. ⚠️ The chapter also supplies the historical case — the **growth of totalitarian political movements in the thirties and forties of the twentieth century** aroused interest in the **'authoritarian personality'**, pursued through **psycho-analytic theory** and **social psychological methods** to understand **extremist political appeals**, notably by **Harold Lasswell (1902-78)**. ⚠️ And the box's clause that political psychology **may also be used for influencing political behaviour of a group** is the sharpest statement of why the challenge is structural rather than incidental.
- ⚠️ **Why this is among the strongest of the nine:** the stem's central object is named three times in the chapter's own pages, and the chapter supplies both the explanatory apparatus and the warning.
- ❌ **Boundary:** the theory of democracy, the institutional safeguards and the evaluation belong to the owner. ❌ No contemporary instance is named here.

## Anchor 6 — 2021, `HXS-B-PHLY`, Q4(b) [15] ⚠️ weak fit, labelled

> ✅ "What are the main causes of female foeticide in India? Is it the result of demonic application of technology only? Discuss."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Gender Discrimination. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What this topic contributes:** only the structure of a multi-causal question. ⚠️ The stem's second half asks whether a single cause suffices, which is the general form of the reductionism problem. 🔶 Lesson 31's named reductionisms give the vocabulary for refusing a single-cause answer, and ✅ the chapter's conclusion that single-discipline approaches **cover a limited range of inquiry** and so **turn out to be partial approaches** states the principle.
- ⚠️ **Fit is weak and is marked weak:** the substantive causes are the owner's entirely, and a methodological clause is all that this topic can honestly supply. ❌ Do not import more. ❌ No statistic, law, programme or period is asserted here.

## Anchor 7 — 2020, `URC-B-PHLY`, Q4(c) [15] ⚠️ moderate fit

> ✅ "How far do the social and political regimes condition corrupt practices in a nation-state? Discuss."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Crime and Punishment. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What this topic contributes:** the verb **condition** is the stem's hinge, and it is a question about how social structure bears on political behaviour. ✅ The chapter's sociological apparatus is directly usable: institutions are **stabilized systems of expectations and actions**; political sociology applies the **analyses of bureaucratic structures developed in the works of Max Weber and Robert Michels** to **governmental agencies, trade unions, political parties, and even economic institutions**; and public decisions, **to be effective, must take into account the social motivations of relevant groups**, which requires inquiry into **value-orientations, attitudes, habits, beliefs and prejudices**.
- ❌ **Boundary:** the concept of corruption, its ethical and systemic analysis and the remedies belong to the owner. ❌ No country or institution is named here.

## Anchor 8 — 2019, `SDF-B-PHLY`, Q2(c) [15] ⚠️ weak fit, labelled

> ✅ "Does technological development lead to progress in the ethical standards of the society? Explain."

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Development and Social Progress. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What this topic contributes:** the stem crosses an empirical antecedent with a normative consequent, and the chapter's closing division of labour is the apparatus for handling that crossing. ✅ Scientific and behavioural studies are undertaken **to find facts and devise tools for achieving the goals of public policy**, whereas **these goals are largely determined with the help of political philosophy**. ⚠️ The chapter also supplies **level of technological development** as the first of the three Marxian factors, and its own caution that science **derives its validity from philosophy itself**.
- ⚠️ **Fit is weak and is marked weak:** the ethical substance belongs to the owner and to the Ethics subject. ❌ Do not expand the methodological clause into the body of the answer.

## Anchor 9 — 2018, `EGT-D-PHLY`, Q3(a) [20] ⚠️ moderate fit

> ✅ "What do you understand by multi-culturalism ? How are globalization and multi-culturalism related ? How does their relationship affect cultural changes ?"

- ✅ **Primary owner:** Humanism, Secularism and Multiculturalism. ❌ Not this topic.
- ⚠️ **What this topic contributes:** the second and third demands are relational and cross-disciplinary — an economic process, a cultural condition and a claimed effect on change. ✅ The chapter's sociological definitions supply the vocabulary: sociology as the study of **the behaviour of men in an associative process**; institutions as **stabilized systems of expectations and actions**; and political sociology's roster, which includes **political culture**, **political socialization**, **political consensus and cleavage** and the **social basis of political behaviour**. ⚠️ Lipset's claim that **politics will be different in caste and non-caste societies** is the chapter's own statement that cultural structure is a political variable.
- ❌ **Boundary:** the concept of multiculturalism, its normative defence and its critics belong to the owner; ➡️ globalisation's substantive treatment belongs to **Topic 12**.

## How to deploy this topic when it is not the subject

| ⚠️ Situation | ✅ Deployment |
|---|---|
| ⚠️ Stem asks whether a political form will **survive** or **be sustained** | ✅ Open with the evidence a sustainability claim requires; ⚠️ name three or four of the nine data items rather than all nine |
| ⚠️ Stem asks a **necessary/sufficient** question across two domains | ✅ State the reductionism risk by name, then use the **efficient and acceptable** double test |
| ⚠️ Stem concerns **propaganda, opinion or leadership appeal** | ✅ Route explicitly to political psychology and name its scope |
| ⚠️ Stem concerns **implementation or policy failure** | ✅ Use the five-item inquiry — value-orientations, attitudes, habits, beliefs, prejudices |
| ⚠️ Stem asks for the **relevance of a classical or historical doctrine** | ✅ Use the two contexts of history and the **rule and exception** test |
| ⚠️ Stem mixes an **empirical antecedent** with a **normative consequent** | ✅ Use the facts-and-tools against goals division in one clause |
| ⚠️ Stem is a **10-marker** | ⚠️ One methodological clause at most; ❌ the owner's content takes the rest |

---

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

⚠️ Six cumulative checks. ⚠️ Each requires material from at least three lessons, which is the skill an examiner is actually testing when a stem spans the chapter.

---

## Check A — the three definitions that do all the work

**Prompt:** The chapter defines a discipline, a social science and the interdisciplinary approach in a single page. Show how the third is derived from the first two, and identify the one limb of the third definition that the derivation makes indispensable.

**Worked response:** ✅ A **discipline** is a **branch of learning, a field or subject of study** — a wide term, illustrated by nine fields running from history to botany. ✅ The **social sciences** are then narrowed to the **systematic studies of the institutions and functioning of human society, man's behaviour in society and the interpersonal relationships of individuals as members of society**. ⚠️ The narrowing does the derivation: if several disciplines study the same three objects, they face **common problems** and treat **different aspects of the same or similar phenomena**, which is exactly the condition the chapter states for the interdisciplinary approach to apply. ✅ Hence the social sciences are the group for which the approach is **most suitable**. 🔑 The indispensable limb is the second: the approach is also constituted by a tendency to **contribute to** more than one discipline, and without that limb the third implication — a broader perspective **with a view to making contribution to the other social sciences** — would have no definitional footing, and the conclusion's demand for a **suitable contribution** would be unsupported.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not treat the opening page as preliminary. ⚠️ Every later move, including the refusal of merger, is drawn from it.

---

## Check B — the four forces that pushed political science outward

**Prompt:** The chapter gives four distinct reasons why political science came to need other disciplines. Name them, say which is logically strongest, and show where each appears.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **One**, the **openness** of the political system: once it is defined as **an 'open system' or a system of interactions** receiving **inputs** and returning **outputs** through **feedback**, the interdisciplinary approach **becomes imperative**. ⚠️ **Two**, the **empirical requisites**: observable facts and data, explanation through correlation and cause-and-effect and generalization, and prediction where the result could be **predicted and verified** — none of which after the first can be met from political material alone. ⚠️ **Three**, the **behavioural turn**: political analysis became an analysis of the behaviour of the electorate, legislators, bureaucrats and executive and judicial authorities, and of groups **whether they belonged to particular structures of the government or were outside it**, which is why **Eulau** held that political behaviour analysis **must be interdisciplinary**. ⚠️ **Four**, **systems analysis**: the political system is **a sub-system within the ambit of the larger social system** and **does not operate independently or in isolation**. 🔑 The strongest is the first, because it is the premise from which three and four follow — Eulau's argument is openness at the level of the person, and the subsystem claim is openness at the level of the system.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not present these as four independent arguments. ⚠️ Presenting them as one argument stated at four levels is both more accurate and more impressive.

---

## Check C — the ladder of borrowing, from a number to an approach

**Prompt:** Arrange everything political science borrows, on the chapter's own account, in order of increasing commitment, and give a named instance of each rung.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **Rung 1 — methodology.** ✅ Political science turned to its neighbours **not only to learn their methodology, but also** for their data, theories and models; methodology is first in the chapter's own sentence because the empirical method **was first developed in other social sciences**. ⚠️ **Rung 2 — data.** ✅ The nine-item roster for judging whether a form of government **will be sustained by a given community**, routed by the three mappings — socialization and culture to **psychology and sociology**, development to **sociology and economics**, communication to **social psychology**. ⚠️ **Rung 3 — theories.** ✅ **Elite theory**, from **Pareto, Mosca and Michels** in sociology; the **base and superstructure** framework, from **economics and sociology**. ⚠️ **Rung 4 — concepts.** ✅ **Easton's** political system, derived from the sociological **social system**. ⚠️ **Rung 5 — whole approaches.** ✅ Structural-functionalism, from **cultural anthropology** via **Parsons** to **Almond and Powell**. 🔑 Commitment rises because a datum can be re-described, a theory can be rejected, but an approach supplies the criteria of selection and therefore decides what the enquiry can see.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not treat the rungs as a chronology. ⚠️ They are degrees of dependence, and the chapter's narrative order happens to run the same way.

---

## Check D — the seven partial approaches, and what the chapter owes each of them

**Prompt:** List the seven partial approaches in the chapter's own order and state, for each, whether the chapter expounds it, prepares it or merely names it.

**Worked response:** ✅ The order is **philosophical, historical, legal, economic, sociological, psychological and geographical**. ✅ **Philosophical** is expounded at pp.145-146 with its own box. ✅ **Historical** is expounded at p.141. ❌ **Legal** is named only in the closing sentence; ⚠️ the chapter supplies no section, though its p.138 objection to accounts framed **merely in terms of formal institutions which may operate differently in actual practice** states the limit of a purely legal reading. ✅ **Economic** is expounded at pp.142-143 with its own box. ✅ **Sociological** is expounded at pp.143-144 with its own box. ✅ **Psychological** is expounded at pp.144-145 with its own box. ⚠️ **Geographical** is named only in the closing sentence, but is **prepared** twice — **human geography** is placed among the social sciences at p.136, and the **rural-urban ratio** appears in the data roster at p.139. 🔑 Five expounded, one prepared-and-named, one named with its limit implied elsewhere.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not write six. ⚠️ **Geographical** is in the list, and one of this topic's own canonical owners omits it.

---

## Check E — the four hyphenated fields, and the pattern they share

**Prompt:** The chapter defines four hyphenated fields in boxes. Name them, state the formula that generates each, and identify the structural feature every one of them shares.

**Worked response:** ✅ **Political economy**, **political sociology**, **political psychology** and **political philosophy**. ⚠️ The generating formula is the same each time: one discipline's concepts and methods are applied to politics, as the chapter says explicitly of political sociology — **the application of various concepts and methods of sociology to the study of political behaviour and institutions has given rise to a new venture**. ⚠️ The shared structural feature is a **two-way limb**. ✅ Political economy's issues are **impact of economic factors on political life; impact of political decisions on economic life**. ✅ Political sociology **also examines the impact of political domination and decisions on social life**. ⚠️ Political psychology's two-way character appears historically, since the **growth of totalitarian political movements in the thirties and forties** set a psychological research agenda. ✅ Political philosophy both **makes use of the conclusions of empirical data** and determines the goals that empirical study then devises tools to achieve. 🔑 That consistency is what licenses the conclusion's demand for a **suitable contribution** back to the donor disciplines.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not describe any of the four as a sub-branch of political science. ⚠️ Political psychology is explicitly **a branch of social psychology**, and political sociology **thrives on the contributions from sociology as well as political science**.

---

## Check F — the chapter's three verdicts on autonomy, and the single weakness they share

**Prompt:** Assemble the chapter's three defences of political science's independent identity and state the one assumption all three rely on.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **Defence one, the purposefulness lock.** ✅ Expanding the scope must be **purposeful and well-conceived**; in entering another sphere **we should not deviate from our main focus**; and reference to economics, sociology, psychology or anthropology must carry **the definite purpose of understanding the problems of politics, not at random**, with **labour-management relations** and the **psychology of crowd behaviour** as the two worked tests. ⚠️ **Defence two, the independence claim in the history section.** ✅ The **expanding horizons** of political science have given it an **independent status and identity**, and it uses historical material **only selectively, although frequently**. ⚠️ **Defence three, the refusal of merger.** ✅ The approach **does not seek to merge all social sciences into a single 'master science'**, requiring instead that results **in any sphere** be **based on and verified from** all relevant social sciences, with a **suitable contribution** in return. 🔑 All three rely on **focus** — on the political question being the organising aim of the enquiry. ⚠️ The chapter never defines focus independently of the researcher's purpose, so the autonomy it secures is procedural rather than substantive, and that is the single point at which every critic of the chapter should press.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not present the autonomy defence as settled. ⚠️ Saying that it is coherent, argued and not fully secured is both more accurate and more impressive than asserting that the chapter has proved it.

---

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ Six original questions — two at each weight — distinct from the thirty-one lesson-local questions above. ⚠️ Ceilings observed: **10-mark models within 150 words; 15- and 20-mark models within 250 words.** ⚠️ Each rubric is quantified and unique.

---

## Question 1 [10 marks]

**Question:** "The interdisciplinary approach widens the scope of political science without widening its subject." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ The chapter supports the distinction. ✅ Scope widens because the political system is an **open system** whose outcomes depend on its environment, so analysis must reach the **economic, cultural, psychological and sociological aspects of the social organization**. ⚠️ Subject does not widen, because the governing question remains political: expansion must be **purposeful and well-conceived**, reference to other disciplines must carry **the definite purpose of understanding the problems of politics, not at random**, and **labour-management relations** or **crowd psychology** may be studied **only to analyse their impact on politics**. ✅ The conclusion seals the distinction by refusing to **merge all social sciences into a single 'master science'** while requiring results to be **based on and verified from** all relevant social sciences. 🔑 Wider evidence, same question. ⚠️ The distinction nonetheless rests on focus, which the chapter never defines independently of purpose.

**Rubric [10]:** the openness ground for widened scope, with at least two of the four aspects **2** · the purposefulness lock quoted or closely reported **2** · at least one of the two worked tests **1** · the refusal of merger **2** · the based-on-and-verified-from requirement **2** · the residual about focus **1**.

---

## Question 2 [10 marks]

**Question:** Discuss what the chapter means by calling an approach "partial". *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ Partiality is defined by range, not by error: some approaches **cover a limited range of inquiry by relying on a single discipline**, and on that ground the **philosophical, historical, legal, economic, sociological, psychological and geographical** approaches **turn out to be partial approaches**. ⚠️ Partial therefore means incomplete, not false — each remains valid for the questions its single discipline can reach. ⚠️ The practical risk is that partiality hardens into reduction, where one contribution is promoted from a source of explanation to the only source: economic reduction loses legitimacy and identity, psychological reduction loses structural constraint, legal reduction loses informal power. ✅ The remedy is not to rank the lenses but to rely on **all other relevant disciplines** for the purpose of **evolving an integrated view of the subject**. 🔑 Seven lenses, each true in its range and none sufficient alone.

**Rubric [10]:** the limited-range definition in the chapter's terms **2** · all seven approaches named **3** · the incomplete-not-false clarification **2** · at least two reduction risks with what each loses **2** · the integrated-view remedy **1**.

---

## Question 3 [15 marks]

**Question:** "Political science borrowed its methods before it borrowed its facts." Critically examine this claim with reference to the development of the interdisciplinary approach. *(Critically examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ The chapter's own sequence supports the claim. ✅ Anthropology, sociology, economics and psychology **spun off from their parent discipline—philosophy—towards the end of the nineteenth century because of the advent of empirical orientation**, while **political science, in this respect, was a relatively late-starter**. ✅ Consequently, since the empirical method **was first developed in other social sciences**, political science was attracted to them **not only to learn their methodology, but also to find out how far their data, theories and models could help**. ⚠️ Methodology stands first in that sentence, and the chapter's subsequent order follows it exactly: data, then theories and models, then the five discipline-by-discipline uses.

⚠️ Two qualifications make the claim critical, not merely descriptive. ⚠️ First, the borrowing did not begin with political science reaching out: early sociologists **both in Europe and America** had already turned to political analysis, and **Arthur Bentley (1870-1957)**, trained in Europe, became **a major intellectual force in political science in America**. ⚠️ Second, the deepest imports were not methods but whole **approaches** — **Easton's** political system derived from the sociological **social system**, and structural-functionalism relayed from **Malinowski and Radcliffe-Brown** through **Talcott Parsons** to **Almond and Powell** — which supply criteria of selection and therefore shape what counts as a fact.

🔑 The claim is right in its order and understates its significance: what arrived first was not a technique but a way of deciding what to look at. ⚠️ The chapter's defence of identity consequently rests on focus, not apparatus.

**Rubric [15]:** the spin-off claim with its four disciplines and dating **2** · the late-starter verdict **1** · the methodology-first sentence quoted or closely reported **3** · the chapter's own subsequent order used as corroboration **2** · the counter-point that sociologists arrived first, with a named instance **3** · the approaches-not-merely-methods qualification with at least one named transfer **3** · a graded conclusion **1**.

---

## Question 4 [15 marks]

**Question:** Examine how the chapter uses history both as evidence for political theory and as a discipline converging with it. *(Examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ As evidence, history is **a rich storehouse of data** serving three purposes: **understanding and interpretation of political phenomena**, **a comparative study of political situations**, and **verification of hypotheses about politics**. ⚠️ The third is decisive: a source that can confirm can also refute. ✅ A stronger use follows: **when history gives us not only an account of events but also cause-and-effect relationships, or rather, laws of historical development (such as Marx's materialist interpretation of history)**, that knowledge illuminates **the course of politics in a given situation**. ⚠️ The second use treats history as theory rather than evidence; the contested status of historical laws is examined where approaches to the study of politics are owned.

✅ Political science is now **deeply concerned with the socio-economic foundations of political phenomena**, with **a corresponding change in the outlook of modern historians**; the chapter calls this a **simultaneous change of outlook** which has **strengthened the age-old relationship**. ✅ Neither is confined to the **'great' acts of kings and princes**, and both attend to the **condition and role of the masses in the making of history as well as the shaping of politics**.

✅ Hence the demand: political science **cannot claim to evolve a foolproof theory unless it is substantiated and verified by historical data, both in terms of rule and exception**, since theory from **mere speculation, visionary perspective or limited observation** will **crumble**. ⚠️ Exceptions separate verification from illustration. 🔑 History tests what political theory asserts, and now asks its questions.

**Rubric [15]:** limb (a) with all three internal uses **3** · limb (b) with its upgrade and named instance **2** · the two-way convergence with the word *simultaneous* **3** · the condition-and-role formulation **2** · the rule-and-exception requirement **2** · all three failure modes **2** · the distinction between verification and illustration **1**.

---

## Question 5 [20 marks]

**Question:** "Interdisciplinarity enlarges political science's evidence while endangering its identity." Critically evaluate with reference to the chapter's argument and its unresolved residues. *(Critically evaluate — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ The enlargement is undeniable. ✅ Political science borrows methodology, then **data, theories and models**; the data roster for judging whether a form of government **will be sustained by a given community** runs from the **rate of economic growth** to the **rural-urban ratio** without a single political item; and whole approaches arrive from outside, **Easton's** political system from the sociological **social system**, and structural-functionalism from **Malinowski and Radcliffe-Brown** through **Parsons** to **Almond and Powell**.

⚠️ The danger is equally real: some imports deny the importer's primacy — **elite theory** holds that a small group dominates **irrespective of its forms of social and political institutions**, and **base and superstructure** holds that politics is shaped by the **prevailing mode of material production**.

✅ The chapter's three defences are the purposefulness lock — borrowing must be **purposeful and well-conceived**, with **the definite purpose of understanding the problems of politics, not at random**; the counter-intuitive claim that the **expanding horizons** of political science have given it an **independent status and identity**; and the refusal to **merge all social sciences into a single 'master science'**, replaced by the requirement that results **in any sphere** be **based on and verified from** all relevant social sciences, with a **suitable contribution** in return.

⚠️ Two residues remain. ⚠️ All three rest on **focus**, never defined independently of the researcher's purpose; and an approach, by supplying criteria of selection, partly determines which questions can be asked. 🔑 The identity secured is procedural and real, but not proved.

**Rubric [20]:** the enlargement evidenced across methodology, data and approaches, with at least two named transfers **4** · the specific danger stated through at least one hostile import, quoted **3** · the purposefulness lock **3** · the expanding-horizons independence claim **3** · the refusal of merger with the two-way requirement **3** · the focus residue identified **2** · the criteria-of-selection residue identified **1** · a graded verdict that neither affirms nor dismisses **1**.

---

## Question 6 [20 marks]

**Question:** Evaluate the chapter's division of labour between empirical political science and political philosophy, and assess whether it survives its own evidence. *(Evaluate — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ✅ The division is stated exactly: scientific and behavioural studies are undertaken **to find facts and devise tools for achieving the goals of public policy whereas these goals are largely determined with the help of political philosophy**. ⚠️ Two hedges are built in — **largely**, and **with the help of** — so philosophy assists, not monopolises.

✅ It has a history. ⚠️ A **growing craze** for scientific method made it **fashionable** to separate political science from political philosophy, and **behaviouralism in the 1950s and 1960s was particularly responsible for this attitude**; but **since early 1970s**, with **post-behaviouralism** and the revival of interest in the **theory of justice** and related principles — **liberty, equality, community** — political philosophy received a **renewed prominence**.

✅ Its deeper ground is logical. ⚠️ Although science uses the **empirical method** and philosophy the **transcendental method**, **science itself is the product of philosophy**: it owes philosophy **origin** and **validity**, as **Auguste Comte (1798-1857)** held in tracing science's foundation to **'positive philosophy'**.

⚠️ It survives its own evidence only in one direction. ⚠️ The box has political philosophy using **philosophical and historical method** to determine the goals of public life, so goal-determination is not purely a priori — a point the chapter absorbs. ⚠️ It does not address the reverse pressure: selecting facts and designing tools embodies evaluative commitments, and political psychology **may also be used for influencing political behaviour of a group**. 🔑 The division holds as a distinction between questions, not as a partition of value.

**Rubric [20]:** the division quoted with both hedges **4** · the dated eclipse and revival with the four revived principles **4** · the origin-and-validity ground, with Comte and positive philosophy **4** · the historical-method clause from the box used as a qualification **3** · the reverse pressure identified, with the influencing clause or an equivalent **3** · a verdict that distinguishes questions from values **2**.

---

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ Sixty-seven errors, grouped by where they arise. ⚠️ Each row states the error as a candidate would actually commit it, and the correction in the chapter's own terms wherever the chapter supplies them.

## Set 1 — definitions and taxonomy (Lessons 1-3)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | ❌ "A discipline is a subject." | ✅ A **branch of learning, a field or subject of study** — three synonyms, illustrated by nine fields spanning history to botany |
| 2 | ❌ "Social sciences study society." | ✅ Three limbs: **institutions and functioning of human society**; **man's behaviour in society**; **interpersonal relationships of individuals as members of society** |
| 3 | ❌ "Gauba lists history among the social sciences." | ❌ He does not. ✅ The five named are political science, economics, sociology, **social** psychology, **cultural** anthropology; ⚠️ history is named as a discipline and the omission is unexplained |
| 4 | ❌ "Psychology is a social science for Gauba." | ✅ **Social** psychology is; ❌ **clinical** psychology is placed with the physical sciences |
| 5 | ❌ "Geography is not a social science." | ⚠️ Half-true. ✅ **Physical** geography belongs to the physical sciences; ✅ **human** geography **obviously falls in the domain of social sciences** |
| 6 | ❌ "Anthropology is a social science." | ⚠️ **Social or cultural** anthropology is; ❌ **physical** anthropology is not |
| 7 | ❌ "Interdisciplinarity means using more than one discipline." | ⚠️ Limb 1 only. ✅ Limb 2 adds a tendency to **draw on or contribute to** more than one discipline, and contribution alone suffices |
| 8 | ❌ "The approach applies wherever two subjects are related." | ✅ The condition is **common problems** or **different aspects of the same or similar phenomena** |
| 9 | ❌ "It gives a wider view." | ⚠️ Three distinct gains: **broader** perspective, understanding **more thoroughly**, understanding **more fruitfully** |
| 10 | ❌ "Interdisciplinarity is possible only in the social sciences." | ✅ The chapter says the social sciences are the group where it is **most suitable** — ❌ not the only group where it is available |

## Set 2 — implications and the purposefulness lock (Lessons 4-5)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 11 | ❌ "Political science uses other disciplines' findings and data — that is implication (a) and (b)." | ⚠️ They are different operations: (a) **makes use of findings, theories and models**; (b) **verifies** political theories from **data and theories**; one builds, the other tests |
| 12 | ❌ "Implication (c) is a courtesy." | ✅ It is the operational form of limb 2 and is restated in the conclusion as a **suitable contribution** |
| 13 | ❌ "Models can be used to verify a political theory." | ⚠️ The chapter lists **models** under (a) and not under (b); ⚠️ a model organises an explanation rather than supplying a test |
| 14 | ❌ "Gauba permits unrestricted borrowing." | ✅ Expansion must be **purposeful and well-conceived**, and **we should not deviate from our main focus** |
| 15 | ❌ "Only economics, sociology and psychology are named in the warning." | ✅ Four are named, including **anthropology** |
| 16 | ❌ "The two tests are the same point twice." | ⚠️ **Labour-management relations** closes the structural door; the **psychology of crowd behaviour** closes the behavioural one |
| 17 | ❌ "Political scientists must not contribute to other disciplines." | ❌ The opposite: the lock restricts the enquiry's purpose, ✅ while implication (c) requires contribution |
| 18 | ❌ "The purposefulness rule is a testable criterion." | ⚠️ It is a norm of purpose; ❌ the chapter supplies no external test |

## Set 3 — genesis (Lessons 6-7)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 19 | ❌ "Interdisciplinarity is entirely modern." | ✅ A **rudimentary form** is traceable in the traditional study of politics |
| 20 | ❌ "Plato and Aristotle were interdisciplinary political scientists." | ✅ Their studies were **not marked by a deeper analysis of the forces shaping the course of events** |
| 21 | ❌ "Plato linked education to politics." | ⚠️ Two variables: the **structure of the family** **and** the **nature of education**, affecting the character and activities of office-holders |
| 22 | ❌ "Aristotle linked wealth to regime type." | ⚠️ **Wealth and status**, and the chapter's verb is **demonstrated a causal relation** |
| 23 | ❌ "Gauba says Marx and Engels were the first." | ✅ **Probably the first** — carry the hedge |
| 24 | ❌ "They founded interdisciplinary political science." | ✅ A new approach **in social sciences**, ❌ not in political science |
| 25 | ❌ "They located politics in production and class." | ⚠️ Three factors: **level of technological development**, **mode of material production**, **prevailing class structure** |
| 26 | ❌ "Interdisciplinarity is a Marxist doctrine." | ✅ **Modern liberal political scientists have persistently** pursued the same requirement, and most of the chapter's evidence is drawn from them |

## Set 4 — the empirical turn (Lessons 8-14)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 27 | ❌ "Interdisciplinarity is useful once politics is seen as a social process." | ✅ The chapter's word is **imperative**, and the conclusion is conditional on two premises |
| 28 | ❌ "The open system means society determines politics." | ⚠️ Two arrows: the relevant aspects **affect** politics **and are affected by it** |
| 29 | ❌ "The empirical approach means using facts." | ✅ Three requisites: **observable facts and data**; **explanation** through correlation, cause-and-effect and generalizations; **prediction**, where the result could be **predicted and verified** |
| 30 | ❌ "Political science has always been empirical since Aristotle." | ✅ Empirical orientation is **relatively recent** and political science was **a relatively late-starter** |
| 31 | ❌ "Master science meant politics was the most important subject." | ✅ The gloss is **to demonstrate its crucial role in the ordering of various relationships within human society** |
| 32 | ❌ "Traditional political science was unscientific superstition." | ✅ It was **founded on ethics or moral philosophy**, and the chapter later holds science to be **the product of philosophy** |
| 33 | ❌ "The social sciences developed independently of one another." | ✅ Anthropology, sociology, economics and psychology **spun off from their parent discipline—philosophy—towards the end of the nineteenth century** |
| 34 | ❌ "Political science borrowed facts first." | ✅ **Not only to learn their methodology, but also** for data, theories and models — methodology is first |
| 35 | ❌ "The empirical turn rejected institutions." | ⚠️ It rejected accounts framed **merely** in terms of formal institutions; ⚠️ the qualifier is essential |
| 36 | ❌ "Political behaviour means voting behaviour." | ✅ The electorate, **legislators, bureaucrats, executive and judicial authorities**, and groups **whether they belonged to particular structures of the government or were outside it**, and **the elite** |
| 37 | ❌ "Eulau defined behaviouralism." | ⚠️ He argued that **political behaviour analysis must be interdisciplinary**, in his article on **'Political Behaviour'** in the *International Encyclopaedia of the Social Sciences* (**1968**) |
| 38 | ❌ "The three organs are passive transmitters of social demand." | ⚠️ The denial is of autarky; ✅ their decisions **in turn have their impact on the social system** |

## Set 5 — data and models (Lessons 15-19)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 39 | ❌ "Political science borrows data for convenience." | ✅ The premise is that the approach treats **society as a totality whose various aspects are closely interrelated** |
| 40 | ❌ "The data list has six items." | ✅ Nine, ending with **etc.**; ⚠️ growth, educational levels and professional distribution are the ones usually dropped |
| 41 | ❌ "Political communication draws on psychology." | ✅ On **social psychology** — the qualifier is the chapter's taxonomy in operation |
| 42 | ❌ "Lipset said caste affects politics in India." | ✅ He said **politics will be different in caste and non-caste societies**, a comparative claim; ❌ no country-specific assertion follows |
| 43 | ❌ "Elite theory says elites rule." | ✅ In **every society**, **irrespective of its forms of social and political institutions**, a **small group of the chosen few always assumes a dominant position** |
| 44 | ❌ "Elite theory came from political science." | ✅ From **sociology**, through **Pareto (1848-1923)**, **Mosca (1858-1941)** and **Michels (1876-1936)** |
| 45 | ❌ "Base and superstructure shapes politics." | ✅ It shapes **politics, culture, morals, ideas and institutions**, and was evolved in **economics and sociology** |
| 46 | ❌ "Structural-functionalism came from sociology." | ✅ From **cultural anthropology** — **Malinowski** and **Radcliffe-Brown** — then **sociology** via **Talcott Parsons**, then political science via **Almond and Powell** |
| 47 | ❌ "Easton's political system is a political-science invention." | ✅ It is **derived from the concept of the social system originally developed in the field of sociology** |
| 48 | ❌ "Lasswell's problem-solving model identifies problems, generates alternatives and evaluates outcomes." | 🔶 That gloss is owner-supplied; ✅ the chapter says only that the model **was derived from psychology** |
| 49 | ❌ "Bargaining comes from economics." | ⚠️ More precisely from the **theories of competition**, specifically **perfect competition** and **monopolistic competition** |
| 50 | ❌ "Nobody applied market models to democracy." | ✅ **Schumpeter and Anthony Downs have sought to analyse democratic politics using the economist's model of the open market** |

## Set 6 — the five uses (Lessons 20-29)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 51 | ❌ "History provides background." | ✅ Three uses in limb (a): **understanding and interpretation**, **comparative study of political situations**, **verification of hypotheses about politics** |
| 52 | ❌ "Freeman said history has no root without politics." | ❌ Reversed. ✅ **Seeley (1834-95)** wrote the root-and-fruit sentence; ✅ **Freeman (1823-92)** wrote **history is past politics, politics is present history** |
| 53 | ❌ "Gauba rejects the relevance of history." | ✅ He rejects the aphorisms as **oversimplification**, while requiring that **any study of the Indian political system must start with its historical antecedents** |
| 54 | ❌ "Interdisciplinarity weakened political science's identity." | ✅ The **expanding horizons** of political science **have given it an independent status and identity** |
| 55 | ❌ "Political science and history converged because political science borrowed." | ✅ The chapter calls it a **simultaneous change of outlook** |
| 56 | ❌ "Verification means finding supporting cases." | ✅ **Both in terms of rule and exception**; ⚠️ exceptions are what separate verification from illustration |
| 57 | ❌ "Economics provides facts about the economy to politics." | ⚠️ And the reverse: economic decisions are **greatly affected by 'non-economic factors', such as cultural values, personality, political needs, and considerations of status** |
| 58 | ❌ "Economics separated from philosophy in the late nineteenth century." | ⚠️ Two separations. ✅ From **philosophy** for methodological reasons at p.138; ✅ from **political theory** under **laissez-faire** at p.142 |
| 59 | ❌ "Gauba says the welfare state guarantees security." | ⚠️ It **claims to**; ✅ and the chapter names three — **welfare state**, **service state**, socialist state — with four guarantees |
| 60 | ❌ "Gauba says all politics is shaped by economic forces." | ⚠️ He reports it: **according to Marxian theory, too** — his own conclusion is that an **adequate knowledge of economics becomes essential** |
| 61 | ❌ "Political economy studies the politics of the economy." | ✅ Its test is that a social policy prove **not only economically efficient but also politically acceptable**; ✅ and traditional political economy concerned the **revenue and expenditure of the state** |
| 62 | ❌ "Institutions are rules and offices." | ✅ **Stabilized systems of expectations and actions** |
| 63 | ❌ "Political sociology is a branch of sociology." | ✅ It **thrives on the contributions from sociology as well as political science**, and its boxed definition includes the reverse limb — **the impact of political domination and decisions on social life** |
| 64 | ❌ "Behaviouralism ended political philosophy." | ⚠️ It made the distinction **fashionable**; ✅ **since early 1970s** political philosophy received **renewed prominence**, and ✅ empirical studies **find facts and devise tools** while **goals are largely determined with the help of political philosophy** |

## Set 7 — conclusion and answer architecture (Lessons 30-31)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 65 | ❌ "The chapter develops six partial approaches." | ✅ It expounds five disciplinary uses and concludes by naming **seven partial approaches**, adding legal and geographical approaches without separate treatment |
| 66 | ❌ "Integration means merging the social sciences." | ✅ The chapter requires reciprocal borrowing and verification while expressly refusing merger into a single **master science** |
| 67 | ❌ "Reductionism is one general error." | 🔶 Name the specific risk created by the borrowed lens — economic, sociological, psychological or historical reductionism — and preserve political science's governing question |

                        |
        +---------------+----------------+
        |                                |
   WHAT MUST BE                   WHAT CONSTRAINS

        |                                |

## Map 6 — The objection-and-reply spine of the whole topic

| ⚠️ Objection | ✅ The chapter's reply | ⚠️ What remains unresolved |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ The social-science definition is so broad that interdisciplinarity is trivially true | ✅ Availability is argued from the definition; **imperativeness** is argued separately from the open-system premise | ⚠️ The definition is a working map, not a demarcation test |
| ⚠️ Limb 2 admits almost everything | ✅ The purposefulness lock narrows it | ⚠️ Decorative borrowing has no test |
| ⚠️ Cross-disciplinary verification equivocates between concepts | ✅ Purposeful borrowing; testing by **rule and exception** | ⚠️ Commensurability is never named |
| ⚠️ The openness argument dissolves every discipline, not only politics | ✅ The chapter accepts the symmetry: results **in any sphere** must be verified from all relevant social sciences | ⚠️ Identity then rests on focus, which rests on purpose |
| ⚠️ Importing models hostile to political autonomy is self-defeating | ✅ Model and thesis are separable; the import becomes a hypothesis, not a constitution | ⚠️ A model carries its donor's ontology and shapes what can be asked |
| ⚠️ The market analogy misdescribes coercive, indivisible, exit-less politics | ⚠️ The chapter reports rather than endorses; **monopolistic** competition is also named; the purposefulness lock applies | ⚠️ No method is offered for detecting model-induced distortion |
| ⚠️ Freeman's identity thesis is close to what the chapter itself requires of history | ✅ The chapter denies identity, not importance: **selectively, although frequently** | ⚠️ Seeley's weaker dependence thesis arguably survives the verdict |
| ⚠️ The rule-and-exception test cannot be met with few, non-independent cases | ✅ It is a necessary condition, and better than the three named failure modes | ⚠️ Incommensurable historical description is untouched |
| ⚠️ Sociology's near-universal domain makes political science a branch | ✅ Domain is not discipline; focus individuates | ⚠️ Political sociology's shared roster blurs the boundary the reply needs |
| ⚠️ Political psychology can be used to influence groups | ⚠️ Assembled reply: accurate description; purposefulness; and the facts-and-tools against goals division | ❌ The chapter never connects the clause to its own division of labour |
| ⚠️ The facts-and-tools division collapses from both sides | ✅ Goals are **largely** determined **with the help of** philosophy, which uses **philosophical and historical method** | ⚠️ The evaluative content of fact-selection and tool-design is not addressed |
| ⚠️ Verified-from-all-relevant-social-sciences is a master science under another name | ✅ Merger would mean one set of concepts, one method, one agenda; integration keeps separate questions and demands a contribution back | ⚠️ Autonomy by focus is procedural, not substantive |

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

⚠️ Compressed recall for the whole topic, organised by the chapter's own structure rather than by a generic template. ⚠️ Every lesson is represented. ❌ No teaching prose is repeated; what follows is what has to be recoverable under examination conditions.

## Source identity and the three facts about it

- ✅ Gauba, *An Introduction to Political Theory*, Fifth Edition **2009**, Chapter **5**, titled **Interdisciplinary Perspective on Political Science**. ⚠️ The repository topic title, *Interdisciplinary Political Analysis*, is a cataloguing label.
- ✅ Range: PDF pp.**136-146** = printed pp.**121-131**; uniform offset **15**.
- ⚠️ Owners print pp.135-146; ❌ PDF p.135 (folio 120) is Chapter 4 substance and nothing is cited from it.
- ⚠️ Chapter 4's offset differed; ❌ do not carry a sibling chapter's offset.

## Vocabulary established before any argument

- ✅ **Discipline** = a branch of learning, a field or subject of study. ✅ Nine examples: history, political science, economics, linguistics, anthropology, physics, chemistry, mathematics, botany.
- ✅ **Social sciences** = systematic studies of (i) the institutions and functioning of human society, (ii) man's behaviour in society, (iii) the interpersonal relationships of individuals as members of society. ✅ Five examples: political science, economics, sociology, **social** psychology, **cultural** anthropology.
- ✅ **Straddling disciplines**: physical geography, physical anthropology and clinical psychology on the physical side; human geography, social or cultural anthropology and social psychology on the social side.
- 🔑 The object does not decide the family; the question does.

## The approach: definition, condition, gain

- ✅ **Two limbs:** reliance on more than one discipline for a given problem, **or** a tendency to **draw on or contribute to** more than one discipline.
- ✅ **Condition:** disciplines dealing with **common problems** or with **different aspects of the same or similar phenomena**; social sciences are the group where it is **most suitable**.
- ✅ **Gain:** a **broader** perspective; understanding **more thoroughly**; understanding **more fruitfully**.
- ✅ **Hinge:** **the study of politics as a social process provides ample scope**.

## The three implications

- ✅ **(a)** make use of the **findings, theories and models** of other social sciences in understanding a political phenomenon.
- ✅ **(b)** **verify** the theories and findings of political science from the **data and theories** of other social sciences.
- ✅ **(c)** develop a broader perspective about political life **with a view to making contribution to the other social sciences**.
- 🔑 (a) builds, (b) tests, (c) exports. ⚠️ Models appear under (a) only.

## The purposefulness lock

- ✅ Expansion must be **purposeful and well-conceived**; **we should not deviate from our main focus**.
- ✅ Reference to **economics, sociology, psychology or anthropology** must carry **the definite purpose of understanding the problems of politics, not at random**.
- ✅ Two tests: **labour-management relations**; **the psychology of crowd behaviour** — ✅ studied **only to analyse their impact on politics**, ❌ not **for their own sake**.
- ⚠️ Structural door and behavioural door, closed by one test each.

## Genesis

- ✅ A **recent trend**, traceable in **rudimentary form** in the traditional study of politics.
- ✅ **Plato**: the **structure of the family** and the **nature of education** deeply affect the character and activities of office-holders.
- ✅ **Aristotle**: **demonstrated a causal relation** between the distribution of **wealth and status** and the type of **political regime**.
- ✅ Verdict: those studies were **not marked by a deeper analysis of the forces shaping the course of events**.
- ✅ **Marx (1818-83)** and **Engels (1820-95)**, around the **middle of the nineteenth century**, **probably the first** systematic interdisciplinary approach **in social sciences**; three factors — **level of technological development**, **mode of material production**, **prevailing class structure** — as the **primary source of political behaviour**.
- ✅ **Modern liberal political scientists have persistently** pursued the same requirement.

## Why the widening is imperative

- ✅ When politics is studied as a **social process** and the political system defined as an **open system** or **system of interactions**, the approach **becomes imperative**.
- ✅ Mechanism: **inputs** from the social system or environment → **outputs** → re-entry through the **feedback** channel.
- ✅ The widening must cover aspects that **affect** politics **and are affected by it**.

## The empirical turn, in five moves

1. ✅ **Empirical method** = reliance on **factual information, observation or direct sense-experience** as opposed to **pure speculation**.
2. ✅ Politics is **one of the oldest disciplines of the Western world**; Aristotle's **master science** was so named **to demonstrate its crucial role in the ordering of various relationships within human society**; but the traditional study was **characterized by its close link with philosophy**.
3. ✅ Anthropology, sociology, economics and psychology **spun off from their parent discipline—philosophy—towards the end of the nineteenth century**; political science was **a relatively late-starter**.
4. ✅ Traditional political science was **founded on ethics or moral philosophy**; its **chief problem** was to **determine norms of public life, and to evolve institutions according to those norms**. ✅ Empirical = facts, descriptive, the real, **what is there**; normative = values, prescriptive, the ideal, **what ought to be done**. ✅ Three requisites: **(a)** observable facts and data; **(b)** explanation — correlation, cause-and-effect, possible generalizations; **(c)** prediction — **predicted and verified**.
5. ✅ Rejected: **images of an ideal state**, and **merely** formal institutions **which may operate differently in actual practice**. ✅ Replaced by politics as **a process which takes place within the larger framework of the social system**. ✅ Political science turned to its neighbours **not only to learn their methodology, but also** for **data, theories and models**.

## Behaviour and systems

- ✅ Participants: the **electorate, legislators, bureaucrats, executive and judicial authorities**; individuals; **groups of various types whether they belonged to particular structures of the government or were outside it**; **the elite**.
- ✅ **Heinz Eulau**, 'Political Behaviour', *International Encyclopaedia of the Social Sciences* (**1968**): political behaviour is **only one aspect of his total behaviour as a social being**, so its analysis **must be interdisciplinary**, weighing **social, cultural and personal** factors.
- ✅ Systems analysis **gave an impetus**; the political system is **a sub-system within the ambit of the larger social system** and **does not operate independently or in isolation**.
- ✅ The **legislature, the executive and the judiciary do not produce their decisions on their own initiative or at their own will**, but **in response to the demands emanating from the social system**, and those decisions **in turn have their impact on the social system**.
- ✅ Four aspects to be understood: **economic, cultural, psychological and sociological**.

## Data borrowed

- ✅ Premise: the approach treats **society as a totality whose various aspects are closely interrelated**.
- ✅ Question: whether a particular form of government, **such as democracy**, **will be sustained by a given community**.
- ✅ Nine items: **rate of economic growth** · **distribution of national income** · **patterns of ownership of material wealth** · **educational levels** · **percentage of literacy** · **religious and cultural attitudes** · **family structures** · **professional distribution** · **rural-urban ratio** — ✅ **etc.**
- ✅ Three routings: **political socialization and political culture** → psychology and sociology, drawn **heavily**; **political development** → sociology and economics, **increased reliance**; **political communication** → **social psychology**, **nearer**.
- ✅ **Lipset**, *Introduction to Politics and the Social Sciences* (**1969**): political development cannot be treated without **the conditions of social mobilization**; men cannot become **citizens in a political sense** without changes in **values and personality orientations**; **politics will be different in caste and non-caste societies**; **class and status relationships** affect **the possibilities for acceptance of a legal-rational political order**.

## Theories and models borrowed

- ✅ Use is **abundant**, and such borrowing is **quite common**.
- ✅ **Elite theory**: in **every society**, **irrespective of its forms of social and political institutions**, a **small group of the chosen few always assumes a dominant position**. ✅ Developed by **Pareto (1848-1923)**, **Mosca (1858-1941)**, **Michels (1876-1936)** in **sociology**; applied to **political institutions**, **leadership**, **group behaviour**, and **new interpretations of democracy**.
- ✅ **Base and superstructure**: **politics, culture, morals, ideas and institutions** shaped by the **prevailing mode of material production**; evolved in **economics and sociology**; **adopted as a tool of political analysis**.
- ✅ **Easton's political system** derived from the sociological **social system**.
- ✅ **Structural-functional approach**: **cultural anthropology** (**Malinowski**, **Radcliffe-Brown**) → **sociology** (**Talcott Parsons**) → **political science** (**Almond and Powell**).
- ✅ **Lasswell's problem-solving model** derived from **psychology**.
- ✅ **Bargaining** from the **theories of competition** — **perfect competition** and **monopolistic competition** — in economics; **Schumpeter** and **Anthony Downs** used the **economist's model of the open market** for democratic politics.

## The use of history

- ✅ Two contexts: **(a)** a **rich storehouse of data** for **understanding and interpretation**, **comparative study of political situations** and **verification of hypotheses about politics**; **(b)** when history yields **cause-and-effect relationships, or rather, laws of historical development (such as Marx's materialist interpretation of history)**, for **understanding the course of politics in a given situation**.
- ✅ **Seeley (1834-95)**: **History without political science has no fruit; political science without history has no root!**
- ✅ **Freeman (1823-92)**: **History is past politics, politics is present history.**
- ✅ Verdict: **oversimplification of a complex issue**; the **expanding horizons** of political science have given it an **independent status and identity**; it uses historical material **only selectively, although frequently**.
- ✅ Two instances: **any study of the Indian political system must start with its historical antecedents**; **the study of international politics must be based on the historical background**.
- ✅ Reconstruction task: historical accounts reconstructed **in the light of political theory** for **the role of various classes including the elites**, **levels of politicization**, and **the impact of changes on the economic life of society**.
- ✅ Convergence: political science is now **deeply concerned with the socio-economic foundations of political phenomena**, with **a corresponding change in the outlook of modern historians**; the **simultaneous change of outlook** has **strengthened the age-old relationship**; neither is confined to the **'great' acts of kings and princes**, their battles, victories, expansion, consolidation, defeats, disintegration and downfall; both attend to the **condition and role of the masses in the making of history as well as the shaping of politics**.
- ✅ Test: political science **cannot claim to evolve a foolproof theory unless it is substantiated and verified by historical data, both in terms of rule and exception**; theory from **mere speculation, visionary perspective or limited observation** will **lack scientific precision and thus crumble**.

## The use of economics

- ✅ Economics analyses the factors affecting **human behaviour** in **material production, distribution and exchange**, and **decisions about investment or use of economically relevant resources—primarily capital, labour and goods**.
- ✅ Those decisions are **greatly affected by 'non-economic factors', such as cultural values, personality, political needs, and considerations of status**.
- ✅ **Eighteenth century**: economics begins **as political economy**, standing for **the study and practice of the management of government and the nation**.
- ✅ **Late nineteenth century**: it **sought its independence from political theory**, becoming **increasingly occupied with price and market behaviour, under the influence of the laissez-faire doctrine**.
- ✅ Consequences of an **unrestrained economic system**: **wide economic disparities**, **mounting exploitation of underprivileged sections**, **growing injustice**, **consequent friction**.
- ✅ Hence **regulation of economic activity and subordination of economics to public control**, and the **renewed mutual interest**.
- ✅ Political theory is primarily concerned with **conflicting demands of various groups and classes within a community or between different nations in the international sphere**, which **though articulated in various forms, are primarily economic demands**.
- ✅ No political system sustains itself without **a somewhat stable equilibrium, harmony or reconciliation**; policy or planning **from the control of foreign trade to the regulation of the internal economic life of the community** is **devoid of essence** without resolving the economic issues.
- ✅ The **'welfare state'**, **'service state'** or socialist state **claims to** secure **economic security** through **full employment, a reasonable standard of living, nutrition and health**.
- ✅ **Developing nations are particularly faced with the challenge of acute and widespread poverty**; **politics cannot deliver the goods until it is able to tackle the economic problem effectively**.
- ⚠️ **According to Marxian theory, too, all politics is shaped by economic forces** — attributed; ✅ the chapter's own conclusion is that **an adequate knowledge of economics becomes essential**.
- ✅ **Political economy box.** Traditional: **revenue and expenditure of the state**, distinguished from the **management of domestic economy**. Modern: political aspects of economic policy-making so that a social policy proves **not only economically efficient but also politically acceptable**. Concepts: **demands, costs, allocation of resources, utility, optimization**. Issues: **impact of economic factors on political life; impact of political decisions on economic life**. Models: **e.g. rational choice theory**. Variant: **Marxist political economy** on **mode of production** and **social formations** — slave-owning, feudal, capitalist, socialist society.

## The use of sociology

- ✅ Sociology is **chiefly concerned with the behaviour of men in an associative process**, inquiring how **institutions, stabilized systems of expectations and actions**, fulfil **the varying needs of man in society, particularly in a complex society**; it covers **almost every aspect of human behaviour**.
- ✅ Early sociologists in **Europe and America** could not ignore **as important a set of institutions as the political ones** or the **formation of attitudes and group norms** in politics.
- ✅ **Max Weber (1864-1920)**, **Robert Michels (1876-1936)**, **Vilfredo Pareto (1848-1923)**, **Emile Durkheim (1858-1917)** turned to political analysis within sociological inquiry.
- ✅ **Arthur Bentley (1870-1957)**, trained as a sociologist in Europe, became **a major intellectual force in political science in America**; **Franklin Giddings (1855-1931)** encouraged **empirical studies of voting behaviour in the early 1920s**.
- ✅ Political sociology applies the **analyses of bureaucratic structures developed in the works of Max Weber and Robert Michels** to **governmental agencies, trade unions, political parties, and even economic institutions**.
- ✅ Public decisions, **to be effective, must take into account the social motivations of relevant groups**; inquiry into **value-orientations, attitudes, habits, beliefs and prejudices** is **indispensable to ascertain the possible response and chances of success of any social measure**.
- ✅ The discipline **thrives on the contributions from sociology as well as political science**.
- ✅ **Political sociology box.** Examines **political institutions, processes and ideologies in the light of corresponding social structures, processes and modes of thought**; **also examines the impact of political domination and decisions on social life**; **treats political system as a subsystem within social system**. Roster: **political socialization, political culture, political participation, social basis of political behaviour, political ideologies, political consensus and cleavage, power, authority and legitimacy, patterns of political leadership**.

## The use of psychology

- ✅ Psychology is interested in the **motives of human behaviour** and the **causes of variations in individual and group behaviour**.
- ✅ Some actions follow **man's mental make-up as such**; others from **participation in varying social situations**; **personality formation** itself depends on **interaction with other individuals in a given social setting** — hence **social psychology**.
- ✅ The **growth of totalitarian political movements in the thirties and forties of the twentieth century** aroused interest in the **'authoritarian personality'**, pursued through **psycho-analytic theory** and **social psychological methods** to understand **extremist political appeals**; **Harold Lasswell (1902-78)** and his disciples followed similar orientations.
- ✅ Four political interests: **formation and expression of public opinion**; **patterns of leadership including charismatic leadership**; **impact of propaganda**; **the role of mass communication in inculcating political culture**.
- ✅ **Political psychology box.** Uses the **models and techniques** of psychology to analyse psychological aspects of political behaviour; ❌ **it may also be used for influencing political behaviour of a group**; it is **a branch of social psychology**. Scope: personality and political behaviour; **sources of similarities and dissimilarities between political attitudes**; **psychological bases of political learning, political socialization and leadership styles**; **techniques and impact of political propaganda**.

## The use of philosophy

- ✅ Philosophy inquires into the **essence of reality, limits of knowledge, nature of truth, relation between man and universe, the purpose of universe, the foundations of morality and beauty**.
- ✅ It **makes use of the conclusions of empirical data** but focuses on **clarification of the conceptual problems** beyond the **limits of empirical knowledge**; applied to politics it is **political philosophy**.
- ✅ Usual contrast: science relies on the **empirical method**, based on **sense-experience**; philosophy uses the **transcendental method**, based on **speculation of what is beyond sense-experience**; science is **descriptive**, philosophy **critical and evaluative**.
- ✅ Refusal: **science itself is the product of philosophy** — it owes philosophy both its **origin** and its **validity**. ✅ **Auguste Comte (1798-1857)**, **a French philosopher**, traced the foundation of science to **'positive philosophy'** and recognised **sociology as the 'mother social science'**.
- ✅ A **growing craze** for scientific method made it **fashionable** to distinguish political science from political philosophy; **behaviouralism in the 1950s and 1960s** was **particularly responsible**; **since early 1970s**, with **post-behaviouralism** and the revival of interest in the **theory of justice** and related principles (**liberty, equality, community**), political philosophy received **renewed prominence**.
- ✅ Settlement: scientific and behavioural studies **find facts and devise tools for achieving the goals of public policy whereas these goals are largely determined with the help of political philosophy**.
- ✅ **Political philosophy box.** Moral and substantive dimensions of politics; central problems — **pursuit of good life**, **norms and values**, **good and evil**, **virtue and vice**, **means and ends**, **right and wrong**, **visions of an ideal state and society**; inquires into **foundations of political life**, **grounds of political obligation**, **moral worthiness of different political systems**; uses **philosophical and historical method** in determining the goals of public life; **a major part** of it **coincides with moral and social philosophy**.

## The conclusion

- ✅ A **modern approach** marking a **departure from the classical approach**, which, **typified by Aristotle's description of politics as the 'master science'**, **failed to distinguish between normative and empirical aspects**.
- ✅ Concession: **it was Aristotle himself who originally identified 'politics' as an independent discipline**, **yet he could not discern political phenomenon from other aspects of man's social life**; **in Aristotle's times all knowledge was covered by philosophy**, and he sought only to distinguish **'political philosophy' from other branches of philosophy**.
- ✅ **Only in the nineteenth century** did aspects of social life become **independent subjects of empirical study**, so that **psychology, sociology, economics, political science** emerged as **independent scientific disciplines**.
- ✅ The approach is **of recent origin** and **assumed special importance in the latter part of the twentieth century**.
- ✅ **It does not seek to merge all social sciences into a single 'master science'**; it insists that results **in any sphere** be **based on and verified from the findings of all relevant social sciences**, and that political science **also make a suitable contribution to an understanding of those disciplines**.
- ✅ **Seven partial approaches** — **philosophical, historical, legal, economic, sociological, psychological and geographical** — each covering **a limited range of inquiry by relying on a single discipline**.
- ✅ Remedy: relying on **all other relevant disciplines** for **evolving an integrated view of the subject**.

## 🔶 Owner extensions, kept separate

- 🔶 **Political anthropology**: political order does not require a centralised state; kinship and lineage authority as evidence; tests authority, legitimacy and sovereignty against non-state cases; ❌ does not transfer mechanically to large modern states.
- 🔶 **Law as evidence** (distinct from the legal approach, which ➡️ Topic 08 owns): constitutional text, statutes and judicial reasoning as primary evidence of formal allocation and constraint; ❌ cannot explain informal power, bargaining or non-compliance.
- 🔶 **Political geography**: spatial location of power and resources shapes conflict and institutional design; boundary disputes, regional imbalance and federal arrangements as evidence; ❌ constrains but does not determine.
- 🔶 **Named reductionisms**: **economic** (loses legitimacy, identity, normative claims) · **psychological** (loses structural, institutional and economic constraint) · **sociological or functional** (loses conflict, agency and deliberate change; conservative bias) · **legal** (loses informal power and non-compliance). ⚠️ **Historical** and **geographical** reductionisms are extensions of the same logic, named in this file's voice only.
- 🔶 **Objection-reply chain 1:** dissolution of subject matter → the refusal of merger, with the political question as the organising centre.
- 🔶 **Objection-reply chain 2:** over-generalisation from small-scale or spatial evidence → evidence weighed for relevance, supported by **rule and exception** and the purposefulness lock.
- 🔶 **Cautious Indian application** as *types* only: political-economy lens on liberalisation, welfare design and fiscal transfers; political-sociology lens on caste, community and regional identity in mobilisation; political-geography lens on reorganisation and resource-sharing disputes. ❌ No figure, scheme, party, case or date.
- ✅ The one chapter-grounded Indian instruction: **any study of the Indian political system must start with its historical antecedents**.

## Answer architecture, compressed

- ⚠️ **10 marks (~150 words):** thesis → 2-3 disciplines as claim → evidence → significance → one reductionism risk → integration not merger.
- ⚠️ **15 marks (~250 words):** thesis and directive decoding → two-limb definition → why the empirical, behavioural and systems turns pushed outward → 4-5 disciplines with claim → evidence → significance → limit, including one of anthropology, law or geography → one named borrowed model → one objection-reply chain → reasoned conclusion.
- ⚠️ **20 marks (~250 words at this file's ceiling):** thesis and decoding → full reconstruction across the five expounded disciplines plus legal and geographical as chapter-named → at least two named models → the specific reductionism for each donor → both objection-reply chains → one hedged Indian application → graded conclusion.
- 🔑 Close with **evolving an integrated view of the subject**.

## Twelve lines that carry the most marks

1. ✅ **The study of politics as a social process provides ample scope for an interdisciplinary approach.**
2. ✅ The approach **becomes imperative** once the political system is an **open system**.
3. ✅ **(a)** use, **(b)** verification, **(c)** contribution.
4. ✅ Borrowing must be **purposeful and well-conceived**, **not at random**.
5. ✅ Political science turned to its neighbours **not only to learn their methodology, but also** for data, theories and models.
6. ✅ **Political behaviour analysis must be interdisciplinary** — Eulau, 1968.
7. ✅ The three organs **do not produce their decisions on their own initiative or at their own will**.
8. ✅ A theory must be verified **both in terms of rule and exception**, or it will **crumble**.
9. ✅ **Politics cannot deliver the goods until it is able to tackle the economic problem effectively.**
10. ✅ Institutions are **stabilized systems of expectations and actions**.
11. ✅ Empirical studies **find facts and devise tools**; **goals are largely determined with the help of political philosophy**.
12. ✅ **It does not seek to merge all social sciences into a single 'master science'** — seven approaches are **partial**; the remedy is **an integrated view of the subject**.
